Scattering theory for C 2 C^{2} long-range potentials Thanks: K.I. is supported by JSPS KAKENHI, grant nr. JP17K05325 and JP23K03163. E.S. is supported by the Danish Council for Independent Research | | Natural Sciences, grant nr. DFF-4181-00042.
K. Ito, E. Skibsted

Abstract
. We develop a complete stationary scattering theory for Schrödinger operators on ℝ d {\mathbb{R}}^{d} , d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , with C 2 C^{2} long-range potentials. This extends former results in the literature, in particular [ Is1 , Is2 , II , GY ] , which all require a higher degree of smoothness. In this sense the spirit of our paper is similar to [ Hö2 , Chapter XXX] , which also develops a scattering theory under the C 2 C^{2} condition, however being very different from ours. While the Agmon–Hörmander theory is based on the Fourier transform, our theory is not and may be seen as more related to our previous approach to scattering theory on manifolds [ IS1 , IS2 , IS3 ] . The C 2 C^{2} regularity is natural in the Agmon–Hörmander theory as well as in our theory, in fact probably being ‘optimal’ in the Euclidean setting. We prove equivalence of the stationary and time-dependent theories by giving stationary representations of associated time-dependent wave operators. Furthermore we develop a related stationary scattering theory at fixed energy in terms of asymptotics of generalized eigenfunctions of minimal growth. A basic ingredient of our approach is a solution to the eikonal equation constructed from the geometric variational scheme of [ CS ] . Another key ingredient is strong radiation condition bounds for the limiting resolvents originating in [ HS ] . They improve formerly known ones [ Is1 , Sa ] and considerably simplify the stationary approach. We obtain the bounds by a new commutator scheme whose elementary form allows a small degree of smoothness.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Setting

In the present paper we construct a stationary long-range scattering theory for
the Schrödinger operator

(1.1)

H=-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta+V+q

on
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
d\geq 2
.
Here
\Delta
is the ordinary Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and we shall often write

-\Delta=p\cdot p=p_{i}p_{i};\quad p_{i}=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{i},\ \ i=1,\dots,d,

with the Einstein convention being adopted without tensorial
superscripts.

We shall address the problem of constructing such theory under a minimal regularity
condition on the long-range part
V
of the potential
V+q
. The
second term

q
is a standard short-range potential. This
corresponds to taking
l=2
in the following
C^{l}
long-range type condition. For technical reasons
we consider below, and throughout the
paper, the following more general condition in which
l\geq 2
is
arbitrary (however typically given as
l=2
). It is a trivial consequence of the condition that
H
is self-adjoint.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
,
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}=(0,\infty)
and
\langle x\rangle=(1+|x|^{2})^{1/2}
for

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For given Banach spaces
X
and
Y
we denote by

{\mathcal{L}}(X,Y)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
the set of bounded and compact
operators
T:X\to Y
, respectively, and for
Y=X
we abbreviate
{\mathcal{L}}(X)={\mathcal{L}}(X,Y)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(X)={\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
.

Condition 1.1
.

Let
V\in C^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
for some
l\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
,
and assume there exist
\sigma\in(0,1)
,
\rho\in(0,1]
and
C>0

such that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\alpha|\leq l
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(1.2a)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}V(x)|\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-m(|\alpha|)};\quad m(k)=\begin{cases}\sigma+k&\text{for }k=0,1,2,\\
\sigma+2+\tfrac{\rho+1}{2}(k-2)&\text{for }k=2,\dots,l.\end{cases}

In addition, let
q\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable, and assume
there exists
\tau\in(0,1)
such that

(1.2b)

\langle x\rangle^{1+\tau}q(x)(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}).

Finally, assume the operator
H=-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta+V+q
does not have positive eigenvalues.

Remarks 1.2
.

(1)

For
l=2
the above
V+q
is called a
2
-admissible
potential
, here adapting the terminology of
[
Hö2
, Definition 30.1.3]
.
Several of our main theorems require only
l=2
.
However for an intermediate key estimate of independent interest we need
l=4

(or with a modification possibly only
l=3
), see
Theorem
1.23
(and Remark
1.24

2
). This estimate
will be used for a certain regularized
4
-admissible
potential constructed from a
given
2
-admissible
potential, see
Remark
1.24

5
. (For the regularized potential the
parameter
\sigma
is the same and
\rho<\sigma
, arbitrarily.) In this sense indeed the key estimate serves as an intermediate result
for our study of
2
-admissible
potentials.

(2)

We call
V
a
classical
C^{l}
long-range
potential
if (
1.2a
) holds with
\sigma\in(0,1)
and either
l=2
(in
which case
\rho
is irrelevant) or
l\geq 3
and in this case
\rho=1
. If this is fulfilled
for all
l\geq 2
we call
V
a

classical
C^{\infty}
long-range potential
. Obviously the
expression for the

order of decay function

m:{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is simplest (in
fact being case-independent)
for a classical potential. Nevertheless, in general, its particular
form is well-suited for an induction argument to be used in the
proof of the basic result Theorem
1.3
(stated
below).

(3)

The local singularities allowed in
(
1.2b
) is not an important issue/difficulty in this
paper. The last assumption on absence of positive
eigenvalues is very weak and can be omitted for example if (
1.2b
) is replaced by
assuming boundedness of the function
\langle x\rangle^{1+\tau}q(x)
.

Under Condition
1.1
for
l=2
we succeed in fully developing a stationary scattering theory:
We characterize the generalized eigenfunctions of
minimal growth by their asymptotics
and construct the

stationary scattering matrix
as well as the
generalized Fourier transforms
,
which unitarily diagonalize the (absolutely) continuous part of
H
.
Such results were formerly obtained by Isozaki
[
Is1
,
Is2
]
, Ikebe–Isozaki
[
II
]
and Gâtel–Yafaev
[
GY
]

for classical
C^{4}
or
C^{3}
long-range potentials. In this paper we extend
them to the category of
2
-admissible
potentials.
Note that a similar
C^{2}
condition very naturally appears
in the classical long-range scattering theory, see for example

[
DG
, Theorem 2.7.1]
. In fact we believe that
our condition should be considered as ‘optimal’, although this
terminology will not be justified in the paper.
Finally we shall prove that
the adjoints of the generalized Fourier transforms coincide with some constructed time-dependent wave operators,
verifying that the stationary and the time-dependent approaches are equivalent to each other.

The long-range scattering theory requires a non-trivial comparison dynamics due to
non-negligible effects from
V
at infinity.
To virtually eliminate such effects we solve the

stationary eikonal equation
, or simply the
eikonal equation
,

(1.3a)

\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert\nabla_{x}S(\lambda,x)\rvert^{2}+V(x)=\lambda,\quad\lambda>0,

in the stationary theory, and the
time-dependent eikonal equation
,
which is more commonly called the
Hamilton–Jacobi equation
,

(1.3b)

\partial_{t}K(t,x)+\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla_{x}K(t,x)|^{2}+V(x)=0,\quad t>0,

in the time-dependent theory.
In this paper we solve the former equation (
1.3a
)
by the geometric method of
[
CS
]
and derive global estimates
of the solution.
For comparison we mention that Isozaki
[
Is1
,
Is2
]
in his
construction of a solution
used a classical
PDE-method solving a Cauchy problem, and
the cited papers
[
II
,
GY
]
rely on Isozaki’s solution.
Once a proper solution to (
1.3a
) is obtained,
one can solve (
1.3b
) by using the Legendre transform, see also
[
IS2
]
.

Another important technical tool is a strong version of the radiation condition bounds for the limiting resolvents,
which in fact considerably simplifies the stationary scattering theory.
Such ‘strong radiation condition bounds’ were first established
by Herbst–Skibsted

[
HS
]
for classical
C^{\infty}
long-range potentials. For a more
restrictive class of classical
C^{\infty}
long-range potentials
(defined by a virial condition) the
bounds were derived uniformly in non-negative energies
[
Sk2
]
,
yielding a stationary scattering theory at fixed energy including
the threshold zero.
In this paper we present a procedure of proof that works within a
low regularity framework (in particular being independent of
pseudodifferential operator theory).
A similar procedure was invented and applied ealier to the short-range Stark
Hamiltonian
[
AIIS1
]
, however our setup is different and we need
to proceed rather
independently.
Since the proof still requires fourth (or possibly
only third) derivatives of the potential,
we shall regularize it
up to an error of short-range type
using a regularization scheme of
Hörmander
[
Hö2
, Lemma 30.1.1]
. This leads to the study of
radiation condition bounds of a new classical
C^{2}
long-range
potential which conforms with (
1.2a
) for an
l\geq 3

but possibly
fails
to be a classical
C^{3}
long-range
potential.
The short-range error from the Hörmander decomposition
(see Lemma
4.1
for the version to be used in our paper) along with the potential
q
will be treated by the second resolvent identity.
Note that the Hörmander decomposition was also employed in
[
II
]
,
however Ikebe and Isozaki considered only classical
C^{4}
long-range potentials.

These two ingredients occupy a considerable part of the paper,
and
in addition to the entailing stationary and time-dependent scattering theories we consider them as main results
of independent interest.

With these preliminaries done we derive a
complete stationary scattering theory. The strong radiation
condition bounds yield a very fast construction of generalized
Fourier transforms using the ‘spherical eikonal coordinates’. These
coordinates were first used in the context of stationary scattering
theory in
[
ACH
]
for a different setting and with different
proofs. Then we ‘pull the results back’ to assertions in the
ordinary spherical coordinates. After a complete stationary theory
is obtained, the time-dependent theory follows naturally, still
being quite non-trivial though. Finally we mention that our low
regularity theory has an application to the 3-body problem. We
shall briefly discuss this aspect in
Subsection
1.2.5
.
Finally, we discuss potential applications to scattering theory in
Subsection
1.2.6
.

1.2.
Main results

Now we present a series of main results of the paper.

1.2.1.
Stationary eikonal equation

Let us first solve the stationary eikonal equation (
1.3a
) outside a large ball.
Take any
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
such that

(1.4)

\displaystyle\chi(t)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{for }t\leq 4/3,\\
1&\mbox{for }t\geq 5/3,\end{cases}\quad\chi^{\prime}\geq 0,

and set for any
R>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(1.5)

\displaystyle\chi_{R}(x)=\chi(|x|/R).

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
for some

l\geq 2
. Fix any closed interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.
Then it follows that for all
R\geq R_{0}
for some
R_{0}>0
, there
exists a real
S\in C^{l}(I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}))
and

s\in C^{l}(I\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that:

(1)

The function
S
solves

(1.6)

\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert\nabla_{x}S\rvert^{2}+\chi_{R}V=\lambda\ \ \text{on }I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}).

(2)

For any
\lambda\in I
,
S(\lambda,\cdot)
coincides with the geodesic distance
from the origin with respect to the Riemannian metric

g=2\bigl(\lambda-\chi_{R}V\bigr)\,{\rm d}x^{2}
.

(3)

The functions
S
and
s
are related as

S=\sqrt{2\lambda}|x|(1+s)\ \ \text{on }I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}),

and
s
vanishes on
I\times\{|x|\leq R\}
.

(4)

There exists
C=C(I,R_{0})>0
(being independent of

R\geq R_{0}
) such that
for any
k+|\alpha|\leq l
and
(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

(1.7)

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial^{k}_{\lambda}\partial^{\alpha}_{x}s(\lambda,x)\bigr|\leq C\lambda^{-1-k}\langle x\rangle^{-m(k+|\alpha|)+k}.

Remarks 1.4
.

(1)

For a variation of (
1.7
) see also Corollary
2.14
.

(2)

We can extend
S
and
s
to be smoothly defined for all
\lambda>0
,
however, allowing
R=R(\lambda)
to be
\lambda
-dependent possibly with
R(\lambda)\to\infty
as
\lambda\to 0_{+}
.
On the other hand,
a bound corresponding to (
1.7
) can be kept uniform in
\lambda>0
.
This can be seen from the proof, but we shall not elaborate on it.

(3)

Our bound (
1.7
) is stronger
than
[
Is1
, Theorem 4.1]
, where Isozaki obtained
(
1.7
), except for
(k,\alpha)=(3,0)
, for a classical
C^{3}
long-range
potential.
Gâtal–Yafaev asserted
(
1.7
) for a classical
C^{3}
long-range
potential in
[
GY
, Lemma 3.1]
,
however it was not proved there, and in fact it was indicated in a paragraph subsequent to it that the
assertion requires a classical
C^{4}
long-range potential.

1.2.2.
Stationary scattering theory at fixed energy

Next we construct a stationary scattering theory at fixed energy
through the WKB approximation of the limiting resolvent.

Let us briefly review the
limiting absorption principle

for the resolvent

R(z)=(H-z)^{-1};\quad z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(H).

It is a basic and well-studied topic, for details see e.g.
[
AIIS2
]
and the references there.
Recall the

Besov spaces
, or the
Agmon–Hörmander spaces
, defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\,\big|\,\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}}<\infty\bigr\},\quad\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}2^{m/2}\|1_{m}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{*}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\,\big|\,\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}^{*}}<\infty\bigr\},\quad\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}^{*}}=\sup_{m\geq 0}2^{-m/2}\|1_{m}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\psi\in\mathcal{B}^{*}\,\Big|\,\lim_{m\to\infty}2^{-m/2}\|1_{m}\psi\|_{{\mathcal{H}}}=0\Bigr\}.

Here we let

(1.8)

\displaystyle 1_{0}

\displaystyle=1\bigl(\{|x|<1\}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad 1_{m}=1\bigl(\{2^{m-1}\leq|x|<2^{m}\}\bigr)\ \ \text{for }m\in\mathbb{N},

with
1(A)
being the sharp characteristic function of a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
It is worthwhile recalling that if we define the standard
weighted
L^{2}
spaces
as

L_{s}^{2}=\langle x\rangle^{-s}{\mathcal{H}}\ \ \text{for }s\in\mathbb{R},

then for any
s>1/2

L^{2}_{s}\subset\mathcal{B}\subset L^{2}_{1/2}\subset\mathcal{H}\subset L^{2}_{-1/2}\subset\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}\subset\mathcal{B}^{*}\subset L^{2}_{-s}.

It is proved in
[
AIIS2
]
that
locally uniformly in
\lambda>0
there exist the
limiting resolvents

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)=\swslim_{z\to\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0_{+}}R(z)\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{*}),

or equivalently stated, for any
\psi,\phi\in\mathcal{B}

\langle\phi,R(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi\rangle=\lim_{z\to\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0_{+}}\langle\phi,R(z)\psi\rangle,

respectively (see Theorem
3.17
for more
general assertions). In particular the singular continuous spectrum of

H
is empty,

\sigma_{\mathrm{sc}}(H)=\emptyset
.

For these limiting resolvents we discuss the
WKB approximations
as follows,
using here and throughout the paper the notation
\mathcal{G}=L^{2}\big({\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\big)
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
for
l=2
.
Let
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a closed interval,
and let
R>0
.
Assume there exists real
S=\sqrt{2\lambda}|x|(1+s)\in C\bigl(I;C^{2}(\{|x|>R\})\bigr)
satisfying:

(1)

For each
\lambda\in I
,
S(\lambda,\cdot)
solves (
1.3a
) on
\{|x|>R\}
.

(2)

For any compact subset
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
there exist
\epsilon,C>0

such that for any
|\alpha|\leq 2
,
\lambda\in I^{\prime}
and
|x|>R

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}s(\lambda,x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-\epsilon-|\alpha|}.

In addition, for any
\xi\in\mathcal{G}
and
(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
set

(1.9)

\displaystyle\phi_{\pm}^{S}[\xi](\lambda,x)=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{1/2}}{(2\lambda)^{1/4}}\chi_{R}(x)|x|^{-(d-1)/2}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}S(\lambda,x)}\xi(\hat{x}),\quad\hat{x}=|x|^{-1}x,

respectively, where
\chi_{R}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is from (
1.5
).
Then the following assertions hold.

(1)

For any
\lambda\in I

there exist unique
F^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{G}})
such that for any

\psi\in\mathcal{B}

(1.10)

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi-\phi_{\pm}^{S}[F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi](\lambda,\cdot)\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0},

respectively,

(2)

The mappings
F^{\pm}\colon I\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{G}

are continuous.

(3)

For any
\lambda\in I
one has the identities

(H-\lambda)F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}F^{\pm}(\lambda)=\delta(H-\lambda),

respectively,
where
\delta(H-\lambda)=\pi^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{*})
.

(4)

For any
\lambda\in I
the ranges
F^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
are dense.

Remarks 1.6
.

(1)

The existence of such
S
for large
R
is
guaranteed by Theorem
1.3
, but here it can be
slightly more general
(thanks to Theorem
1.3
the
condition
2
is fulfilled with

\epsilon=\sigma
for
I^{\prime}=I
). Note also that according to Remark
1.4

2

we may let
I={\mathbb{R}}_{+}

if we allow a
\lambda
-dependent
R
.

(2)

Clearly for any
\xi\in\mathcal{G}
and
\lambda\in I
, the functions
\phi_{\pm}^{S}[\xi](\lambda,\cdot)\in\mathcal{B}^{*}
.
We may think of these
quasi-modes
as purely outgoing/incoming distorted spherical waves, respectively.

(3)

In Theorem
1.10
below we will
see the ‘completeness property’
F^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}

for any
\lambda\in I
.

Now we have stationary versions of scattering quantities.

Definitions 1.7
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.5
, let
\lambda\in I
.

(1)

The operators
F^{\pm}(\lambda)\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{G}
are the

restricted stationary wave operators

at energy
\lambda
.

(2)

The adjoints
F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\colon\mathcal{G}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
are the
stationary wave matrices
.

(3)

The
stationary scattering matrix at energy

\lambda\in I

is the unitary operator
\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}

obeying

(1.11)

\displaystyle F^{+}(\lambda)=\mathsf{S}(\lambda)F^{-}(\lambda).

Indeed the scattering matrix is well-defined, stated as follows.

Corollary 1.8
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.5
, at any energy

\lambda\in I
the stationary scattering matrix
\mathsf{S}(\lambda)
uniquely exist.
Moreover, the mapping
I\ni\lambda\mapsto\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{G})
is strongly continuous.

Remarks 1.9
.

(1)

The stationary scattering matrix
\mathsf{S}(\lambda)
is defined pointwise for
all

\lambda\in I
, not only for a.e.
\lambda\in I
,
unlike in the abstract construction commonly adopted in the
time-dependent approach.

(2)

Although the usual well-known short-range
scattering theory (defined for
V+q=q
) is a different subject, let us remark that in this
case it is more conventional to define the quasi-modes
(
1.9
) slightly different, more precisely with
(
1.9
) modified by the factor
{\rm e}^{\mp{\rm i}\pi(d-3)/4}
. In particular if also the short-range potential
q

vanishes, it then follows that in fact
\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\equiv I
.

(3)

The dependence of the
restricted stationary wave operators on the given function

S(\lambda,x)
is almost canonically given by (
1.9
) and (
1.10
). It is given by an explicit multiplication operator
of modulus one, see Remark
4.11
and
its appearance in Theorem
1.12
(
3
).

The stationary scattering theory is intimately related to the
asymptotics of the
minimal generalized eigenfunctions

\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
,
where

{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}=\{\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\,|\,(H-\lambda)\phi=0\ \text{in the distributional sense}\};\quad\lambda>0.

It is a non-trivial subspace of
\mathcal{B}^{*}
by Theorem
1.5
,
and it is minimal in the sense that
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}\cap\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}=\{0\}
(see Remark
4.15

2
).

Theorem 1.10
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.5
, let
\lambda\in I
.

(1)

For any one of
\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
or
\xi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{G}
the
two other quantities in
\{\phi,\xi_{+},\xi_{-}\}
uniquely exist
such that

(1.12a)

\displaystyle\phi-\phi_{+}^{S}[\xi_{+}](\lambda,\cdot)+\phi_{-}^{S}[\xi_{-}](\lambda,\cdot)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}.

(2)

The above correspondences
\xi_{\pm}\to\phi
and

\xi_{\mp}\to\xi_{\pm}
are given by the formulas

(1.12b)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=2\pi\mathrm{i}F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\xi_{\pm}\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{+}=\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\xi_{-}.

(3)

The wave matrices

F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}

are topological linear isomorphisms as

\mathcal{G}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
.
In addition, for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
and
\xi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{G}
satisfying (
1.12a
)
one has

(1.12c)

\displaystyle\|\xi_{\pm}\|_{\mathcal{G}}=\tfrac{(2\lambda)^{1/4}}{(2\pi)^{1/2}}\lim_{m\to\infty}2^{-m/2}\lVert 1_{m}\phi\rVert_{\mathcal{H}},

where
1_{m}
is from (
1.8
).

(4)

The operators

F^{\pm}(\lambda)\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{G}
and

\delta(H-\lambda)\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}
are surjective.

Remarks 1.11
.

(1)

We can also express
\xi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{G}
as simple oscillatory weak limits of
\phi\in\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
at infinity,
see Step III of the proof.

(2)

The above result extends in the Euclidean setting
[
GY
,
IS1
]
to 2-admissible potentials.
See also
[
II
]
.

1.2.3.
Generalized Fourier transforms

The stationary scattering theory at fixed energy
applies to the construction of
the
generalized (or distorted) Fourier transforms
, also
referred to as the
stationary wave operators
,
which unitarily transform the continuous part of the Schrödinger
operator
H
into a simple multiplication operator.

As in Theorem
1.5
the subset
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

denotes a closed interval, and we let
P_{H}(I)
denote the
corresponding spectral projection for
H
. We introduce the notation

\displaystyle H_{I}=H_{|{\mathcal{H}}_{I}},\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{I}={P_{H}(I){\mathcal{H}}},\quad\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{I}=L^{2}(I,{\rm d}\lambda;{\mathcal{G}}).

Thanks to Theorem
1.5
we can also introduce the operators

\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}=\int_{I}^{\oplus}F^{\pm}(\lambda)\,{\rm d}\lambda\colon\mathcal{B}\to C(I;\mathcal{G})\cap\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{I}.

These operators can be extended as to be acting from
P_{H}(I)\mathcal{B}
and then in
turn to operators acting from
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
. These assertions are part of the
following main theorem (see also Step II in the proof).

Theorem 1.12
.

In the setting of
Theorem
1.5
the following asssertions hold:

(1)

The operators
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}

induce unitary operators
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{I}\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{I}
,
respectively.

(2)

The induced unitary operators
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
satisfy

\mathcal{F}^{\pm}H_{I}(\mathcal{F}^{\pm})^{*}=M_{\lambda},

respectively,
where
M_{\lambda}
denotes the operator of multiplication by
\lambda
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
.

(3)

Suppose also
S_{1}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.5
,
and let
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}_{1}
be the associated unitary operators as above.
Then there exists the limit

(1.13a)

\Theta(\lambda,\omega):=\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(S_{1}(\lambda,r\omega)-S(\lambda,r\omega)\big)

taken locally uniformly in

(\lambda,\omega)\in I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and it follows that

(1.13b)

\mathcal{F}^{\pm}_{1}=\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta}\mathcal{F}^{\pm},

respectively.

Remarks 1.13
.

(1)

Under the conditions of Theorem
1.3
one can easily extend the assertion to
I={\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, so that
the whole absolutely continuous part

H_{\mathrm{ac}}=H_{|\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{ac}}},\ \mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{ac}}=P_{H}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})\mathcal{H}
, is diagonalized.
One has only to cover
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
with disjoint intervals
and take a direct sum of the associated generalized Fourier transforms, or
to adopt a function
S
defined for all
\lambda>0
in Theorem
1.5
,
see
Remarks
1.4

2

and
1.6

1
.
This is straightforward.

(2)

In
[
II
,
GY
]
the diagonalization was carried out successfully
for classical
C^{4}
or
C^{3}
long-range potentials. Our result extends
these previous results to the
C^{2}
case.
For related works we refer the reader to
[
Sa
,
IS1
]
,
still
the present
C^{2}
case is not covered in these
works. On the other hand
[
Hö1
]
and the Agmon–Hörmander theory

[
Hö2
, Chapter XXX]
cover 2-admissible
potentials in some form (see
[
DG
, Proposition 3.4.2]

for a somewhat
relevant comparison within their theory). However their approach (specialized
to the Schrödinger operator) is very different from
ours, for example it is fundamentally based on the Fourier
transform. Moreover the stationary scattering theory of

[
Hö2
, Chapter XXX]
is only
developed up to the point of showing asymptotic completeness.

1.2.4.
Time-dependent scattering theory

We present our results on time-dependent
theory, which relate to the time-dependent eikonal equation
(
1.3b
). For that purpose we introduce
the space-time regions

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid\lvert x\rvert\geq\mu t\bigr\},\quad\mu>0,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu,T}

\displaystyle=\{(t,x)\in\Omega_{\mu}\mid t>T>0\},\quad T>0.

Theorem 1.14
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
for some

l\geq 2
.
Fix any
\mu>\mu^{\prime}>0

and let
S
be given as in Theorem
1.3
with

I=I_{\mu^{\prime}}=[\mu^{\prime 2}/2,\infty)
(assuming here and for other purposes that
R>0
is sufficiently large).
Then for each
(t,x)\in\Omega_{\mu}
there exists a unique critical point
\lambda_{c}=\lambda_{c}(t,x)\in I

of

(1.14)

\widetilde{K}(\lambda,t,x)=S(\lambda,x)-\lambda t

considered as a function of
\lambda\in I
. In addition, if one sets
K=\widetilde{K}(\lambda_{\rm c},\cdot,\cdot)
, then:

(1)

The function
K\in C^{l}(\Omega_{\mu})
, and it solves

(1.15)

\partial_{t}K+\tfrac{1}{2}\lvert\nabla_{x}K\rvert^{2}+\chi_{R}V=0\ \ \text{on }\Omega_{\mu}.

(2)

There exists
C>0
(being independent of all large

R
) such that
for any
k+|\alpha|\leq 2
and
(t,x)\in\Omega_{\mu}

(1.16)

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial^{k}_{t}\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\bigl(K(t,x)-x^{2}/(2t)\bigr)\bigr|\leq Ct^{1-k}\langle x\rangle^{-\sigma-|\alpha|}.

Remark 1.15
.

For
l\geq 3
it is possible to extend (
1.16
) to higher
order derivatives (given by
3\leq k+|\alpha|\leq l
)
with appropriate exponents on the right-hand side. Since
such estimates are not
needed in the present paper, they will not be presented.

Definition 1.16
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.14
, in particular under the
fixed condition
\mu>\mu^{\prime}>0
, the function
K\in C^{l}(\Omega_{\mu})
is called the

Legendre transform
of
S\in C^{l}(I_{\mu^{\prime}}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}))
.

Theorem 1.17
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
with
l=2
.
Let
\mu,T>0
, and assume
K\in C^{2}(\Omega_{\mu,T};{\mathbb{R}})
satisfies:

(1)

K
solves the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
1.3b
) on
\Omega_{\mu,T}
.

(2)

There exist
\epsilon,C>0
such that for any
|\alpha|\leq 2
and
(t,x)\in\Omega_{\mu,T}

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\bigl(K(t,x)-x^{2}/(2t)\bigr)\bigr|\leq Ct\langle x\rangle^{-\epsilon-|\alpha|}.

In addition, let
J=J_{\mu}=[\mu^{2}/2,\infty)
and define
isometries

U^{\pm}(t)\colon\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{J}\to\mathcal{H}
as follows:
For any
(t,h)\in(T,\infty)\times\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{J}
,

(U^{\pm}(t)h)(x)={\rm e}^{\mp 3\pi{\rm i}/4}t^{-1}\lvert x\rvert^{1-d/2}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}K(t,x)}h\big(x^{2}/(2t^{2}),\hat{x}\big);\quad\hat{x}=|x|^{-1}x.

Then the following two assertions hold.

(1)

There exist the strong limits

(1.17)

W^{\pm}:=\slim_{t\to\infty}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}tH}U^{\pm}(t)\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{J}\to\mathcal{H},

respectively. They are isometries, mapping

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{J^{\prime}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{J}
into
{\mathcal{H}}_{J^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}

for any closed subinterval
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
.

(2)

Let
K_{1}
also satisfy the same assumptions as
K
, and let
W_{1}^{\pm}

be the associated isometries as above.
Then there exists the limit

(1.18a)

\Phi(\lambda,\omega):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(K_{1}(t,(2\lambda)^{1/2}t\omega)-K(t,(2\lambda)^{1/2}t\omega)\big)

taken locally uniformly in

(\lambda,\omega)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and it follows that

(1.18b)

W_{1}^{\pm}=W^{\pm}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi},

respectively.

Remarks 1.18
.

(1)

Thanks to Theorem
1.14
such
K
always exists for
T
large enough.

(2)

Our choice of
free comparison dynamics

U^{\pm}(t)
is
motivated in Remark
5.3
.

(3)

Under the conditions of Theorem
1.14
we can extend

U^{\pm}(t)
, and hence
W^{\pm}
as to be acting on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}
,
by covering

{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
with disjoint intervals (as in Remark
1.13

1
) and then taking a direct sum of the
restrictions to these intervals.

Definition 1.19
.

The limits
W^{\pm}
from (
1.17
) are called the
time-dependent wave operators
.
They are
asymptotically complete on
J^{\prime}
(for a
closed subinterval
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
), if they are unitary operators mapping
\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}}_{J^{\prime}}
onto

{{\mathcal{H}}}_{J^{\prime}}
.

The following result shows that the stationary and the
time-dependent wave operators are essentially mutually
inverses. First we state a general result for classes of solutions
to the stationary and the time-dependent eikonal equations, then we
specialize to the concrete ones constructed geometrically and by
the Legendre transform, in which case they are indeed mutually
inverses.

Theorem 1.20
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
with
l=2
.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
, along with a closed interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
R>0
and a
stationary
eikonal equation solution

S
, be given as in
Theorem
1.12
.
Let
W^{\pm}
, along with
\mu,T>0
,
J=[\mu^{2}/2,\infty)
and a
time-dependent eikonal equation solution
K
,
be given as in Theorem
1.17
. Then there exists
\Psi\in C\bigl((I\cap J)\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1};\mathbb{R}\bigr)

such that

(1.19)

\big(W^{\pm}\big)^{*}=\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Psi}\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{H}_{I\cap J}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I\cap J},

respectively.
In particular,
W^{\pm}
are asymptotically complete on
I\cap J
.

(2)

Under the conditions of Theorems

1.3
and
1.14
, let
K\in C^{2}(\Omega_{\mu})
denote the Legendre transform of
S\in C^{2}\bigl(I_{\mu^{\prime}}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\bigr)
;

I_{\mu^{\prime}}=[\mu^{\prime 2}/2,\infty)
,
\mu>\mu^{\prime}>0
. Then
(
1.19
) holds with
I=I_{\mu^{\prime}}=[\mu^{\prime 2}/2,\infty)
,

J=[\mu^{2}/2,\infty)
and with
\Psi
taken
identically zero, i.e.

(1.20)

\big(W^{\pm}\big)^{*}=\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{H}_{J}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{J}.

In particular,
W^{\pm}
are asymptotically complete on
J
.

Remark 1.21
.

The above completeness results resemble asymptotic completeness for
2-admissible potentials as stated in
[
Hö2
, Theorem 30.5.10]
,
however there given with a different free dynamics.
Our approach is more related to our previous study in a geometric setting

[
IS1
,
IS2
]
, though more regularity of
V

is imposed there.
In the geometric short-range setting
[
IS3
]
we employed only time-dependent methods,
but the third order derivative was required for the
analogous
K
, see

[
IS3
, Condition 1.3 and (1.8b)]
.

1.2.5.
Application to the 3-body problem

Our results apply to a recent development in the stationary
scattering theory for
3
-body long-range Hamiltonians
[
Sk1
]
.
Let us see how the application comes about in a slightly simplified form.
Below we only present a brief outline, and refer the reader to

[
Sk1
]
for precise definitions, terminologies and procedure. See also Remark

4.11
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a finite dimensional real inner product space, and
\{\mathbf{X}^{a}\}_{a\in\mathcal{A}}

a family of subspaces of
\mathbf{X}
closed under addition.
Consider the
3
-body problem, i.e. assume that
\#a_{\min}=3
.
Let
V^{a}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{X}^{a})
,
a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\min},a_{\max}\}
, be pair potentials,
and assume there exists
\mu\in(\sqrt{3}-1,1)
such that
for any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim\mathbf{X}^{a}}

\displaystyle\partial^{\alpha}V^{a}(x^{a})={\mathcal{O}}\big(\langle x^{a}\rangle^{-\mu-|\alpha|}\big).

Note that
\sqrt{3}-1\approx 0,732
.
We then define cut-off pair potentials
W^{a}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{X})
as

\displaystyle W^{a}(x)=\chi\bigl(\lvert x^{a}\rvert/|x|^{\mu}\bigr)V^{a}(x^{a}),

where
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is chosen in agreement with
(
1.4
).

Lemma 1.22
.

The cut-off pair potentials
W^{a}
,

a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\min},a_{\max}\}
, satisfy
Condition
1.1
with
l=2
.

Proof.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim\mathbf{X}}
we have
\partial^{\alpha}W^{a}(x)={\mathcal{O}}\big(\langle x\rangle^{-\mu(|\alpha|+\mu)}\big).

The condition

\mu(2+\mu)>2
(required for
|\alpha|=2
) is fulfilled exactly for
\mu>\sqrt{3}-1
.
∎

Remark
.

As a consequence of Lemma
1.22
our results
apply to the
3
-body Hamiltonian
using cut-off pair potentials of long-range type.
In particular one can in a stationary manner derive asymptotic
completeness for the
3
-body
long-range Hamiltonian
along with formulas for scattering quantities, cf.
[
Sk1
]
and Remark

4.11
.
Note that the previous methods in the literature do not apply at this
point since the third derivatives of
W^{a}

may not be of the form
\mathcal{O}(\langle x\rangle^{-3-\sigma})

for some
\sigma\in(0,1]
.

1.2.6.
A further perspective: Generalization to manifolds

Our arguments are not really dependent on a specific structure of the Euclidean space,
but rather on solutions to the eikonal equations (
1.3a
) and
(
1.3b
), and the estimates of their derivatives.
In fact we do not even use the (ordinary) Fourier transform, and
neither pseudodifferential operators nor advanced functional calculus.
In our previous related works
[
IS1
,
IS2
]
we studied in the same
spirit long-range scattering theory on a manifold with ends,
employing approximate solutions to the eikonal equations. We did not develop a
C^{2}
regularity theory using exact solutions as done in the
present paper in the Euclidean setting. Moreover we used weaker radiation conditions bounds
entailing a more complicated construction of the stationary scattering
theory than presented here. For completeness of presentation let us
note that our older work
[
IS3
]
may be seen as a
C^{3}

scattering theory on a manifold in that it involves an exact
C^{3}

solution to the (‘free’)
time-dependent eikonal equation, however this theory is entirely
time-dependent and allows only short-range potentials.

If one could construct a ‘good solution’ on a more general manifold,
say
for a suitable
C^{2}
perturbation (by the variational method of this
paper or by any other means),
then the elementary techniques of the present paper would conceivably work there.
Hence the methods of this paper potentially could also contribute to
stationary scattering theory on manifolds, in particular to
developing a more refined low
regularity theory.

1.3.
Key bounds

In our stationary scattering theory it is a major challenge to verify the WKB approximation (
1.10
).
The following strong radiation condition bounds constitute a powerful tool for that verification,
and are themselves (along with Theorem
1.3
) the most important technical novelty of the paper.

Let
S\in C^{l}(I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}))
be given in
agreement with Theorem
1.3
,
and define the
gamma observables
(or alternatively referred
to as

radiation observables
)

(1.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma&=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{d})=p\mp(\nabla_{x}\chi_{1}S),\\
\gamma_{\|}&=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl((\nabla_{x}\chi_{1}S)\cdot\gamma\bigr)=(\nabla_{x}\chi_{1}S)\cdot\gamma-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(\Delta_{x}\chi_{1}S),\end{split}

where we have included
\chi_{1}
from (
1.5
)
just to cut-off a singularity at the origin.
We also set

(1.22)

\displaystyle\beta_{c}=\min\{2,1+\sigma+\rho\}.

Theorem 1.23
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
with
l=4
and
q\equiv 0
.
Let

I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a closed interval and let

S\in C^{4}(I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
be the
function from Theorem
1.3
given for each
R\geq R_{0}
(for some
R_{0}>0
),
and define correspondingly
\gamma_{j}
and
\gamma_{\|}
as
above. Then the
following bounds hold for any compact subset

I^{\prime}\subseteq I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
:

(1)

For any
\beta\in(0,\beta_{c})

and
R\geq R_{0}
there exists
C>0
, such that
for all

\lambda\in I^{\prime}
and
\psi\in L^{2}_{\beta+1/2}

(1.23a)

\displaystyle\bigl\|\gamma_{\|}R(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi\bigr\|_{L^{2}_{\beta-1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq{C}\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}_{\beta+1/2}},

(1.23b)

\displaystyle\bigl\|\gamma_{i}\gamma_{j}R(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi\bigr\|_{L^{2}_{\beta-1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq{C}\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}_{\beta+1/2}};\quad i,j=1,\dots,d.

(2)

For any

\beta^{\prime}\in(0,\beta_{c}/2)
,
t>1/2
and
R\geq R_{0}

there exists
C^{\prime}>0
, such that for all

\lambda\in I^{\prime}
and
\psi\in L^{2}_{\beta^{\prime}+t}

(1.23c)

\displaystyle\bigl\|\gamma_{i}R(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi\bigr\|_{L^{2}_{\beta^{\prime}-t}}

\displaystyle\leq{C^{\prime}}\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}_{\beta^{\prime}+t}};\quad i=1,\dots,d.

Remarks 1.24
.

(1)

These bounds are ‘strong’ in the sense that
\beta_{c}>1
,
while Isozaki
[
Is1
]
obtained similar bounds for classical
C^{3}

long-range potentials with
\beta_{c}<1
.
Such strong versions first appeared in
[
HS
]
for
classical
C^{\infty}
potentials.

(2)

Our proof is elementary,
relying only on the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the product rule for differentiation,
and it admits considerably weaker assumptions than in
[
Is1
,
HS
]
.
In fact, it applies even to
C^{3}
potentials by redefining appropriately

\gamma_{\|}

and
\beta_{c}>1
, see Remark
3.16
.

(3)

See Subsection
3.2
for a motivation from Classical Mechanics.
The third or higher order derivatives of
V
appear as purely ‘quantum effects’,
and there is no classical interpretation of their appearances.
Note also that in our setting these derivatives might
not have classical decay.

(4)

We may let
I={\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by letting
C,C^{\prime}>0
from
(
1.23a
)–(
1.23c
) be dependent on
\lambda\in I
, cf. Remark
1.4

2
.

(5)

The potential here is more smooth than in the previous subsection.
We will apply Theorem
1.23

to a regularized potential appearing as a technical tool, see Lemma
4.1
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
we prove Theorem
1.3

following the scheme of
[
CS
]
.
Section
3
is
devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.23
,
for which a commutator-type argument plays a central role.
After these preliminaries,
we prove Theorems
1.5
,
1.10
and
1.12
and Corollary
1.8

in Section
4
,
where we see that the strong radiation condition bounds provide simple and intuitive proofs.
Finally in Section
5
we prove Theorems
1.14
,
1.17
and
1.20
.

2.
Eikonal equation

In this section we prove Theorem
1.3
.
We will follow the framework of Cruz–Skibsted
[
CS
]
,
and solve (
1.6
) in a somewhat abstract manner.
The equation we investigate here is of the form

(2.1)

\displaystyle(\nabla\Phi)\cdot G^{-1}(\nabla\Phi)=1

with
G
being a given (
d\times d
)-matrix-valued function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
which is assumed to be sufficiently close to the identity matrix
I_{d}
.
Note that the
potential
eikonal equation (
1.6
)
is always translated into the
geometric
eikonal equation (
2.1
)
through the change of variables

(2.2)

\displaystyle\Phi=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}S,\quad G=\bigl(1-\lambda^{-1}\chi_{R}V\bigr)I_{d}.

Thus the arguments of this section on (
2.1
) will
readily apply to an
S
solving (
1.6
).

The paper
[
CS
]
adopts a variational method,
and we recall the precise setting in Subsection
2.1
,
quoting some results from there.
The existence and smoothness of a solution to (
2.1
) obtained in
[
CS
]

yield the corresponding assertions (
1
)–(
3
) of Theorem
1.3
.
Then in Subsection
2.2
we discuss
the remaining problems, i.e. the smoothness and the uniform bounds (
1.7
),
under our more restrictive assumption of our paper.
This completes the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
At the end of the section we present Corollary
2.14
,
a modification of (
1.7
), which will be necessary in
Sections
4
and
5
.

2.1.
Terminologies and results from
[
CS
]

2.1.1.
Class of Riemannian metrics

In this subsection we discuss the following class
\mathcal{M}_{d}^{l}
of functions with values in square matrices
of order
d
,
or of Riemannian metrics on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Denote the set of all the real symmetric matrices of order
d
by
\mathcal{S}_{d}({\mathbb{R}})
.

Definition 2.1
.

For any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we set

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{l}_{d}=\bigl\{G=(g_{ij})_{i,j}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{d}({\mathbb{R}})\,\big|\,G\text{ is of class }C^{l},\ \|G\|_{l}<\infty\bigr\}

along with

(2.3)

\begin{split}\|G\|_{l}=\sup\bigl\{&\langle x\rangle^{|\alpha|}\left|\partial^{\alpha}g_{ij}(x)\right|\,\big|\,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\ |\alpha|=0,\dots,l,\ i,j=1,\dots,d\bigr\}.\end{split}

In addition, we set

(2.4)

\mathcal{M}^{l}_{d}=\bigl\{G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{l}_{d}\mid\exists a,b>0\text{ s.t.\ }\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ aI_{d}\leq G(x)\leq bI_{d}\bigr\}.

Remark 2.2
.

Obviously

\mathcal{M}^{l}_{d}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{l}_{d}
is
open with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{l}
. Let
I
be any closed
interval in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(as in Theorem
1.3
).
If we then let
R>0
be large enough,

G
from (
2.2
) can be arbitrarily close to
I_{d}
in the
class
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{d}
uniformly in
\lambda\in I
. This is due to
the extra order of decay
\sigma\in(0,1)
in
(
1.2a
). This uniformity will be vital for our proof of Theorem
1.3
.

2.1.2.
Energy functional and geodesics

Next we define a
geodesic
for
G\in\mathcal{M}^{1}_{d}

in a variational manner.
For any
p\in(1,\infty)
we introduce the Banach space

(2.5)

{X^{p}=\big(W_{0}^{1,p}((0,1);\mathbb{R})\bigr)^{d},}\quad\|\kappa\|_{p}=\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{1}|\dot{\kappa}_{i}(t)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\Biggr)^{1/p}\ \ \text{for }\kappa\in X^{p},

although for the moment we shall only use the space
X:=X^{2}
,
which is a Hilbert space equipped with the inner product

\langle\mu,\nu\rangle_{X}=\langle\mu,\nu\rangle=\langle I_{d};\mu,\nu\rangle={\int_{0}^{1}\,\dot{\mu}(t)\cdot\dot{\nu}(t)\,{\rm d}t;}\quad\mu,\nu\in X.

For any
\kappa\in X
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

define a path
\kappa_{x}\in(H^{1}(0,1))^{d}
from
0
to
x
as

\kappa_{x}(t)=tx+\kappa(t)\ \ \text{for }t\in[0,1].

Now, given
G\in\mathcal{M}^{1}_{d}
, we consider the
energy functional

(2.6)

\mathcal{E}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times X\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}},\quad\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa)=\int_{0}^{1}\dot{\kappa}_{x}(t)\cdot G(\kappa_{x}(t))\dot{\kappa}_{x}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and denote the space of the paths
\kappa_{x}
by

(2.7)

X_{x}=\bigl\{\kappa_{x}\in(H^{1}(0,1))^{d}\,\big|\,\kappa\in X\bigr\}.

We call
\gamma_{x}\in X_{x}
a
geodesic for
G\in\mathcal{M}^{1}_{d}
from
0

to
x
in unit time
if the associated
\gamma\in X
satisfies

(2.8)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(x,\gamma)=0\in X^{\prime}.

Remarks 2.4
.

(1)

The duality pairing of

\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa)\in X^{\prime}

and
\mu\in X
is directly computed as

(2.9)

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa),\mu\bigr\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\{2\dot{\mu}\cdot G(\kappa_{x})\dot{\kappa}_{x}+\dot{\kappa}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{i}\partial_{i}G)(\kappa_{x})\dot{\kappa}_{x}\bigr\}\,{\rm d}t.

(2)

Classically, a geodesic for
G=(g_{ij})_{i,j}

is defined as a smooth curve
y=y(t)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
solving

(2.10)

\displaystyle\ddot{y}_{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}g^{ij}\bigl(\partial_{k}g_{jl}+\partial_{l}g_{jk}-\partial_{j}g_{kl}\bigr)\dot{y}_{k}\dot{y}_{l}=0\ \ \text{for }i=1,\dots,d,

where
G^{-1}=(g^{ij})_{i,j}
, or equivalently

(2.11)

G\ddot{y}=-((\dot{y}\cdot\nabla)G)\dot{y}+\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{y}\cdot(\nabla_{\bullet}G)\dot{y}.

Clearly (
2.8
) is a weak form of (
2.11
) according to the expression (
2.9
).
A geodesic in the sense of Definition
2.3

is equivalent to an
H^{1}
curve solving the
H^{-1}
equation (
2.11
).
Note that due to the Sobolev embedding theorem for
y\in(H^{1}(0,1))^{d}

the right-hand side of (
2.11
)
belongs to
(L^{1}(0,1))^{d}\subseteq(H^{-1}(0,1))^{d}
.

(3)

We shall use the notation
\gamma,\gamma_{x}
exclusively when
discussing geodesics,
and
\kappa,\kappa_{x}
or
\mu,\mu_{x}
for general paths.
There would be no confusion since, as long as
G
is close to
I_{d}
, a geodesic is unique,
see Theorem
2.6
.

For any
G\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{d}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

there always exists at least one geodesic from
0
to
x
, given as follows.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
G\in{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{d}
with
a,b>0
as in (
2.4
), and let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then there exists a minimizer
\gamma\in X

of
\mathcal{E}(x,\cdot)
, i.e.,

\mathcal{E}(x,\gamma)=\inf\{\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa)\,|\,\kappa\in X\},

and hence the associated
\gamma_{x}
is a geodesic for
G
.
Moreover, for any
t\in[0,1]

(2.12a)

\mathcal{E}(x,\gamma)=\dot{\gamma}_{x}(t)\cdot G(\gamma_{x}(t))\dot{\gamma}_{x}(t)

and

(2.12b)

\tfrac{a}{b}|x|^{2}\leq|\dot{\gamma}_{x}(t)|^{2}\leq\tfrac{b}{a}|x|^{2},\quad\tfrac{a}{b}|tx|^{2}\leq|\gamma_{x}(t)|^{2}\leq\tfrac{b}{a}|tx|^{2}.

Proof.

It is a part of
[
CS
, Lemma 1]
. We omit the proof.
∎

In general a geodesic is not necessarily associated with the minimizer of

\mathcal{E}(x,\cdot)
.
However it is the case when
G
is sufficiently close to
I_{d}
.

Theorem 2.6
.

There exists a neighbourhood
U\subseteq{\mathcal{M}}^{2}_{d}
of
I_{d}
such that
the following holds.

(1)

For any
G\in U
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
there exists a unique geodesic
\gamma_{x}\in X_{x}
.

(2)

There exists
c>0
such that for any
G\in U
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\kappa\in X

\bigl\langle(\partial_{\kappa}^{2}\mathcal{E})(x,\gamma);\kappa,\kappa\bigr\rangle\geq c\|\kappa\|_{2}^{2}.

Moreover, for any
G\in U\cap\mathcal{M}^{l}_{d}
with
l\geq 2
the map
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\ni x\mapsto\gamma\in X

from (
1
) is of class
C^{l-1}
.

Remark 2.7
.

The second derivative of
\mathcal{E}(x,\cdot)
reads for any
\kappa,\mu,\nu\in X
as

(2.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigl\langle\partial_{\kappa}^{2}\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa);\mu,\nu\bigr\rangle&=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(2\dot{\mu}\cdot G(\kappa_{x})\dot{\nu}+2\dot{\mu}\cdot(\nu_{i}\partial_{i}G)(\kappa_{x})\dot{\kappa}_{x}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}\qquad{}+2\dot{\nu}\cdot(\mu_{i}\partial_{i}G)(\kappa_{x})\dot{\kappa}_{x}+\dot{\kappa}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{i}\nu_{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}G)(\kappa_{x})\dot{\kappa}_{x}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}t.\end{split}

Proof.

The assertions (
1
) and (
2
)
follows from
[
CS
, Theorem 1 (a)]
, if we allow
c>0
to be dependent on
G\in U
.
However, if we let
U
be small enough,
we can choose
c
uniformly in
G\in U
due to
[
CS
, Lemma 6]
.
The last assertion follows from
[
CS
, Proposition 2 1)]
.
Hence we are done.
∎

2.1.3.
Solution to the eikonal equation

We are ready to construct and study a specific solution to the eikonal equation (
2.1
).

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{2}_{d}
be given as in Theorem
2.6
,
l\geq 2

and define for each
G\in U\cap\mathcal{M}^{l}_{d}

(2.14)

\Phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to[0,\infty),\quad\Phi(x)=\mathcal{E}(x,\gamma)^{1/2}=\bigl(\inf\{\mathcal{E}(x,\kappa)\,|\,\kappa\in X\}\bigr)^{1/2}.

Then for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
the identity

(2.15)

\displaystyle\nabla(\Phi(x)^{2})=2G(x)\dot{\gamma}_{x}(1)

holds.
Moreover,
\Phi
is a
C^{l}
solution to (
2.1
)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

The assertion is due to
[
CS
, (36)]
and
[
CS
, Proposition 2.2)]
,
and we omit the proof.
∎

Using the above results we can prove a part of Theorem
1.3
.
The proofs of the
(\lambda,x)
-smoothness and (
4
)
will given in Subsection
2.2
.

Proof of a part of Theorem
1.3
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
for some

l\geq 2
and let
I
be any given closed interval in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(as in the theorem).

(
1
)
Here we let

G(\lambda,x)=\bigl(1-\lambda^{-1}\chi_{R}(x)V(x)\bigr)I_{d}

as in (
2.2
).
Let
U\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{2}_{d}
be given as in Theorem
2.6
,
and take large
R>0
,
so that
G(\lambda,\cdot)\in U\cap\mathcal{M}^{l}_{d}
for all
\lambda\in I
.
Then for such
G(\lambda,\cdot)
we can find a solution

\Phi(\lambda,\cdot)\in C^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
to (
2.1
)
by Theorem
2.8
.
Thus, if we set

(2.16)

\displaystyle S(\lambda,\cdot)=(2\lambda)^{1/2}\Phi(\lambda,\cdot)\in C^{l}((\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})

as in (
2.2
), it obviously solves (
1.6
).
We actually have joint smoothness
S\in C^{l}(I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}))
,
but we will verify it later.

(
2
)
By the definition (
2.14
) and (
2.12a
) it follows
that

\Phi(\lambda,x)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{E}(x,\gamma)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\dot{\gamma}_{x}(t)\cdot G(\gamma_{x}(t))\dot{\gamma}_{x}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Hence
\Phi(\lambda,x)
is the distance with
respect to the Riemannian metric
(2\lambda)^{-1}g
from the
origin to
x

along

\gamma_{x}
. Using again (
2.12a
) and the fact that
\Phi

solves (
2.1
) it follows that indeed

\Phi(\lambda,x)
is the geodesic distance from the origin to
x
.
Obviously then
S(\lambda,\cdot)
is the geodesic distance from
the origin with respect to
g
.

(
3
)
Set for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}

s(\lambda,x)=|x|^{-1}\Phi(\lambda,x)-1=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}|x|^{-1}S(\lambda,x)-1.

Then
s(\lambda,\cdot)\in C^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\})
,
and it satisfies the asserted identity.
Furthermore it extends
smoothly to the origin by letting
s(\lambda,0)=0
.
In fact we have
G(\lambda,x)=I
for
|x|\leq R
, and
this implies
\Phi(\lambda,x)=|x|
or
s(\lambda,x)=0
there.
The joint smoothness of
s
follows from that of
S

(in turn to be given in Subsection
2.2
).
∎

2.2.
Improved bounds

In this subsection we prove the remaining assertions of Theorem
1.3
.
As for (
1.7
), note that the similar bounds from
[
CS
, Theorem 1 (b)]
do not suffice,
so we have to argue properly with the more restrictive assumptions of
the present paper.
The following proposition is a key for our proof.
See (
2.5
) for the definitions of
X^{p}
and

\|\cdot\|_{p}
. The appearing curve
\gamma\in X
comes
from using Theorem
2.8
with the considered
G
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
for some

l\geq 2
and let
I
be any given closed
interval in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(as in Theorem
1.3
).
Consider the matrix
G=\bigl(1-\lambda^{-1}\chi_{R}V\bigr)I_{d}
with

\lambda\in I
and
R>0
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
there exists
C>0
such that
uniformly in all sufficiently large
R>0
,
k+|\alpha|=1,\dots,l-1
,

p\in[1+\delta,1+\delta^{-1}]
,

\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma]
with
\sigma^{\prime}p\leq 1-\delta
and

(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the corresponding
\gamma\in X

obeys

(2.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\gamma\|_{p}\leq C\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(k+\lvert\alpha\rvert+1)+k},\end{split}

where
m^{\prime}(k)=m(k)-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}
.

Remarks 2.10
.

(1)

Note that
\gamma=0
for
|x|\leq R
.
Thus
|x|
on the right-hand side of (
2.17
) can be replaced by
\langle x\rangle
.

(2)

Obviously the strongest
x
-decay is achived choosing

\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
in (
2.17
). This will be used in the proof of (
1.7
)
simply by using the estimate for any
p\in(1,\infty)
with
\sigma p<1
.
The (local) uniformity in
p
and
\sigma^{\prime}
in the assertion is technically necessary
for the proof of Proposition
2.9
itself,
which relies on induction in
l
. However our proof does not extend to
the assertion of having uniformity in
p\in(1,\infty)
rather than
the stated condition
p\in[1+\delta,1+\delta^{-1}]
.

In the proof of Proposition
2.9

we will repeatedly use the following
Hardy

and
generalized Hölder inequalities
.

Lemma 2.11
.

For any
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\kappa\in X^{p}
one has

(2.18)

\displaystyle\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{1}|t^{-1}\kappa_{i}(t)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\Biggr)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{p}{p-1}\|\kappa\|_{p}.

Proof.

The assertion follows from the one for
d=1
, which is the well-known Hardy inequality.
We omit the details.

∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\in[1,\infty]
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfy

p_{1}^{-1}+\dots+p_{n}^{-1}=1
.
Then for any
f_{i}\in L^{p_{i}}(0,1)
,
i=1,\dots,n
, one has

(2.19)

\int_{0}^{1}|f_{1}(t)|\cdots|f_{n}(t)|\,{\rm d}t\leq\|f_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}}\cdots\|f_{n}\|_{L^{p_{n}}}.

Proof.

The assertion follows easily by repeated application of the familiar Hölder’s inequality.
We omit the details.
∎

The below lemma will be useful in a reduction procedure in the proof of Proposition
2.9
,
see Steps I, III and IV there.

Lemma 2.13
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)

take a sufficiently small neighbourhood
U\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{d}^{2}
of
I_{d}
.
Then it follows that uniformly in

p,q\in[1+\delta,1+\delta^{-1}]
with
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
,
G\in U
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mu\in X\cap X^{p}
and
\nu\in X\cap X^{q}
, the
corresponding
\gamma\in X
from Theorem
2.6
obeys

\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(x,\gamma)-2I_{d};\mu,\nu\bigr\rangle\bigr|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|\mu\|_{p}\|\nu\|_{q}.

Proof.

Take any
\delta\in(0,1)
.
We first let
U\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{2}_{d}
be small enough that
not only Theorem
2.6
is available,
but also we can find
a,b>0
as in (
2.4
) uniformly in
G\in U
.
Then by (
2.13
),
(
2.12b
), Hölder’s inequality and Lemma
2.11

we can bound

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(x,\gamma)-2I_{d};\mu,\nu\bigr\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl|2\dot{\mu}\cdot(G-I_{d})\dot{\nu}+2\dot{\mu}\cdot(\nu_{i}\partial_{i}G)\dot{\gamma}_{x}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+2\dot{\nu}\cdot(\mu_{i}\partial_{i}G)\dot{\gamma}_{x}+\dot{\gamma}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{i}\nu_{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}G)\dot{\gamma}_{x}\Bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\|G-I_{d}\|_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(|\dot{\mu}||\dot{\nu}|+|\dot{\mu}||\nu|\langle tx\rangle^{-1}|x|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ +|\dot{\nu}||\mu|\langle tx\rangle^{-1}|x|+|\mu||\nu|\langle tx\rangle^{-2}|x|^{2}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\|G-I_{d}\|_{2}\|\mu\|_{p}\|\nu\|_{q},

where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
only depend on the constants
\delta,a,b
.
Thus, possibly by letting
U
be smaller, the assertion follows.
∎

With these preparations we can now prove Proposition
2.9
.

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

The proof proceeds by induction in
l
with the
interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
being fixed. Note that the assertion of the
proposition amounts to the statement that for each

l\geq 2
the bounds (
2.17
) hold uniformly in various
parameters. This is a statement amenable to
induction.

Fix any

l\geq 2
and
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
as in the proposition.
Fix also any
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then
the conclusion of Lemma
2.13
is available for

G=(1-\lambda^{-1}\chi_{R}V)I
uniformly in
\lambda\in I
and all
sufficiently large
R>0
.
In particular it follows
from the classical implicit function theorem (see e.g.
[
De
, Theorem 15.1]
or
[
Ir
, Theorem C.7]
) applied to the equation
(
2.8
) with parameter
(\lambda,x)
, that
the mapping

(2.20)

\displaystyle I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to X,\ \ (\lambda,x)\mapsto\gamma,

is of class
C^{l-1}
, cf. Theorem
2.6
.

Step I.
We first let
l=2
,
and we start with the case
k=0
and
|\alpha|=1
.
It suffices to show that
we have
uniformly in
i=1,\dots,d
,
p,q\in[1+\delta,1+\delta^{-1}]
with
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
,

\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma]
with
\sigma^{\prime}p\leq 1-\delta
,
(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

and
\mu\in X\cap X^{q}

(2.21)

\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\bigr|\leq C_{1}\lambda^{-1}|x|^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\|\mu\|_{q}.

In fact if (
2.21
) holds true,
we obtain in
combination with Lemma
2.13
that

(2.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\langle\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu\rangle|&\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl|\bigl\langle 2I_{d}-(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\bigr|+\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\bigr|\\
&\leq\tfrac{1}{4}\|\partial_{i}\gamma\|_{p}\|\mu\|_{q}+\tfrac{C_{1}}{2}\lambda^{-1}|x|^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\|\mu\|_{q}.\end{split}

Note that any
\mu\in X\cap X^{q}
takes the form

\mu(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\big(g(s)-\int_{0}^{1}g(\tau){\rm d}\tau\big){\rm d}s
with
g\in L^{2}((0,1);{\mathbb{R}})^{d}\cap L^{q}((0,1);{\mathbb{R}})^{d}
, and vice versa. Using arbitrary
g
in
this class,
the standard
L^{p}
-
L^{q}
duality argument and density of

L^{2}\cap L^{q}
in
L^{q}
allow us to compute

\displaystyle\|\partial_{i}\dot{\gamma}\|_{L^{p}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|g\|_{L^{q}}\leq 1}\,|\langle\partial_{i}\dot{\gamma},g\rangle_{L^{2}}|=\sup_{\|g\|_{L^{q}}\leq 1}\,|\langle\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu_{g}\rangle|;

\displaystyle\mu_{g}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\Big(g(s)-\int_{0}^{1}g(\tau){\rm d}\tau\Big){\rm d}s,\quad t\in(0,1).

Noting that

\|\mu_{g}\|_{q}=\|\dot{\mu}_{g}\|_{L^{q}}\leq 2\|g\|_{L^{q}},

it now follows from (
2.22
) that

\|\partial_{i}\gamma\|_{p}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|\partial_{i}\gamma\|_{p}+C_{1}\lambda^{-1}|x|^{-\sigma^{\prime}},

yielding the assertion (
2.17
) for
k=0
and
|\alpha|=1
with
C=C_{2}=2C_{1}
.

Step II.
Here we prove (
2.21
). For that
we first claim that for any
\mu\in X

(2.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle={}&\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(2\dot{\mu}\cdot(I_{d}-G(\lambda,\gamma_{x}))e_{i}-2t\dot{\mu}\cdot(\partial_{i}G)(\lambda,\gamma_{x})\dot{\gamma}_{x}\\
&\qquad{}-2\dot{\gamma}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{j}\partial_{j}G)(\lambda,\gamma_{x})e_{i}\\
&\qquad{}-t\dot{\gamma}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}G)(\lambda,\gamma_{x})\dot{\gamma}_{x}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}t.\end{split}

In fact, if we differentiate (
2.8
) in
x_{i}
, it follows that
for any
\mu\in X

(2.24)

\displaystyle\bigl\langle(\partial_{i}\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma),\mu\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle=0.

On the other hand, also differentiating (
2.9
) in
x_{i}
,
we have for any
\kappa,\mu\in X

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\partial_{i}\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{E}(\lambda,x,\kappa),\mu\bigr\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(

\displaystyle 2\dot{\mu}\cdot G(\lambda,\kappa_{x})e_{i}+2t\dot{\mu}\cdot(\partial_{i}G)(\lambda,\kappa_{x})\dot{\kappa}_{x}

\displaystyle{}+2\dot{\kappa}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{j}\partial_{j}G)(\lambda,\kappa_{x})e_{i}+t\dot{\kappa}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}G)(\lambda,\kappa_{x})\dot{\kappa}_{x}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}t.

We obtain (
2.23
) by
combining the above identities and using that
\mu(0)=\mu(1)=0
.

Next we estimate the right-hand side of (
2.23
).
By using (
2.12b
), Hölder’s inequality and the Hardy inequality (
2.18
)
we have uniformly in the relevant parameters

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{i}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\lambda^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl(|\dot{\mu}||tx|^{-\sigma^{\prime}}+t|\dot{\mu}||tx|^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}}|x|

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\qquad\qquad\quad{}+|\mu||tx|^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}}|x|+t|\mu||tx|^{-2-\sigma^{\prime}}|x|^{2}\Bigr)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\lambda^{-1}|x|^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(|\dot{\mu}|t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}+|\mu|t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigr)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}\lambda^{-1}|x|^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\|\mu\|_{q}.

We have shown (
2.21
) and hence indeed (
2.17
) for
k=0
and
|\alpha|=1
.

Step III.
Next we prove the assertion for
k=1
and
|\alpha|=0

along the lines of Steps I and II.
In fact, we can first reduce it to proving

(2.25)

\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{\lambda}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\bigr|\leq C_{6}\lambda^{-2}|x|^{1-\sigma^{\prime}}\|\mu\|_{q}

uniformly in the relevant parameters.
The reasoning is the same as in Step I with Lemma
2.13
,
and we omit it.
Then in order to prove (
2.25
) we deduce the
following expression valid for any
\mu\in X
:

(2.26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{\lambda}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle={}&\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(-2\dot{\mu}\cdot(\partial_{\lambda}G)(\lambda,\gamma_{x})\dot{\gamma}_{x}\\
&\qquad{}-\dot{\gamma}_{x}\cdot(\mu_{j}\partial_{j}\partial_{\lambda}G)(\lambda,\gamma_{x})\dot{\gamma}_{x}\bigr)\,{\rm d}t.\end{split}

Again, this formula follows in a manner similar to the first part of Step II
(similar, but actually slightly simpler),
and we omit the proof.
Then we bound the right-hand side of (
2.26
)
by using (
2.12b
), Hölder’s inequality and the Hardy inequality (
2.18
)

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial_{\lambda}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq C_{7}\lambda^{-2}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(|\dot{\mu}||tx|^{-\sigma^{\prime}}|x|+|\mu||tx|^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}}|x|^{2}\bigr)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{8}\lambda^{-2}|x|^{1-\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(|\dot{\mu}|t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}+|\mu|t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigr)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{9}\lambda^{-2}|x|^{1-\sigma^{\prime}}\|\mu\|_{q}.

This amounts to (
2.25
), and we have shown (
2.17
) for
k=1
and
|\alpha|=0
.

Step IV.
From here to the end of the proof we let
l\geq 3
.
It suffices to discuss only the case
k+|\alpha|=l-1

since the cases
k+|\alpha|=1,\dots,l-2
follow by the induction hypothesis.
In the following let for short

\beta=(k,\alpha)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d+1},\quad|\beta|=k+|\alpha|=l-1,\quad\partial^{\beta}=\partial_{\lambda,x}^{\beta}=\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}.

Here we only note that the proof is reduced to verifying

(2.27)

\bigl|\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial^{\beta}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\bigr|\leq C_{1}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(l)+k}\|\mu\|_{q}

uniformly in
\mu\in X\cap X^{q}
and the relevant parameters.
This is indeed a valid reduction, which may be seen by mimicking
Step I. Again we omit the details.

Step V.
To show (
2.27
) we proceed in parallel to Steps II and III,
differentiating (
2.8
). Indeed repeated differentiation of (
2.8
)
in
(\lambda,x)
yields the Faà di Bruno formula

(2.28)

\begin{split}&\bigl\langle(\partial^{2}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial^{\beta}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle\\
&=\sum_{n,\beta^{0},\dots,\beta^{n}}C_{*}\bigl\langle(\partial^{\beta^{0}}\partial^{n+1}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma);\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma,\dots,\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma,\mu\bigr\rangle,\end{split}

where the indices
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\beta^{i}=(k^{i},\alpha^{i})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
,

i=0,\dots,n
, run over

(2.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&n=0,\dots,l-1,\quad\beta^{0}+\dots+\beta^{n}=\beta,\quad 1\leq|\beta^{i}|\leq l-2\ \ \text{for }i=1,\dots,n.\end{split}

Here and below combinatorial constants are denoted simply by
C_{*}\in\mathbb{{\mathbb{N}}}

without distinction.

Let us further write down a detailed expression of the summand of (
2.28
).
Directly differentiating the definition (
2.6
),
we can compute
\partial^{n+1}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E}(\lambda,x,\kappa)
as

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\partial^{n+1}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E}(\lambda,x,\kappa);\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{n+1}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\eta|=n+1}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}(\partial_{x}^{\eta}g_{ab})(\dot{\kappa}_{x})_{a}(\dot{\kappa}_{x})_{b}\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\sum_{|\eta|=n}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}(\partial_{x}^{\eta}g_{ab})(\dot{\kappa}_{x})_{b}\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\dot{\mu}^{i}_{a}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\sum_{|\eta|=n-1}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}(\partial_{x}^{\eta}g_{ab})\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\dot{\mu}^{i}_{a}\cdots\dot{\mu}^{j}_{b}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t.

Here and
henceforth
\dot{\mu}^{i}_{a}
and
\dot{\mu}^{j}_{b}
replace the corresponding factors,
and for short we omit appropriate summations in

a,b,c_{1},\dots,c_{n+1},i,j
as well as a
specification of
the combinatorial constants
C_{*}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Also we abbreviate
(\partial_{x}^{\eta}g_{ab})(\kappa_{x})=(\partial_{x}^{\eta}g_{ab})
.
We further proceed, applying
\partial^{\beta^{0}}
, as

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\partial^{\beta^{0}}\partial^{n+1}_{\kappa}\mathcal{E}(\lambda,x,\kappa);\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{n+1}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{|\eta|=n+1,}{e_a+e_b\le\alpha^0}}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|-2}\bigl(\partial^{(k^{0},\alpha^{0}-e_{a}-e_{b}+\eta)}g_{ab}\bigr)\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{|\eta|=n+1,}{e_b\le\alpha^0}}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|-1}\bigl(\partial^{(k^{0},\alpha^{0}-e_{b}+\eta)}g_{ab}\bigr)(\dot{\kappa}_{x})_{a}\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\sum_{|\eta|=n+1}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|}\bigl(\partial^{(k^{0},\alpha^{0}+\eta)}g_{ab}\bigr)(\dot{\kappa}_{x})_{a}(\dot{\kappa}_{x})_{b}\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{|\eta|=n,}{e_b\le\alpha^0}}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|-1}\bigl(\partial^{(k^{0},\alpha^{0}-e_{b}+\eta)}g_{ab}\bigr)\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\dot{\mu}^{i}_{a}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\sum_{|\eta|=n}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|}\bigl(\partial^{(k^{0},\alpha^{0}+\eta)}g_{ab}\bigr)(\dot{\kappa}_{x})_{b}\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\dot{\mu}^{i}_{a}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\sum_{|\eta|=n-1}C_{*}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|}\bigl(\partial^{(k^{0},\alpha^{0}+\eta)}g_{ab}\bigr)\mu^{1}_{c_{1}}\cdots\dot{\mu}^{i}_{a}\cdots\dot{\mu}^{j}_{b}\cdots\mu^{n+1}_{c_{n+1}}\,{\rm d}t.

We substitute
\kappa=\gamma,\mu^{1}=\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma,\dots,\mu^{n}=\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma

and
\mu^{n+1}=\mu

in the above formula, and denote the corresponding
six types of integrals on the
right-hand side simply as
I_{1},\dots,I_{6}
, respectively.
Then it suffices to estimate each such integral in agreement with (
2.27
).

Step VI.
Finally we bound the above
I_{1},\dots,I_{6}
.
They are treated similarly
by using (
1.2a
), (
2.12b
),
the generalized Hölder inequality (
2.19
),
the Hardy inequality (
2.18
) and the induction hypothesis.
We may let
|x|\geq R
below, see Remark
2.10

1
.

We carefully bound
I_{1}
,
while we record only key steps for
I_{2},\dots,I_{6}
(they are treated similarly).
Use (
1.2a
), (
2.12b
) and
|\eta|=n+1
, to bound it for
|x|\geq R
as

(2.30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|I_{1}|&\leq C_{2}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|-2}|tx|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+|\eta|-2)}\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t\\
&\leq C_{3}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n-1)}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|t^{-1}\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t.\end{split}

We take large
p_{0},\dots,p_{n}\in(1,\infty)
and small

\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\sigma^{\prime}_{n}\in(0,\sigma]
such that

p_{0}^{-1}+\dots+p_{n+1}^{-1}=1-q^{-1}=p^{-1},\quad\sigma^{\prime}p_{0}<1,\quad\sigma^{\prime}_{1}p_{1}<1,\ \dots,\ \sigma^{\prime}_{n+1}p_{n+1}<1,

and apply the generalized Hölder inequality (
2.19
) and the Hardy inequality (
2.18
)
to (
2.30
). Note that here and henceforth
\delta\in(0,1)
,
p\in[1+\delta,1+\delta^{-1}]
and

\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma]
with
\sigma^{\prime}p\leq 1-\delta
are given parameters
as in Proposition
2.9
.
Then we use the induction hypothesis with a
sufficiently smaller
\delta\in(0,1)
(to make sure that
p_{i}\in[1+\delta,1+\delta^{-1}]
and
\sigma^{\prime}p_{i}\leq 1-\delta
)
and with
m^{\prime}_{i}(k)=m(k)-\sigma+\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
.

Noting also (
2.29
) and
|\beta^{0}|-k^{0}+n+1=|\alpha^{0}|+n+1\geq 2

by
e_{a}+e_{b}\leq\alpha^{0}
,
it follows that for
|x|\geq R

(2.31)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|I_{1}|&\leq C_{4}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n+1)}\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{n}}\|\mu\|_{q}\\
&\leq C_{5}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\beta^{0}|-k^{0}+n+1)+(2-m_{1}^{\prime}(|\beta^{1}|+1)+k^{1})+\dots+(2-m_{n}^{\prime}(|\beta^{n}|+1)+k^{n})}\|\mu\|_{q}\\
&\leq C_{6}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\beta|-k^{0}+1)+k-k^{0}}\|\mu\|_{q}\\
&\leq C_{7}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(l)+k}\|\mu\|_{q}.\end{split}

This agrees with (
2.27
), as wanted.

As for
I_{2},\dots,I_{6}
, we aim at deducing a bound similar to the first line of (
2.31
),
since then the remaining arguments are essentially the
same.
More precisely, for the cases where
|\beta^{0}|-k^{0}+n+1\geq 2
is valid,
this condition and the induction hypothesis suffice for the
final conclusion of (
2.31
). If
|\beta^{0}|-k^{0}+n+1=1
we
can proceed similarly to reach from the first line of
(
2.31
) to the final line of the estimation.

We can bound
I_{2}
similarly as

\displaystyle|I_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{8}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|-1}|tx|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+|\eta|-1)}|x|\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{9}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{1-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|t^{-1}\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{10}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n+1)}\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{n}}\|\mu\|_{q},

and from there we proceed as in (
2.31
) (as explained above).
As for
I_{3}
, we bound it as

\displaystyle|I_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{11}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|}|tx|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+|\eta|)}|x|^{2}\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{12}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n+1)}\int_{0}^{1}|t|^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|t^{-1}\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{13}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n+1)}\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{n}}\|\mu\|_{q}.

From
I_{4}
there appears a factor which is either directly bounded by

\|\partial^{\beta^{*}}\gamma\|_{p_{*}}

without the Hardy bound, or alternatively a factor
bounded by the
L^{q}
-norm of
\dot{\mu}
. Hence typically
I_{4}
and
I_{5}
are bounded as

\displaystyle|I_{4}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{14}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|-1}|tx|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+|\eta|-1)}\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{i}}\dot{\gamma}_{a}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{15}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n-1)}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{i}}\dot{\gamma}_{a}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|t^{-1}\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{16}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n+1)}\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{n}}\|\mu\|_{q},

and

\displaystyle|I_{5}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{17}\lambda^{-1-k_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|}|tx|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+|\eta|)}|x|\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{i}}\dot{\gamma}_{a}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{18}\lambda^{-1-k_{0}}|x|^{1-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n)}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{i}}\dot{\gamma}_{a}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|t^{-1}\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{19}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n+1)}\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{n}}\|\mu\|_{q}.

Finally we can typically bound
I_{6}
as

\displaystyle|I_{6}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{20}\lambda^{-1-k_{0}}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}t^{|\alpha^{0}|}|tx|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+|\eta|)}\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{i}}\dot{\gamma}_{a}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{j}}\dot{\gamma}_{b}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\bigl|\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{21}\lambda^{-1-k_{0}}|x|^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n-1)}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma_{c_{1}}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{i}}\dot{\gamma}_{a}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|\partial^{\beta^{j}}\dot{\gamma}_{b}\bigr|\cdots\bigl|t^{-1}\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma_{c_{n}}\bigr|\bigl|t^{-1}\mu_{c_{n+1}}\bigr|\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{22}\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{2-m^{\prime}(|\alpha^{0}|+n+1)}\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{1}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{1}}\cdots\bigl\|\partial^{\beta^{n}}\gamma\bigr\|_{p_{n}}\|\mu\|_{q}.

Therefore we are done.
∎

We complete the proof of Theorem
1.3
as follows.

Completion of the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Fix a closed interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
with
\sigma p<1
,
and let
R>0
be large as in Proposition
2.9
.
For any
k+|\alpha|\leq l
it suffices to argue for
|x|>R
since
s
vanishes for
|x|\leq R
.
All the estimates below are tacitly understood to be uniform in
\lambda\in I
.

Step I.
We first show that for any
k+|\alpha|\leq l-1
and
|x|>R

(2.32)

\lvert\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\dot{\gamma}(1)\rvert\leq C_{1}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m(k+\lvert\alpha\rvert+1)+k}.

We may let
k+|\alpha|\geq 1
since the case
k+|\alpha|=0

follows from (
2.32
) with
k=0
and
|\alpha|=1
and integration.
Now let us use a representation

(2.33)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\dot{\gamma})(1)

\displaystyle=2\int^{1}_{1/2}\biggl((\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\dot{\gamma})(t)+\int^{1}_{t}(\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\ddot{\gamma})(\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau\biggr)\,{\rm d}t.

This is due to the fundamental theorem of calculus,
but in fact we have to check integrability of the integrands.

By Proposition
2.9
and Hölder’s inequality it follows that

(2.34)

\lVert\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\dot{\gamma}\rVert_{L^{1}(1/2,1)^{d}}\leq C_{2}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m(k+|\alpha|+1)+k},

where the norm on the left-hand side denotes the
L^{1}
-norm
of a function on the interval
(1/2,1)
with values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

On the other hand, as for

\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\ddot{\gamma}
, we compute it using
the expression

(2.35)

\displaystyle\ddot{\gamma}_{n}=-\tfrac{1}{2}g^{nl}\bigl(\partial_{i}g_{jl}+\partial_{j}g_{il}-\partial_{l}g_{ij}\bigr)(\dot{\gamma}_{x})_{i}(\dot{\gamma}_{x})_{j}=:-\Gamma^{n}_{ij}(\dot{\gamma}_{x})_{i}(\dot{\gamma}_{x})_{j}

following from (
2.10
).
By (
2.35
), the product rule and the chain rule of
differentiation
we can write

(2.36)

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\ddot{\gamma}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum C_{*}\bigl(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{0}}\partial_{x}^{\eta}\Gamma^{n}_{ij}\bigr)\Biggl(\prod_{a=1}^{d}\prod_{b=1}^{\eta_{a}}\bigl(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{ab}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{ab}}\gamma_{x}\bigr)_{a}\Biggr)\bigl(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{1}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}\dot{\gamma}_{x}\bigr)_{i}\bigl(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{2}}\dot{\gamma}_{x}\bigr)_{j},

where the indices
k^{0},k^{ab},k^{1},k^{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and

\eta,\alpha^{ab},\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
run over

(2.37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&k^{ab}+|\alpha^{ab}|\geq 1,\quad k^{0}+\sum_{a=1}^{d}\sum_{b=1}^{\eta_{a}}k^{ab}+k^{1}+k^{2}=k,\\
&|\eta|=0,\dots,k+|\alpha|,\quad\sum_{a=1}^{d}\sum_{b=1}^{\eta_{a}}\alpha^{ab}+\alpha^{1}+\alpha^{2}=\alpha.\end{split}

Note we read

\prod_{b=1}^{\eta_{a}}\bigl(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{ab}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{ab}}\gamma_{x}\bigr)_{a}=1

if
\eta_{a}=0
.

We recall that
\gamma_{x}(\tau)=\tau x+\gamma(\tau)
, and since

\tau\in[1/2,1]
, we do not have to worry about inverse powers of

\tau
when estimating
\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{0}}\partial_{x}^{\eta}\Gamma^{n}_{ij}
by Lemma
2.5

(as we did in the proof of Proposition
2.9
). On the other hand when taking
x
-derivatives of
\gamma_{x}
,
there are still contributions from differentiating the first term
\tau x
, that
in addition to the derivatives of
\gamma
need consideration.
The latter are treated by Proposition
2.9
.
We prefer the following uniform treatment,

(2.38a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lVert\big(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{ab}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{ab}}\gamma_{x}\big)_{a}\rVert_{L^{p}(1/2,1)}&\leq C_{p}\lambda^{-k^{ab}}|x|^{-\frac{\rho+1}{2}(k^{ab}+|\alpha^{ab}|-1)+k^{ab}},\\
\lVert\big(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{1}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}\dot{\gamma}_{x}\big)_{i}\rVert_{L^{p}(1/2,1)}&\leq C_{p}\lambda^{-k^{1}}|x|^{1-\frac{\rho+1}{2}(k^{1}+|\alpha^{1}|)+k^{1}},\\
\lVert\big(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{2}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{2}}\dot{\gamma}_{x}\big)_{j}\rVert_{L^{p}(1/2,1)}&\leq C_{p}\lambda^{-k^{2}}|x|^{1-\frac{\rho+1}{2}(k^{2}+|\alpha^{2}|)+k^{2}}.\end{split}

For any term with
\eta\neq 0
in the expansion (
2.36
), say denoted by

T
, we can show an estimate agreeing with (
2.34
). In fact
by using (
2.12b
), (
2.37
), (
2.38a
)
with
p=\lvert\eta\rvert+2
, the generalized Hölder
inequality (
2.19
) (with this
p
applied to each of the

p
factors), the Hardy inequality (
2.18
) and
Proposition
2.9
we can estimate

\displaystyle\lVert T\rVert_{L^{1}(1/2,1)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\sum\lambda^{-1-k^{0}}|x|^{-m(|\eta|+1)}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\Biggl(\prod_{a=1}^{d}\prod_{b=1}^{\eta_{a}}\,\lambda^{-k^{ab}}|x|^{-\frac{\rho+1}{2}(k^{ab}+|\alpha^{ab}|-1)+k^{ab}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\lambda^{-k^{1}}|x|^{1-\frac{\rho+1}{2}(k^{1}+|\alpha^{1}|)+k^{1}}\,\lambda^{-k^{2}}|x|^{1-\frac{\rho+1}{2}(k^{2}+|\alpha^{2}|)+k^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m(k+|\alpha|+1)+k}.

For any term with
\eta=0
in the expansion (
2.36
)
we use the following estimates.
For
k^{1}+|\alpha^{1}|\geq 1
the second bound of
(
2.38a
) can be
replaced by a sharper bound (similar to the first bound of
(
2.38a
)),

(2.38b)

\lVert\big(\partial_{\lambda}^{k^{1}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{1}}\dot{\gamma}_{x}\big)_{i}\rVert_{L^{p}(1/2,1)}\leq C_{p}^{\prime}\lambda^{-k^{1}}|x|^{-\frac{\rho+1}{2}(k^{1}+|\alpha^{1}|-1)+k^{1}},\quad k^{1}+|\alpha^{1}|\geq 1,

and similarly for the third bound for

k^{2}+|\alpha^{2}|\geq 1
.

Thanks to these sharper bounds we can then argue similarly for the case
\eta=0
, obtaining an estimate
for any such term also agreeing with (
2.34
). This is
straightforward, and we omit the details. Hence we conclude that

(2.39)

\|\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\ddot{\gamma}\|_{L^{1}(1/2,1)^{d}}\leq C_{5}\lambda^{-1-k}|x|^{2-m(k+|\alpha|+1)+k}.

Clearly (
2.33
) and the bounds (
2.34
) and
(
2.39
) yield (
2.32
).

Step II.
Now to check the smoothness of
S
and

s
in
(\lambda,x)
up to order
l
, it suffices for partial
derivatives with
\alpha\neq 0
to
prove the continuity of derivatives
of
\dot{\gamma}_{x}(1)
(or of
\dot{\gamma}(1)
