) up to order
l-1
. Here
we use (
2.16
) and (
2.15
). However this is already
implicitly done in our treatment of
(
2.33
) in Step I, which is based on the
C^{l-1}
-smoothness
of (
2.20
) from Proposition
2.9
. For derivatives with
\alpha=0
, i.e. containing only
\lambda
-derivatives we differentiate (
2.14
) in
\lambda
, leading (thanks to the stationarity condition

(\partial_{\kappa}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma)=0
) to

2|x|\Phi(\partial_{\lambda}s)=(\partial_{\lambda}\mathcal{E})(\lambda,x,\gamma)=\lambda^{-2}\int_{0}^{1}\chi_{R}V\lvert\dot{\gamma}_{x}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}t,

from which we deduce the formula

(2.40a)

\partial_{\lambda}s=2^{-1}|x|^{-2}(1+s)^{-1}\lambda^{-2}\int_{0}^{1}\chi_{R}V\lvert\dot{\gamma}_{x}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}t.

Inductively we can differentiate
(
2.40b
) up
to

l-1
times in
\lambda
, manifestly yielding continuous expressions for

\partial^{k}_{\lambda}s
,
k=1,\dots,l
.

Step III.
We are left with (
1.7
).
Integrating the expression (
2.15
) using (
2.2
) and (
2.32
) for
k+\lvert\alpha\rvert=0
,
we obtain

\displaystyle S(\lambda,x)^{2}=(2\lambda)\bigl(x^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\lambda^{-1}|x|^{2-\sigma}\bigr)\bigr),

and hence (
1.7
) for
k+|\alpha|=0
.
Next by (
2.15
), (
2.2
) and Theorem
1.3

(
3
) we can write

(2.40b)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}s=|x|^{-2}(1+s)^{-1}\Bigl[\dot{\gamma}(1)-\lambda^{-1}x\chi_{R}V-\lambda^{-1}\chi_{R}V\dot{\gamma}(1)-xs(2+s)\Bigr].

Using this representation and (
2.32
) we can inductively obtain
(
1.7
) for
k=0
and all
|\alpha|\leq l
. The
straightforward details are omitted. Along the same line any general
mixed derivative can be computed and estimated from
(
2.40b
). Hence
we conclude (
1.7
) for all

k+|\alpha|\leq l
, assuming
\alpha\neq 0
.

To treat pure
\lambda
-derivatives we use (
2.40a
), leading to

\lvert\partial_{\lambda}s\rvert\leq C_{6}\lambda^{-2}\lvert x\rvert^{-2}\int_{0}^{1}\lvert tx\rvert^{-\sigma}\,\lvert x\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}t=C_{7}\lambda^{-2}\lvert x\rvert^{-\sigma},

showing
(
1.7
) for
k=1
and
\alpha=0
.
We can inductively show (
1.7
) for
|\alpha|=0
and
all
k\leq l
by repeated
\lambda
-differentiation of
(
2.40a
) and check of the
resulting expressions. This is
more complicated than for mixed derivatives, so let us give some
details of the proof.
We compute
\partial^{k}_{\lambda}\int_{0}^{1}\chi_{R}V\lvert\dot{\gamma}_{x}\rvert^{2}\,{\rm d}t
by differentiating inside the integral using the
product rule. Since
(\chi_{R}V)(\gamma_{x}(t))
is a composition we need
the chain rule to compute
\lambda
-derivatives of this factor, more
or less as we did in Step I. This leads to multiple factors of

\partial^{k}_{\lambda}\gamma_{x}(t)/t
for which we have good

L^{p}
-bounds. So the main thing is to bound the derivatives

(\partial_{x}^{\eta}(\chi_{R}V))(\gamma_{x}(t))
of the external factor, and
for that we mimic Step VI of the proof of Proposition
2.9
estimating as

\lvert\partial_{x}^{\eta}(\chi_{R}V)\rvert\leq C_{8}|tx|^{-m(|\eta|)}.

In parallel to the estimation of
\partial_{\lambda}s
this yields the factor

|x|^{-m(|\eta|)}
as well as the factor

t^{-m(|\eta|)}=t^{-\sigma}t^{\sigma-m(|\eta|)}
. Choosing a small
enough
p>1
the familiar generalized Hölder
inequality yields a bound in terms for the
L^{1}
-norm of
t^{-\sigma p}
and (after a redistribution of powers of
t^{-1}
as in Step VI)
products of various
X^{p_{j}}
-norms for which Proposition
2.9
applies. We omit the book-keeping details.

Hence we have proven (
1.7
) for all
k+|\alpha|\leq l
.
∎

In Sections
4
and
5

we will actually use the following modification of (
1.7
).

Corollary 2.14
.

For any closed interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
let
s_{R}\in C^{l}(I\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
be given
as in Theorem
1.3
for
R\geq R_{0}
, where
R_{0}>0
is
taken sufficiently large.
Then there exists
C>0
such that
for any
R\geq R_{0}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma]
,
k+|\alpha|\leq l
and
(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial^{k}_{\lambda}\partial^{\alpha}_{x}s(\lambda,x)\bigr|\leq CR^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}\lambda^{-1-k}\langle x\rangle^{-m^{\prime}(k+|\alpha|)+k}.

where
m^{\prime}(k)
is given in Proposition
2.9
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from the arguments of the subsection
with all the estimates involving

|\partial^{\alpha}\chi_{R}V|\leq C_{1}\langle x\rangle^{-m(|\alpha|)}

replaced by

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}\chi_{R}V|\leq C_{2}R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}\langle x\rangle^{-m^{\prime}(|\alpha|)}.

It is straightforward, and we omit repeating the arguments.
∎

3.
Strong radiation condition bounds

In this section we prove Theorem
1.23
.
Our main tool is a commutator-type argument.
We adopt a second order differential operator as our conjugate operator,
whereas the standard Mourre theory employs a first order one.
This might appear rather peculiar,
since the resulting operator would be of third order, which usually cannot have a sign.
However, we repeatedly use a certain
increment or decrement identity
to make it of even order
with a definite sign.

We first introduce notation of the section in Subsection
3.1
,
and then discuss Classical Mechanics interpretations in Subsection
3.2
.
Motivated by the classical picture,
the main propositions of the section will be presented in Subsection
3.3
.
After some preliminaries in Subsection
3.4
these propositions will be proved in
Subsection
3.5
.
Finally in Subsection
3.6

we will complete the proof of Theorem
1.23
.

3.1.
Notation

Throughout the section we assume Condition
1.1
with
l=4
and
q\equiv 0
.
Let
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a closed interval,
and let
S\in C^{4}(I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}))
be the function
from
Theorem
1.3
given for
R\geq R_{0}>0
.

According to Theorem
1.3
,
S(\lambda,\cdot)
is
a geodesic distance from the origin.
It is convenient to normalize this function and
consider
\Phi(\lambda,\cdot)=S(\lambda,\cdot)/\sqrt{2\lambda}
,
exactly as done in
Section
2
, but even then the singularity
at the origin might cause problems. Consequently we regularize
\Phi(\lambda,\cdot)

as follows.

Lemma 3.1
.

For all
R\geq R_{0}

there exists
f\in C^{4}(I\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that (with the dependence on
\lambda\in I
and
R\geq R_{0}

being suppressed):

(3.1)

\text{F}or\,\,any\,\,\lambda\in I\text{ and }|x|>R:\quad f(x)=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}S(\lambda,x).

Moreover (with all constants below
being independent of
\lambda
and
R
):

(1)

There exist
c,C>0
such that for any
(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.2a)

c\langle x\rangle\leq f(x)\leq C\langle x\rangle;

(2)

There exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that
for any
|\alpha|\leq 4
,
(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.2b)

\displaystyle\left|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}f(x)^{1-\min\{|\alpha|,m(|\alpha|)\}}.

(3)

There exists
C^{\prime\prime}>0

such that for any
(\lambda,x)\in I\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

(3.2c)

\displaystyle\bigl(1-C^{\prime\prime}f^{-\sigma}\bigr)I_{d}\leq(\nabla f)\otimes(\nabla f)+f(\nabla^{2}f)\leq\bigl(1+C^{\prime\prime}f^{-\sigma}\bigr)I_{d}.

Proof.

Such modification is clearly possible due to Theorem
1.3
.
We omit the details.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

In Section
4
we will use the
slight modification of (
3.2c
) given with
C^{\prime\prime}f^{-\sigma}

replaced by
C^{\prime\prime\prime}R^{-\sigma^{\prime}+\sigma}f^{-\sigma^{\prime}}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma)
.

3.2.
Classical Mechanics

Here we present a stationary bound holding along
a classical scattering orbit in the phase space
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
The arguments of this subsection are not necessary for our purpose,
but they serve as important motivation for our proof of
Theorem
1.23
.
Moreover the proof here will be directly ‘lifted’ to Quantum Mechanics,
apart from the fact that quantum observables of course do not generally commute.

3.2.1.
Free Hamiltonian

Let us start with the trivial case with
V\equiv 0
for simplicity.
Hence we consider the free classical Hamiltonian

H^{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}(x,\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\xi^{2}\ \ \text{for }(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},

and the associated Hamilton equations

\dot{x}=\xi,\quad\dot{\xi}=0.

Then for any initial data
(y,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}

we have an explicit classical orbit

\displaystyle x(t)=\eta t+y,\quad\xi(t)=\eta.

Assuming the orbit has a positive energy

\displaystyle\lambda=H^{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}(x(t),\xi(t))=\tfrac{1}{2}\eta^{2}>0,

which is fixed, we discuss the asymptotic relation between observables along the orbit.
Obviously it follows that (forward in time)

(3.3)

\displaystyle\xi=\sqrt{2\lambda}|x|^{-1}x+\mathcal{O}(t^{-1})\ \ \text{as }t\to\infty,

and hence the momentum
\xi
is comparable to
\sqrt{2\lambda}|x|^{-1}x
.
However we would like to express it in a stationary manner without time parameter.
For that purpose note

\displaystyle|x|=\sqrt{2\lambda}t+\mathcal{O}(1)\ \ \text{as }t\to\infty.

This implies that the quantity
|x|
is an ‘effective time’ up to a constant factor,
allowing us to replace the time parameter.
Now let us introduce
the ‘classical gamma observables’ as

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}&=\xi-(\nabla S_{0}),\quad\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\|}=(\nabla S_{0})\cdot\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}};\quad S_{0}=\sqrt{2\lambda}|x|,\end{split}

cf. (
1.21
).
Then we obtain a stationary expression of (
3.3
) as

(3.5)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}=\mathcal{O}(|x|^{-1}).

Furthermore it follows that

(3.6)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\|}=H^{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}-\lambda-\tfrac{1}{2}(\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}})^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(|x|^{-2}).

Note that the bound
\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\|}=\mathcal{O}(|x|^{-2})

is sharper than the bound resulting from substituting
(
3.5
) into the middle expression of (
3.4
) .
This is our starting point.

3.2.2.
Perturbed Hamiltonian

Next we turn to the general case.
We discuss the perturbed classical Hamiltonian

(3.7)

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{cl}}(x,\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\xi^{2}+\chi_{R}(x)V(x)\ \ \text{for }(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},

cf. (
1.6
). The associated Hamilton equations are given as

(3.8)

\displaystyle\dot{x}=\xi,\quad\dot{\xi}=-\nabla(\chi_{R}V).

We are interested in the asymptotic stationary estimates along a
forward scattering orbit
(x(t),\xi(t))
(meaning beyond
(
3.8
) that
|x(t)|\to\infty
for
t\to\infty
)
with a fixed positive energy

\displaystyle\lambda=H^{\mathrm{cl}}(x(t),\xi(t))>0.

Parallel to the free case we can study details of propagation along
this orbit in
terms of
the classical gamma observables given as

(3.9)

\displaystyle\gamma=\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}=\xi-(\nabla S)\,\text{ and }\,\gamma_{\|}=\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\|}=(\nabla S)\cdot\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}},

where as in (
1.21
) the function
S
comes from Theorem
1.3
.
Until the end of the subsection we drop the superscript
cl
for short.

Proposition 3.3
.

Fix any
\lambda>0
, and define
\gamma
and
\gamma_{\|}
as above.
Let
(x(t),\xi(t))
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, be a classical orbit
for the Hamiltonian (
3.7
) with energy
\lambda
such that

|x(t)|\to\infty\ \text{ as }t\to+\infty.

Then for any
\beta\in(0,2)
there exists
C>0
such that for
t\geq 0
and along the
orbit
(x(t),\xi(t))

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\leq Cf^{-\beta}\,\text{ and }\,|\gamma_{\|}|\leq Cf^{-\beta}.

Remark 3.4
.

This is the classical counterpart of Theorem
1.23

intuitively explaining why the operator
\gamma_{\|}
accepts a doubled weight compared to the
operators
\gamma_{i}
.
See also
[
HS
]
.
On the other hand,
the accepted range of the exponent
\beta
in
Theorem
1.23
is in general smaller than
in Proposition
3.3
,
because higher order derivatives of
S
are involved in the proof for
the quantum
mechanical case.

Proof.

All the quantities below are considered along the forward scattering
orbit
(x(t),\xi(t))
,
and the dependence on
t\geq 0
is suppressed.
It suffices to argue only for large
t\geq 0
,
so that we may consider
x
to stay away from the origin, in fact
quantitatively as

|x(t)|>2R
.
Hence by (
1.6
) we have the identity for large
t

(3.10)

\displaystyle 0=H^{\mathrm{cl}}-\lambda=\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}+\gamma_{\|}.

This identity is important (it will be used repeatedly) and deserves to be named the
classical increment or decrement identity
.
By (
3.10
) it suffices to show the bound

(3.11)

\displaystyle P^{\mathrm{cl}}:=(f^{\beta}\gamma_{\|})^{2}\leq C_{1},

and for that we compute the time-derivative of
P^{\mathrm{cl}}
.
However the time-derivative coincides with the Poisson bracket, hence

D:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\{H^{\mathrm{cl}},\cdot\}
. In any
case we easily compute

(3.12)

DP^{\mathrm{cl}}=2\beta f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}(Df)+2f^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\bigl(D\gamma_{\|}\bigr),

motivating us to show that the right-hand side
eventually is negative along the orbit.

We compute and bound the first term on the right-hand side
by using (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
1.6
) as

\displaystyle 2\beta f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}(Df)

\displaystyle=2\beta f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}(\nabla f)\cdot\xi

\displaystyle=2\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}+|\nabla S|^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=2\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}\bigl(-\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}+2\lambda-2\chi_{R}V\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq-\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}\gamma^{2}+2\beta(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}+C_{2}f^{2\beta-1-\sigma}\gamma_{\|}^{2}.

To compute the second term of (
3.12
) we note the identity obtained
by differentiating the equation (
1.6
)

(3.13)

\displaystyle(\nabla^{2}S)\nabla S=\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla|\nabla S|^{2}=-\nabla(\chi_{R}V).

Then by (
3.9
), (
3.13
) and (
3.10
)
this second term
is computed as

\displaystyle 2f^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\bigl(D\gamma_{\|}\bigr)

\displaystyle=2f^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\bigl(\xi\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma+(\nabla S)\cdot\bigl(-(\nabla\chi_{R}V)-(\nabla^{2}S)\xi\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle=2f^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\bigl(\gamma\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma-(\nabla S)\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)(\nabla S)-(\nabla S)\cdot(\nabla\chi_{R}V)\bigr)

\displaystyle=-(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{2\beta}\gamma^{2}\gamma\cdot(\nabla^{2}f)\gamma.

Combining the above computations and using (
3.2c
) and (
3.10
),
we bound (
3.12
) as

\displaystyle DP^{\mathrm{cl}}

\displaystyle\leq-(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma^{2}\gamma\cdot\bigl(\beta(\nabla f)\otimes(\nabla f)+(f\nabla^{2}f)\bigr)\gamma

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\beta(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}+C_{2}f^{2\beta-1-\sigma}\gamma_{\|}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-(\min\{1,\beta\})(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma^{4}\bigl(1-C_{3}f^{-\sigma}\bigr)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\beta(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}+C_{2}f^{2\beta-1-\sigma}\gamma_{\|}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-(\min\{4-2\beta,2\beta\})(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}^{2}+C_{4}f^{2\beta-1-\sigma}\gamma_{\|}^{2},

so that for any sufficiently large
t

(3.14)

\displaystyle DP^{\mathrm{cl}}

\displaystyle\leq-c_{1}f^{-1}P^{\mathrm{cl}}\leq 0.

Hence indeed
P^{\mathrm{cl}}
is bounded as
t\to\infty
,
verifying (
3.11
), and we are done.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

We have presented a stationary proof without explicit time parameter,
so that the scheme extends to the quantum setup, see Proposition
3.6

and its proof.
We could have given a simpler proof computing
D(f^{\beta}\gamma_{\|})
,
however we are not aware of any analogous procedure in Quantum
Mechanics. Note that in the literature on scattering theory of
Schrödinger operators conjugate
operators are usually of first order, and consequently our scheme
of proof (not being of this sort)
is rather non-conventional.

3.3.
Main propositions of the section

Clearly we can assume that the arbitrary compact subset

I^{\prime}\subseteq I
in Theorem
1.23
is
taken as
I^{\prime}=I
, and hence that also
I
is compact. We make these
assumption in the remaining part of the section.

To prove Theorem
1.23

we are going to compute and bound a quantum observable corresponding
to the expression

D((f^{\beta}\gamma^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\|})^{2})

appearing in the proof of Proposition
3.3
.
As a quantum observable corresponding to (
3.11
),
we consider

(3.15)

\displaystyle P=\gamma_{\|}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|},\quad\beta\in(0,2).

Here the weight
\theta
is defined as

\displaystyle\theta=\theta_{\delta,\nu}(f)=\int_{0}^{f}(1+t/\nu)^{-1-\delta}\,\mathrm{d}t;\quad\nu\geq 1,\ \ {\delta>0}.

It is a refinement of the so-called
Yosida approximation
of
f
.
Note that
\theta
is bounded for each
\nu\geq 1
, but that

\theta^{\prime}\uparrow 1\text{ \ and\ \ }\theta\uparrow f\ \ \text{pointwise as }\nu\to\infty,

where
\theta^{\prime}
denotes the derivative of
\theta
as a function of
f
.

The main propositions of the section present upper and lower bounds of a ‘distorted commutator’

\displaystyle 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))

with

\displaystyle z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}:=\bigl\{z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,\lambda\in I,\ \Gamma\in(0,1)\bigr\}.

Note that it is comparable with

DP^{\mathrm{cl}}=\{H^{\mathrm{cl}},P^{\mathrm{cl}}\}
considered before in Classical Mechanics.
In fact, if
z=\lambda\in I
, it is nothing but the commutator
\mathrm{i}[H,P]
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Define
P
and
\theta
as above
with
\beta\in(0,2)
and
\delta>0
arbitrarily fixed.
Then there
exist
c>0
and
\nu_{0}\geq 1
such that for all

z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}
,
R\geq R_{0}

and
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
(with the constant
C>0
being independent of

\lambda\in I
but possibly depending on
R\geq R_{0}
),

\displaystyle\pm 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))

\displaystyle\leq-c\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+Cf^{-1-2\beta_{c}+3\delta}\theta^{2\beta}+C(H-z)^{*}f^{1+\delta}\theta^{2\beta-\delta}(H-z)

as quadratic forms on
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Suppose the same setting of Proposition
3.6
, and let

\epsilon\in(0,1]
. Then there exists
\nu_{0}\geq 1
that for all

z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}
,
R\geq R_{0}

and
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
(with the constant
C>0
being independent of

\lambda\in I
but possibly depending on
R\geq R_{0}
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pm 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))&\geq-\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}-C\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}-Cf^{-1-2\beta_{c}+3\delta}\theta^{2\beta}-C(H-z)^{*}f^{1+\delta}\theta^{2\beta-\delta}(H-z)\end{split}

as quadratic forms on
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
.

Remark 3.8
.

Proposition
3.6
is obviously a
quantum analogue of (
3.14
)
with some negligible errors coming from commutation of
observables,
while Proposition
3.7
only says that
the left-hand side of Proposition
3.6
is negligible too.
In the proof of Theorem
1.23

we shall take expectation of these bounds
in the state
\phi=R(z)\psi
, and take the limits
\Gamma\to 0_{+}

and
\nu\to\infty
.
Then the second, third and fourth terms on the right-hand sides
of Propositions
3.6
and
3.7

are in fact negligible for
\beta<\beta_{\rm c}
and

\delta
taken small. This is
due to the factor
\Gamma
,
the limiting absorption principle bounds
and cancellation of
H-z
and
R(z)
, respectively.
We will give the details in Subsection
3.6
.

The propositions will be proved
in Subsection
3.5
,
after some preliminaries in Subsection
3.4
.

3.4.
Preliminaries

Here we gather some identities and estimates
that will be frequently cited in the later subsections.
Throughout the subsection we adapt the setting of Proposition
3.6
.
In particular,

\beta\in(0,2)
and
\delta>0
are fixed
along with
I
,
R
,
S
and
f

from Subsection
3.1
.

We first record several bounds for the weight
\theta
.
We denote the derivatives of
\theta
in
f
by primes or similar superscripts such as

\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime},\dots,\theta^{(k)}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

There exist
c_{0},C_{0}>0
such that uniformly in
\lambda\in I
and
\nu\geq 1

\displaystyle c_{0}\min\{\nu,f\}\leq\theta\leq\min\{C_{0}\nu,f\},\quad\theta^{\prime}\leq f^{-1}\theta.

Furthermore, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}

there exist
c_{k},C_{k}>0
such that uniformly in
\lambda\in I
and
\nu\geq 1

\displaystyle c_{k}\nu^{1-k}f^{-k-\delta}\theta^{k+\delta}\leq(-1)^{k-1}\theta^{(k)}\leq C_{k}\nu^{1-k}f^{-k-\delta}\theta^{k+\delta}.

Remark 3.10
.

In the rest of the section we will mostly use the simplified bounds

c_{1}f^{-1-\delta}\theta^{1+\delta}\leq\theta^{\prime}\leq f^{-1}\theta,\quad|\theta^{(k)}|\leq C_{k}^{\prime}f^{-k}\theta.

Proof.

According to whether
f\leq\nu
or
f\geq\nu
, we have

\theta\geq\int_{0}^{f}2^{-1-\delta}\,\mathrm{d}s=2^{-1-\delta}f\quad\text{or}\quad\theta\geq\int_{0}^{\nu}2^{-1-\delta}\,\mathrm{d}s=2^{-1-\delta}\nu,

respectively. On the other hand

\displaystyle\theta=\delta^{-1}\nu\bigl(1-(1+f/\nu)^{-\delta}\bigr)\leq\delta^{-1}\nu\,\text{ and }\,\theta\leq\int_{0}^{f}\mathrm{d}s=f.

Hence we obtain the asserted bounds for
\theta
.

We also have

\theta^{\prime}=(1+f/\nu)^{-1-\delta}=f^{-1}\int_{0}^{f}(1+f/\nu)^{-1-\delta}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq f^{-1}\theta.

In addition, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
we can find a constant
C_{k}>0
such that

\displaystyle\theta^{(k)}=(-1)^{k-1}C_{k}\nu^{1-k}(1+f/\nu)^{-k-\delta}=(-1)^{k-1}C_{k}\nu^{1-k}f^{-k-\delta}(f^{-1}+\nu^{-1})^{-k-\delta}.

Here the last factor satisfies

\tfrac{1}{2}\min\{\nu,f\}\leq(f^{-1}+\nu^{-1})^{-1}\leq\min\{\nu,f\}.

Hence we are done.
∎

We next present a simple key identity corresponding to (
3.10
).
It involves gamma observables of different orders,
and hence it can be used either to increment or decrement the order
of a differential operator (it will be used both ways many times).

Lemma 3.11
.

For any
z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}
on the subset
\{|x|>2R\}
the identities

(3.16)

H-z=\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\pm\gamma_{\|}\mp\mathrm{i}\Gamma

hold, respectively.

Proof.

The assertion is straightforward by (
1.6
) and (
1.21
).
(Recall that throughout the section,
q\equiv 0
in (
1.1
).)
∎

The following commutator relations of the gamma observables are also important.

Lemma 3.12
.

For any
\lambda\in I
and
i,j=1,\dots,d

one has on
\{|x|\geq 2\}

(3.17a)

\displaystyle[\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j}]

\displaystyle=0,

(3.17b)

\displaystyle[\gamma_{\|},\gamma_{i}]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}((\nabla\partial_{i}S)\cdot\gamma),

(3.17c)

\displaystyle[\gamma_{\|},\gamma^{2}]

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\gamma\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(\Delta^{2}S).

Proof.

By conjugation by
\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}S}
the relation (
3.17a
) reduces to that for

p
,
which is trivial.
We can verify (
3.17b
) immediately by (
1.21
) and (
3.17a
).
As for (
3.17c
) we use (
3.17b
) to compute

\displaystyle[\gamma_{\|},\gamma^{2}]

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathrm{i}\gamma\cdot(\nabla\partial_{i}S)-\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{i}\Delta S)\bigr)\gamma_{i}+\gamma_{i}\bigl(\mathrm{i}(\nabla\partial_{i}S)\cdot\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{i}\Delta S)\bigr)

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\gamma\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(\Delta^{2}S).

Thus we obtain the assertion.
∎

Lastly in this subsection we present several handy ’ellipticity estimates’.
We will often use the following identities holding
for any
a\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
:

(3.18a)

\displaystyle p\cdot ap

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(ap^{2})+\tfrac{1}{2}(\Delta a),

(3.18b)

\displaystyle p\cdot(p\cdot ap)p

\displaystyle=p^{2}ap^{2}-p\cdot(\nabla^{2}a)p+p\cdot(\Delta a)p.

The lemma below implies that
any compactly supported differential operator of order at most four is
bounded by the last two terms of Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
.
For short we shall denote their sum as

(3.19)

\displaystyle Q=f^{-1-2\beta_{c}+3\delta}\theta^{2\beta}+(H-z)^{*}f^{1+\delta}\theta^{2\beta-\delta}(H-z).

Lemma 3.13
.

For any
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})

there exists
C>0
such that
uniformly in
z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}
,

R\geq R_{0}
and
\nu\geq 1

(3.20)

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\leq CQ,\quad\gamma\cdot\eta\gamma\leq CQ,\quad\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot\eta\gamma)\gamma\leq CQ.

Proof.

The first bound from (
3.20
) is
trivial. For the second and
third bounds it suffices to show the bounds with

\gamma
replaced by
p
.
As for the second, we use (
3.18a
) and
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to bound it for any
N\geq 0
as

\displaystyle p\cdot\eta p

\displaystyle=2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\eta(H-z))-2\eta(V-z)+\tfrac{1}{2}(\Delta\eta)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}f^{-N}+C_{1}(H-z)^{*}f(H-z).

The second bound follows by choosing
N=5
.
Lastly we rewrite the left-hand side of the third bound of
(
3.20
) by using (
3.18b
) as

\displaystyle p\cdot(p\cdot\eta p)p

\displaystyle=4(H-z)^{*}\eta(H-z)-8\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\eta(V-z^{*})(H-z)\bigr)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+4\eta|V-z|^{2}-p\cdot(\nabla^{2}\eta)p+p\cdot(\Delta\eta)p.

The third bound then follows by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
and the first and second bounds.
∎

The next lemma implies various forms of negligible terms
can be absorbed into the leading term

\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}
,
or
\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma)\gamma
,
cf. Lemma
3.15
.

Lemma 3.14
.

For any
\epsilon>0

and
R\geq R_{0}
there exist
C=C(\delta)>0
and
\nu_{0}=\nu_{0}(\delta)\geq 1
, such that
for all
z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}
and
\nu\geq\nu_{0}

(3.21a)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\|}f^{-1-\delta}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+CQ,

(3.21b)

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot f^{-1-\beta_{c}+\delta}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+CQ.

(3.21c)

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot f^{-1-\delta}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma)\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma)\gamma+CQ,

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
By Lemma
3.9

we can find
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
, such
that uniformly in
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
for some
\nu_{0}\geq 1
chosen sufficiently large

\displaystyle f^{-1-\delta}\theta^{2\beta}\leq\epsilon\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}+\eta.

Then we conclude (
3.21a
) and
(
3.21c
) by Lemma
3.13
.
To prove (
3.21b
)
note by conjugation by
{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}\chi_{1}S}
we can rewrite (
3.18a
) as

(3.22)

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot a\gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(a\gamma^{2})+\tfrac{1}{2}(\Delta a).

In addition, set

\displaystyle t=1+\beta_{c}-\delta.

Then by (
3.22
), (
1.5
), Lemmas
3.11
and
3.9
, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Lemma
3.13

we can estimate (using the cut-off function
\chi_{2R}
from (
1.5
))

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot f^{-t}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\mp 2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\chi_{2R}f^{-t}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|})+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\chi_{2R}f^{-t}\theta^{2\beta}(H-z))

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{1}f^{-t-2}\theta^{2\beta}+\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl((1-\chi_{2R})f^{-t}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{\|}f^{-1-\delta}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}+{C_{1}}f^{-t-2}\theta^{2\beta}+C_{2}f^{-2t+1+\delta}\theta^{2\beta}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{2}(H-z)^{*}f^{-1-\delta}\theta^{2\beta}(H-z)+C_{2}Q.

Applying in turn (
3.21a
), this yields (
3.21b
).
∎

The final lemma says
4\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}
and

\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma\bigr)\gamma

are interchangeable up to small errors.

Lemma 3.15
.

For any
\epsilon>0
and
R\geq R_{0}
there exist
C>0
and
\nu_{0}\geq 1
such that
for all
z=\lambda+\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{+}
and
\nu\geq\nu_{0}

\displaystyle\pm\bigl\{4\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}-\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma\bigr)\gamma\bigr\}\leq\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}+CQ.

The same bounds also hold uniformly in
z=\lambda-\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{-}
and
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
.

Proof.

We discuss only
z=\lambda+\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{+}

since the other is proved in the same manner.
Similarly to (
3.22
), by conjugation by
{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\chi_{1}S}

we have (
3.18b
) rewritten as

(3.23)

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot a\gamma)\gamma=\gamma^{2}a\gamma^{2}-\gamma\cdot(\nabla^{2}a)\gamma+\gamma\cdot(\Delta a)\gamma.

Then it follows by Lemma
3.9
that

(3.24)

\displaystyle\pm\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma\bigr)\gamma

\displaystyle\leq\pm\gamma^{2}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma^{2}+C_{1}\gamma\cdot f^{-3}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma.

The second term on the right-hand side of (
3.24
)
can be bounded by (
3.21b
),
and hence it suffices to discuss the first term of (
3.24
).
With the localization factor
\chi_{2R}
inserted in
this term we are allowed to isolate
\gamma^{2}
in
(
3.16
) and substitute into the
two appearing factors of
\gamma^{2}
. After
expansion
we then use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to absorb cross terms into the diagonal ones.
Hence it follows that

\displaystyle\pm\gamma^{2}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma^{2}

\displaystyle=\pm 4\bigl(-\gamma_{\|}+\mathrm{i}\Gamma+(H-z)\bigr)^{*}\chi_{2R}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\bigl(-\gamma_{\|}+\mathrm{i}\Gamma+(H-z)\bigr)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\pm\gamma^{2}(1-\chi_{2R})\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\pm 4+\epsilon)\gamma_{\|}\chi_{2R}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C_{2}\Gamma^{2}f^{-1}\theta^{2\beta}+C_{2}Q.

We can remove
\chi_{2R}
from the first term on the right-hand side above
by retaking
C_{2}>0
larger,
and hence it suffices to discuss the second term.
By the expression (
1.21
), the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
and Lemma
3.9
we can proceed as

(3.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma^{2}f^{-1}\theta^{2\beta}&=\tfrac{1}{2}\Gamma\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl((\nabla f^{-1}\theta^{2\beta})\cdot\gamma\bigr)+\tfrac{1}{2}\Gamma(\nabla f^{-1}\theta^{2\beta})\cdot(\nabla S)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}-\Gamma\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(f^{-1}\theta^{2\beta}(H-z)\bigr)\\
&\leq C_{3}\gamma_{\|}f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}+C_{3}\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}+C_{3}Q.\end{split}

The first term on the right-hand side of (
3.25
)
can be bounded as asserted by using (
3.21a
).
Hence we obtain the assertion.
∎

3.5.
Upper and lower bounds for the distorted commutator

Here we prove Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
.
We start with Proposition
3.6
.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Step I.

Let us prove the assertion only for the upper sign, since the lower one follows
in the same manner.
With reference to the constants
0<c\leq C
of (
3.2a
) and
the function
\chi
of (
1.4
) we
introduce the smooth cut-off function

\widetilde{\chi}=\chi(f/4CR)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\widetilde{\chi}\subseteq\{|x|>2R\},\quad\widetilde{\chi}=1\ \ \text{on }\{|x|\geq 8RC/c\}.

In particular (
3.16
) applies on the support of

\widetilde{\chi}
. Hence by (
3.15
) and Lemma
3.11

we can split the distorted commutator on the left-hand side of the assertion as

(3.26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))&=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\gamma^{2}\bigr)+2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}^{2}\bigr)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}(1-\widetilde{\chi})\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}(H-\lambda)\bigr)-2\Gamma\gamma_{\|}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}.\end{split}

In the following steps
we will further compute and bound each term of (
3.26
).

First we comment on our notation.
Throughout the proof we fix
\epsilon>0
such that
for some
c_{1}>0
, it follows that uniformly in
\lambda\in I

(3.27)

\displaystyle(2\lambda)^{1/2}\min\{4-2\beta,2\beta\}-19\epsilon>c_{1}>0.

We shall consider only
\nu\geq\nu_{0}
with
appropriate
\nu_{0}\geq 1
tacitly retaken
each time we apply Lemmas
3.14
or
3.15
.
In addition, we shall adopt the notation
Q
from (
3.19
).
We particularly note that, when computing (
3.26
),
once a derivative hits
\widetilde{\chi}
,
the corresponding term is immediately bounded by
C_{1}Q
for some
C_{1}>0

due to Lemma
3.13
.
We shall also tacitly implement such estimates.

Step II.

Now we start with the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.26
).
By (
1.21
) and (
3.17c
) we can compute it as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\gamma^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}[\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta},\gamma]\cdot\gamma\gamma_{\|}\bigr)+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}[\gamma_{\|},\gamma^{2}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}(\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta})^{\prime}\gamma_{\|}^{2}\bigr)+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\Delta^{2}S)\bigr).

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, (
3.13
),
(
3.2b
), and Lemmas
3.13
and
3.14
,
we can bound it as

(3.28a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\gamma^{2}\bigr)&\leq 2\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}^{2}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)+\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C_{2}Q.\end{split}

The second term of (
3.26
) can be computed by (
1.21
), (
1.6
) and Lemma
3.14
as

(3.28b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}^{2}\bigr)&=\gamma_{\|}(\nabla S)\cdot\bigl(\nabla\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\bigr)\gamma_{\|}\\
&\leq\bigl(2\beta(2\lambda)^{1/2}+\epsilon\bigr)\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C_{3}Q.\end{split}

The third and fourth terms of (
3.26
) is bounded trivially as

(3.28c)

\displaystyle 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}(1-\widetilde{\chi})\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}(H-\lambda)\bigr)-2\Gamma\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\leq C_{4}Q.

Hence by (
3.26
), and (
3.28a
)–(
3.28c
) we obtain

(3.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))&\leq 2\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}^{2}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+\bigl(2\beta(2\lambda)^{1/2}+2\epsilon\bigr)\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C_{5}Q.\end{split}

Step III.

We continue to compute (
3.29
).
We next increment the order of the first and second terms of (
3.29
)
by substituting the following version of (
3.16
),

(3.30)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\|}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}-\mathrm{i}\Gamma+(H-z)^{*},

In fact, using (
3.30
) we can rewrite the first and second terms of (
3.29
)
as

(3.31)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&2\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}^{2}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)\\
&=-\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma^{2}\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma^{2}\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl((H-z)^{*}\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl((H-z)^{*}\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr).\end{split}

Let us discuss each term on the right-hand side.
The first term of (
3.31
) can be bounded
by using (
3.17b
), the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Lemma
3.14
as

(3.32a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma^{2}\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)\\
&=-\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\Bigl\{\gamma\cdot\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\gamma+\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma\cdot[\gamma,\gamma_{\|}]\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma\cdot\gamma_{\|}[\gamma,\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}]\gamma_{\|}\bigr)+\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma\cdot\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}[\gamma,\gamma_{\|}]\bigr)\Bigr\}\\
&\leq-\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\gamma\cdot\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\gamma+\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+\epsilon\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma)\gamma+C_{6}Q.\end{split}

The second term of (
3.31
) can be bounded by using
(
3.2b
) and Lemma
3.14
as

(3.32b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\gamma^{2}\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)&=-\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma\cdot\bigl(\Delta\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\bigr)\gamma\\
&\leq-\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)\gamma+\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C_{7}Q.\end{split}

As for the third and fourth terms of (
3.31
),
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (
3.2b
) and Lemma
3.14
we have

(3.32c)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&2\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl((H-z)^{*}\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)+2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl((H-z)^{*}\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)\\
&\leq C_{8}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)f^{1-\delta}\theta^{-2\beta}\bigl(\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\bigr)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{8}\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)f^{1-\delta}\theta^{-2\beta}\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)+C_{8}Q\\
&\leq\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+\epsilon\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma)\gamma+C_{9}Q.\end{split}

Hence by (
3.29
), (
3.31
) and (
3.32a
)–(
3.32c
)
we conclude that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))&\leq-\beta(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\gamma\cdot\gamma_{\|}\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\gamma-\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\widetilde{\chi}\theta^{2\beta}(\nabla^{2}S)\gamma\bigr)\gamma\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\epsilon\gamma\cdot(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma)\gamma+\bigl(2\beta(2\lambda)^{1/2}+5\epsilon\bigr)\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{10}Q.\end{split}

Step IV.

Now we use (
3.2c
) to the right-hand side of (
3.33
).
Also using Lemmas
3.9
and
3.14
,
we obtain

(3.34)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))&\leq-\bigl((2\lambda)^{1/2}\min\{1,\beta\}-3\epsilon\bigr)\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma\bigr)\gamma\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+\bigl(2\beta(2\lambda)^{1/2}+6\epsilon\bigr)\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}+C_{11}Q.\end{split}

Next we rewrite the first term of (
3.34
) by using Lemma
3.15
,
so that

\displaystyle 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))

\displaystyle\leq-\bigl((2\lambda)^{1/2}\min\{4-2\beta,2\beta\}-19\epsilon\bigr)\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{12}\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}+C_{12}Q.

Hence by (
3.27
) we obtain the assertion.
∎

Next we prove Proposition
3.7
.
Compared to Proposition
3.6
,
it is much simpler.

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.

Let us discuss only the upper sign.
Similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.6
,
we adopt
Q
from (
3.19
).
By the definition (
3.15
),
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, Lemma
3.9
,
(
1.21
) and Lemma
3.14

we can bound for any
\epsilon\in(0,1]

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))

\displaystyle\geq-\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{-2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}\theta^{2\beta}\gamma_{\|}-C_{1}\epsilon^{-1}Q

\displaystyle\geq-C_{2}\epsilon\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma\bigr)\gamma-C_{2}\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}-C_{2}\epsilon^{-1}Q,

where
C_{*}>0
are independent of
\epsilon\in(0,1]
.
Then by Lemma
3.15

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(P(H-z))

\displaystyle\geq-C_{3}\epsilon\gamma_{\|}\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}-C_{3}\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}-C_{3}\epsilon^{-1}Q,

and we are done.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

We can modify the arguments of the subsection to be applicable to the case
l=3
,
avoiding fourth derivatives of
S
.
For that we should employ

(3.35)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{\|}=(\nabla S)\cdot\gamma-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}(d-1)}{2}(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{-1},\quad\widetilde{\beta}_{c}=1+\sigma

instead of
\gamma_{\|}
,
\beta_{c}
, respectively.
Note, although
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\|}
is not symmetric, it well approximates

\gamma_{\|}
thanks to Theorem
1.3
,
and we can avoid
\Delta^{2}S
coming from (
3.17c
).
The fourth order derivatives of
S
appear also from other parts of the
above arguments,
but we can manage them by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.
We omit the details.
Note also that, although
\widetilde{\beta}_{c}
is worse than
\beta_{c}
,
it is still greater than
1
, and the associated radiation condition bounds
are stronger than the ordinary ones.

3.6.
Proof of strong radiation condition bounds

Finally in this section we prove Theorem
1.23
.
We will use the standard limiting absorption principle bounds on the following form.

Theorem 3.17
.

There exists
C>0
such that
uniformly in
z\in I_{\pm}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\|R(z)\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}^{*}}\leq C\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}},\quad\|\Delta R(z)\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}^{*}}\leq C\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Moreover, for any
t>1/2
there exist uniform limits
in
\lambda\in I
:

R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{I_{\pm}\ni z\to\lambda}R(z),\quad\Delta R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{I_{\pm}\ni z\to\lambda}\Delta R(z)

in the norm topology of
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{t},L^{2}_{-t})
.

Remark 3.18
.

We do not need to assume
q\equiv 0
for this result.

Proof.

This is the standard result in the theory of the Schrödinger operators,
and we omit a proof. We refer the reader to
[
AIIS2
]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.23
.

We note by the density argument
it suffices to prove the asserted bounds
(
1.23a
)–(
1.23c
) for
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(1)
Let
\beta\in(0,\beta_{c})
, and choose
\delta>0
such that

(3.36)

\displaystyle 2\beta+3\delta<2\beta_{c},\quad\delta\leq 2\beta.

By Propositions
3.6
and
3.7

we can find
C_{1}>0
and
\nu_{0}\geq 1
such that
uniformly in
z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}
and
\nu\geq\nu_{0}

(3.37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma_{\|}\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma_{\|}&\leq C_{1}\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}+C_{1}f^{-1-2\beta_{c}+3\delta}\theta^{2\beta}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{1}(H-z)^{*}f^{1+\delta}\theta^{2\beta-\delta}(H-z).\end{split}

Take the expectation of the above inequality in the state
\phi=R(z)\psi
for any
z=\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\Gamma\in I_{\pm}
and

\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and we obtain by (
3.36
) and
Theorem
3.17

\displaystyle\|\theta^{\prime 1/2}\theta^{\beta-1/2}\gamma_{\|}\phi\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\Gamma\|f^{-1}\theta^{\beta}\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C_{1}\|f^{-1/2-\beta_{c}+3\delta/2}\theta^{\beta}\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{1}\|f^{(1+\delta)/2}\theta^{\beta-\delta/2}\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\Gamma\|f^{-1}\theta^{\beta}\phi\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C_{2}\|f^{\beta}\psi\|_{L^{2}_{1/2}}^{2}.

Next we take the limit
\Gamma\to 0_{+}
, and obtain by Theorem
3.17

\displaystyle\|\theta^{\prime 1/2}\theta^{\beta-1/2}\gamma_{\|}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{2}\|f^{\beta}\psi\|_{L^{2}_{1/2}}.

Finally we let
\nu\to\infty
,
and then by the monotone convergence theorem the bound (
1.23a
) follows.

Combining Lemma
3.15
and (
3.37
), we also have

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot\bigl(\gamma\cdot\theta^{\prime}\theta^{2\beta-1}\gamma\bigr)\gamma

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\Gamma f^{-2}\theta^{2\beta}+C_{3}f^{-1-2\beta_{c}+3\delta}\theta^{2\beta}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+C_{3}(H-z)^{*}f^{1+\delta}\theta^{2\beta-\delta}(H-z).

Hence we can verify (
1.23b
) similarly to (
1.23a
).

(2)
Let
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and consider a quantity

F(\zeta)=\bigl\langle\psi^{\prime},(\gamma\cdot f^{2\beta^{\prime}}\gamma)^{\zeta}f^{-t}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)f^{-t}f^{-2\beta^{\prime}\zeta}\psi\bigr\rangle.

It is obviously analytic in
0<\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\zeta<1
.
For
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\zeta=0
we have by Theorem
3.17

|F(\zeta)|\leq C_{4}\|\psi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}},

and for
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\zeta=1
by (
1.23b
), (
1.23a
) and Theorem
3.17

|F(\zeta)|\leq C_{5}\|\psi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

Hence we obtain by the Hadamard three-lines theorem

\bigl|\bigl\langle\psi^{\prime},(\gamma\cdot f^{2\beta^{\prime}}\gamma)^{1/2}f^{-t}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)f^{-t}f^{-\beta^{\prime}}\psi\bigr\rangle\bigr|=|F(\tfrac{1}{2})|\leq C_{6}\|\psi^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{H}},

or for any
\psi^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\|(\gamma\cdot f^{2\beta^{\prime}}\gamma)^{1/2}f^{-t}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{6}\|f^{\beta^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{t}}.

However, we can rewrite the square of the left-hand side
by using the inner product and Theorem
3.17
as

\displaystyle\|(\gamma\cdot f^{2\beta^{\prime}}\gamma)^{1/2}f^{-t}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|f^{\beta^{\prime}}\gamma_{i}f^{-t}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\|f^{\beta^{\prime}}\gamma_{i}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{-t}}^{2}-C_{7}\|\psi^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathcal{B}}^{2}.

Therefore we obtain (
1.23c
).
∎

4.
Stationary scattering theory

In this section we discuss the stationary scattering theory for
H
,
proving Theorems
1.5
,
1.10
and
1.12
and Corollary
1.8
.
In order to use the strong radiation condition bounds
of Theorem
1.23

we need more regularity for the potential than required in these assertions.
Subsection
4.1
reviews a decomposition
V=V_{\mathrm{S}}+V_{\mathrm{L}}

due to Hörmander
[
Hö2
, Lemma 30.1.1]
,
so that the strong radiation condition bounds are available for
H_{\mathrm{L}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta+V_{\mathrm{L}}
.
Then in Subsection
4.2

we introduce the spherical eikonal coordinates associated with
V_{\mathrm{L}}
, and study its geometry.
In these coordinates we can quickly construct the
stationary wave operators for
H_{\mathrm{L}}
, mimicking the procedure of
[
Sk2
]
.
They are then fused to those for
H
by a change of coordinates and the second resolvent identity.
This is implemented in Subsection
4.3
,
and the proofs of Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
1.8
are done.
The proofs of Theorems
1.10
and
1.12
are rather routine,
and they are presented in Subsections
4.4
and
4.5
,
respectively. Then our stationary scattering theory is completed.

4.1.
Hörmander’s regularization

The asserted Theorem
1.23
requires
four derivatives on the potential
V
, which clearly is not at
disposal
for a
2
-admissible potential. Consequently, to implement the radiation condition
bounds of the theorem
we need first to
regularize
V
. This is done by using the scheme of Hörmander
[
Hö2
, Lemma 30.1.1]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose Condition
1.1
with
l=2
.

(1)

For any
\rho\in(0,\sigma)
there exists a splitting

V=V_{\mathrm{S}}+V_{\mathrm{L}};\quad V_{\mathrm{S}}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}),\ \ V_{\mathrm{L}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}),

satisfying:
There exists
C>0
such that for any
|\alpha|\leq 2
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(4.1a)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}V_{\mathrm{S}}(x)|\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-1-\sigma+\rho-|\alpha|(\rho+1)/2},

and for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}

there exists
C_{\alpha}>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(4.1b)

|\partial^{\alpha}V_{\mathrm{L}}(x)|\leq C_{\alpha}\langle x\rangle^{-m(|\alpha|)},

where
m
is defined by
(
1.2a
) with the parameters
\sigma
and
\rho
(and for
any
l\geq 2
).

(2)

For any
\rho\in(0,\sigma)
and
\epsilon,\delta>0
there exists a splitting

V=V_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}+V_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime};\quad V_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}),\ \ V_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}),

satisfying: For
any
|\alpha|\leq 2
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}V_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}(x)|\leq\epsilon\langle x\rangle^{-1-\sigma+\rho+\delta-|\alpha|(\rho+1)/2},

and for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}

there exists
C_{\alpha}>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(4.2)

|\partial^{\alpha}V_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}(x)|\leq C_{\alpha}\langle x\rangle^{-m(|\alpha|)},

where
m
is given as in (
4.1b
).
The constants
C_{\alpha}
can for
|\alpha|\leq 2
be chosen independently of
\epsilon,\delta>0
.

Remarks 4.2
.

(1)

Ikebe–Isozaki
[
II
]
adopted a decomposition similar to
(
1
) for classical
C^{4}
long-range potentials.
Note that our
\partial^{\alpha}V_{\mathrm{L}}
has worse decay rate than theirs for
|\alpha|\geq 3
.

(2)

The assertion (
2
) will only be employed in Subsection
5.2.2
,
the last part of the paper, for the proof of
Theorem
1.20
(
2
).

Proof.

(
1
)
Although the bounds for
V_{\mathrm{S}}
are slightly better than those in
[
Hö2
, Lemma 30.1.1]
, the same proof works well.
Let us review it since we will use its modification below for the assertion (
2
).
Fix any real
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\{|x|<2\})
with
\eta=1
for
|x|\leq 1
, and set

V_{0}(x)=\eta(x)V(x),\quad V_{n}(x)=\bigl(\eta(2^{-n}x)-\eta(2^{1-n}x)\bigr)V(x)\ \ \text{for }n\in\mathbb{N}.

We also take a real
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi\,\mathrm{d}x=1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x_{j}\chi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0\ \ \text{for }j=1,\dots,d,

and set

\chi_{n}(x)=2^{-dn(1+\rho)/2}\chi(2^{-n(1+\rho)/2}x).

Then we define

V_{\mathrm{L}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\chi_{n}*V_{n},\quad V_{\mathrm{S}}=V-V_{\mathrm{L}},

and they satisfy the asserted bounds. We omit further details.

(2)
For
N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
we consider

V_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}=\sum_{n\geq N_{1}}\chi_{n}*V_{n}+\sum_{n<N_{1}}\chi_{N_{2}}*V_{n},\quad V_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}=V-V_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}.

With a proper adjustment of the parameters (fix first
N_{1}
large and
then suitably large
N_{2}
) indeed
V_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}
and
V_{\mathrm{L}}^{\prime}
satisfy the asserted bounds.
We are done.
∎

The main part of the section is devoted to the analysis of
V_{\mathrm{L}}
from Lemma
4.1
(
1
),
while the effects from
V_{\mathrm{S}}+q
are taken into account only
in the last steps.
Note that the bound (
4.1b
) clearly agrees with Condition
1.1
for any
l\geq 2
,
and thus the results from the previous sections are available for
V_{\mathrm{L}}
.
We denote

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{L}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta+V_{\mathrm{L}},\quad R_{\mathrm{L}}(z)=(H_{\mathrm{L}}-z)^{-1}\ \ \text{for }z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(H_{\mathrm{L}}),

and

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)=\swslim_{z\to\lambda\pm{\rm i}0_{+}}R_{\mathrm{L}}(z)\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{*})\ \ \text{for }\lambda>0.

Throughout the section we fix any closed interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
and let
S_{\mathrm{L}}\in C^{l^{\prime}}(I\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}))

and
s_{\mathrm{L}}\in C^{l^{\prime}}(I\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
be
given as in Theorem
1.3
for
V_{\mathrm{L}}
and
any fixed
l^{\prime}>1+2/\rho
.
We will actually possibly need to take
R>0
larger than needed
for Theorem
1.3
in
Subsections
4.2.3
and
5.1
,
implementing Corollary
2.14
,
but the
R
-dependence is suppressed.

Remark 4.3
.

This specific requirement
l^{\prime}>1+2/\rho
will be needed only in the proof of Lemma
5.2
.
Otherwise it suffices to take
l^{\prime}=4
, so that Theorem
1.23
is available.
Although Theorem
1.3
does not provide bounds for

k+|\alpha|>l^{\prime}
, nevertheless the function
s_{\mathrm{L}}\in C^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and similarly for
S_{\mathrm{L}}
. This is a
consequence of the fact that

V_{\mathrm{L}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the implicit function
theorem, see the proof of Theorem
1.3
(
1
).

4.2.
Spherical eikonal coordinates

4.2.1.
Eikonal flow at fixed energy

In order to define so-called spherical eikonal coordinates,
in which the function
S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)
,
\lambda\in I
, from the last
subsection plays the roll
of
eikonal distance
from the origin,
consider the
eikonal flow

y
satisfying, for any given
(\lambda,\theta)\in I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

(4.3a)

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}y(\lambda,t,\theta)=\big(|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}\big)(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))

with

(4.3b)

\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}y(\lambda,t,\theta)=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}y(\lambda,t,\theta)=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\theta.

Lemma 4.4
.

The solution
y
to (
4.3a
) with (
4.3b
) is smooth in

(\lambda,t,\theta)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
,
and for any
\lambda\in I
it induces a (smooth) diffeomorphism

(4.4)

\displaystyle y(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)\colon{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}.

In addition, for any
(\lambda,t,\theta)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
one has

(4.5)

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))=t.

Remarks 4.5
.

(1)

From a geometric point of view the flow

y(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)
is nothing but the exponential map from
the unit-sphere in the tangent space at the origin in the
space
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
equipped with the metric
g_{L}=2\bigl(\lambda-\chi_{R}V_{L}\bigr)\,{\rm d}x^{2}
.

(2)

The flow
y(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)
constitutes a
family of reparametrized classical orbits of energy
\lambda

for the classical Hamiltonian

H^{\mathrm{cl}}_{\mathrm{L}}(x,\xi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\xi^{2}+\chi_{R}(x)V_{\mathrm{L}}(x).

In fact, if we set

\displaystyle z(\tau)=y(\lambda,t,\theta),\quad\tau=\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,s,\theta))|^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}s,

then by using (
1.6
)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}z=\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}},\quad\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}z=(\nabla^{2}S_{\mathrm{L}})(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})=-\nabla(\chi_{R}V_{\mathrm{L}}),

cf. (
3.8
).
The reparametrizing factor
|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}
in (
4.3a
)
is the proper normalization guaranteeing (
4.5
).
This point of view was taken in the proof of
an analogous statement
[
ACH
, Proposition 2.2]
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

By Theorem
1.3
(
2
)
we conclude that
y
is defined at least on
I\times(0,(2\lambda)^{1/2}R]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
and

(4.6)

y(\lambda,t,\theta)=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}t\theta\ \ \text{for }(\lambda,t,\theta)\in I\times\bigl(0,(2\lambda)^{1/2}R\bigr]\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

On the other hand, by (
4.3a
), (
4.3b
) and (
4.6
)

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}S_{\mathrm{L}}\bigl(\lambda,y\bigl(\lambda,t,\theta\bigr)\bigr)=0.

This implies
y(\lambda,t,\theta)
never hits the origin for

t>(2\lambda)^{1/2}R
, and neither it can
reach infinity in finite time.
Hence the vector field
|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}
is forward complete,
and
y
is globally defined on
I\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
,
satisfying (
4.5
).

Next, we note that
y(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)
is bijective.
In fact, by the uniqueness for the initial-value problem of ODEs the injectivity follows.
To see the surjectivity, starting at any given
point
in
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, we solve the ODE
(
4.3a
) in the backward time-direction.
Then we obtain a
‘crossing’ initial
angle
\theta
from where indeed the forward flow for a proper
time
t
satisfies
y(\lambda,t,\theta)=x
. (Alternatively,
y(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)
is bijective since any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}

can be connected to the origin by a unique geodesic, cf. Theorems
1.3
(
2
)
and
2.6
(
1
).)

Finally we show that
y(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)
is a diffeomorphism.
Note that the maps
y(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)
and
y(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
are
smooth, viewed as a solution to an initial-value problem with
data specified on
the sphere
(2\lambda)^{-1/2}{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\simeq{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
at time
t=1
. Thus
it suffices to check the non-degeneracy of the map.
Take any local coordinates
\theta^{\prime}=(\theta_{2}^{\prime},\dots,\theta_{d}^{\prime})
of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
and let
J^{\prime}
be the Jacobian of (
4.4
)
in these local coordinates.
Now we claim that for any
(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})

(4.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}J^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})=\bigl(\nabla\cdot|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}\bigr)(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))J^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}).

In fact, differentiating the defining expression of the Jacobian, we can write

\partial_{t}J^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\det\mathcal{J}^{(i)},

where
\mathcal{J}^{(i)}
are matrix-valued functions whose components are given by

\mathcal{J}^{(i)}_{jk}=\begin{cases}\partial_{k}y_{j}&\text{for }j\neq i,\\
\partial_{t}\partial_{k}y_{i}&\text{for }j=i\end{cases};\quad j=1,\dots,d,\ \,k=t,\theta^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\theta^{\prime}_{d}.

However, thanks to the flow equation (
4.3a
), we can compute

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{k}y_{i}=\partial_{k}\bigl(|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{i}\bigr)=\bigl(\partial_{l}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{i}\bigr)\partial_{k}y_{l}.

Thus we obtain the claimed identity (
4.7
),
noting that the determinant is alternating and multilinear.
By (
4.6
)
J^{\prime}
is non-vanishing for
t\in(0,(2\lambda)^{1/2}R]
,
and hence with (
4.7
) we can conclude that so it is for
all
t>0
. We are done.
∎

Now the spherical eikonal coordinates are defined as follows.

Definition 4.6
.

The
spherical eikonal coordinates
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
at energy
\lambda\in I

are the entries of the inverse of (
4.4
).
We denote them by
(t,\theta)
, or by
(\lambda,t,\theta)
to clarify the
\lambda
-dependence.
They are also denoted by
(t,\theta^{\prime})
or
(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})
if local coordinates
\theta^{\prime}=(\theta_{2}^{\prime},\dots,\theta_{d}^{\prime})

of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
are specified. We call
t=S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))
and
\theta
(or
\theta^{\prime}
) the
radial and
spherical components
of the spherical eikonal coordinates, respectively.

The Euclidean metric splits into the radial and spherical components in the spherical
eikonal coordinates.
Let us present it as a corollary, although we will not use it in the present paper.
The entries of
\theta^{\prime}
are distinguished by Greek indices always
running over
2,\dots,d
, while the entries of
y
canonically are
distinguished by Roman ones always running over
1,\dots,d
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
(t,\theta^{\prime})
be spherical eikonal coordinates at any
\lambda\in I
. Then

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}y_{i}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))(\partial_{t}y_{i}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=|(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))|^{-2},

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}y_{i}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for }\alpha=2,\dots,d.

In addition, the Euclidean metric takes the form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x^{2}

\displaystyle=|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}t^{2}+g_{\alpha\beta}\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{\beta}.

Proof.

The former formulas follow from (
4.3a
) and (
4.5
),
and the last formula is an immediate consequence of the former ones.
∎

4.2.2.
Volume and surface measures

Let
J\colon I\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,\infty)
be a function
such that the Euclidean volume measure can be expressed in
(\lambda,t,\theta)
as

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x(\lambda,t,\theta)=\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}(\lambda,t,\theta)=J(\lambda,t,\theta)\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}A(\theta),

where
\mathrm{d}A
denotes the standard surface measure on
{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
.
In fact, if we take any local coordinates
\theta^{\prime}=(\theta^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\theta^{\prime}_{d})
of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
and let
J^{\prime}
be the Jacobian from the proof of Lemma
4.4
,
then
J
can be computed through the relation

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm d}A_{\lambda,t}(\theta(\theta^{\prime})):=J(\lambda,t,\theta(\theta^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{d}A(\theta(\theta^{\prime}))=J^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{d}.\end{split}

Note for any
\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(4.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!{\rm d}t\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\phi(y(\lambda,t,\theta))\,{\rm d}A_{\lambda,t}(\theta).

By the co-area formula
[
Ev
, Theorem C.5]
the element

|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\,{\rm d}A_{\lambda,t}(\theta)
is the
Euclidean surface element on the
distorted sphere
\{S_{L}=t\}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

(1)

One has an explicit formula

\displaystyle J(\lambda,t,\theta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\lambda}R^{d-1}\bigl|(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\bigr|^{-2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot{\rm exp}\biggl(\int_{(2\lambda)^{1/2}R}^{t}\bigl(|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\Delta S_{\mathrm{L}}\bigr)(\lambda,y(\lambda,\tau,\theta))\,{\rm d}\tau\biggr).

(2)

There exist the following limits
uniformly in
(\lambda,\theta)\in I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
:

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}t^{-(d-1)}J(\lambda,t,\theta)

\displaystyle=(2\lambda)^{-d/2},

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-(d-1)}J(\lambda,t,\theta)

\displaystyle=:J_{+}(\lambda,\theta)>0.

Remark
.

For similar assertions in a wider
geometric setting, see
[
IS1
]
.

Proof.

(1)
Note that for any
(\lambda,t,\theta)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}J(\lambda,t,\theta)=\bigl(\nabla\cdot|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}\bigr)(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))J(\lambda,t,\theta).

In fact (
4.11
) follows from (
4.7
)
since in any local coordinates of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

J^{\prime}/J
is a function independent of
t
, cf. (
4.9
).
Then, since

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\bigl(|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\Delta S_{\mathrm{L}}-\partial_{t}\ln\bigl(|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{2}\bigr),

it follows from (
4.11
) that
for some
C(\lambda,\theta)\geq 0

\displaystyle J(\lambda,t,\theta)

\displaystyle=C(\lambda,\theta)\bigl|(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\bigr|^{-2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot{\rm exp}\bigg(\int_{(2\lambda)^{1/2}R}^{t}\bigl(|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\Delta S_{\mathrm{L}}\bigr)(\lambda,y(\lambda,\tau,\theta))\,{\rm d}\tau\biggr).

We can determine
C(\lambda,\theta)=\sqrt{2\lambda}R^{d-1}

by (
1.6
) and (
4.6
),
which shows the assertion.

(2)
The assertion is clear for
t\to 0_{+}
thanks to the explicit expression (
4.6
).
Let
t\geq(2\lambda)^{1/2}R
.
Then by (
1.6
), Theorem
1.3

and (
4.5
) it follows that

\displaystyle\bigl||(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))|^{-2}-(2\lambda)^{-1}\bigr|\leq C_{1}t^{-\sigma},

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl(|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\Delta S_{\mathrm{L}}\bigr)(\lambda,y(\lambda,\tau,\theta))-(d-1)\tau^{-1}\bigr|\leq C_{1}\tau^{-1-\sigma}.

This verifies the asymptotics for
t\to\infty
.
∎

4.2.3.
Change of coordinates at infinity

Here we investigate relation between the spherical eikonal coordinates and the ordinary spherical coordinates.
For each
\lambda\in I
we can change from

(t,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

to

(r,\omega)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

through

(4.12)

\displaystyle r(\lambda,t,\theta)=|y(\lambda,t,\theta)|,\quad\omega(\lambda,t,\theta)=\hat{y}(\lambda,t,\theta)=|y(\lambda,t,\theta)|^{-1}y(\lambda,t,\theta).

We claim that (
4.12
) induces a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
at infinity provided ‘
s_{\mathrm{L}}
is
small’ (which thanks to Corollary
2.14
can be assumed by taking
R
sufficiently
big). More precisely we claim the following assertion.

Lemma 4.9
.

Uniformly in
\lambda\in I
there exists the limit

(4.13)

\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\omega(\lambda,t,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

in the
C^{1}
-topology.
Moreover, possibly for enlarged
R>0
only, the map
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)

is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

depending continuously on
\lambda\in I
, and for any
(\lambda,\theta)\in I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}A(\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta))=(2\lambda)^{d/2}J_{+}(\lambda,\theta)\,\mathrm{d}A(\theta),

where
J_{+}
is from Lemma
4.8
.

Remark
.

For fixed
\lambda\in I
the map
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}

is called
the
asymptotic direction map
and its inverse

\theta_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)=\omega_{+}^{-1}(\lambda,\cdot)=(\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot))^{-1}
the
inverse asymptotic direction map
.

Proof.

Take
R_{0}>0
as in Corollary
2.14
,
and let
\sigma^{\prime}\in(0,\sigma)
.
For the moment all the estimates below are uniform in
R\geq R_{0}
.
We will possibly need
R
to be larger in Step IV.

Step I.
For any fixed
(\lambda,\theta)\in I\times{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
we prove the
existence of the limit (
4.13
) in the pointwise sense.
For each
i=1,\dots,d

we compute and bound, omitting the arguments, as

(4.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\omega_{i}&=|y|^{-1}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{i}-|y|^{-3}y_{i}y_{j}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}\\
&=|y|^{-1}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\bigl(\delta_{ij}-|y|^{-2}y_{i}y_{j}\bigr)\bigl((\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}-(2\lambda)^{1/2}|y|^{-1}y_{j}\bigr)\\
&={\mathcal{O}}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}}),\end{split}

where we have used Theorem
1.3

and Lemma
4.4
.
The integrability of (
4.15
) in
t\in(1,\infty)

implies that there exists the limit (
4.13
) in the pointwise sense.
In addition,
\omega_{+}\colon I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is continuous
since (
4.15
) is uniform in
(\lambda,\theta)\in I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Step II.
Take any local coordinates
\theta^{\prime}=(\theta^{\prime}_{2},\dots,\theta^{\prime}_{d})
of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
and set for any
\alpha=2,\dots,d

\Omega(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})=(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{1}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))^{2}+\dots+(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{d}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))^{2}.

We claim that for any compact subset
K
of the
associated (open) coordinate region
there exist
c_{1},C_{1}>0
such that for any
(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times K

(4.16)

\displaystyle c_{1}\leq\Omega(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})\leq C_{1}.

For that we compute and bound the
t
-derivative of
\Omega
as in Step I.
Since we have

(4.17)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i})=|y|^{-1}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i})-|y|^{-3}y_{i}y_{j}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j}),

we can write by using (
4.3a
)

(4.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i})&=-|y|^{-3}y_{j}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i})+|y|^{-1}(\nabla|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2})_{j}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{i}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+|y|^{-1}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla^{2}S_{\mathrm{L}})_{ij}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}+3|y|^{-5}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{k}y_{i}y_{j}y_{k}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}-|y|^{-3}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{i}y_{j}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})-|y|^{-3}y_{i}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}-|y|^{-3}y_{i}y_{j}(\nabla|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2})_{k}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{k})\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}-|y|^{-3}y_{i}y_{j}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla^{2}S_{\mathrm{L}})_{jk}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{k})\\
&=:B_{1}+\dots+B_{8}.\end{split}

To bound the terms on the right-hand side of (
4.18
)
we will first prove that

(4.19)

\displaystyle|y|^{-1}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i})=(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i})+\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}\langle t\rangle^{-\sigma^{\prime}})\Omega^{1/2}.

To keep the notation simple we prefer henceforth to state errors
like
\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}\langle t\rangle^{-\sigma^{\prime}})
as
\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}{t}^{-\sigma^{\prime}})
.
Now with this convention, it follows by (
4.5
) and Theorem
1.3
that

(4.20)

\displaystyle|y|=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}t+\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{1-\sigma^{\prime}}),\quad(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i})(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{i}=0,

so that (again thanks to Theorem
1.3
) we can rewrite (
4.17
) as

\displaystyle|y|^{-1}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i})-|y|^{-2}y_{i}\bigl((2\lambda)^{-1/2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}-|y|^{-1}y_{j}\bigr)(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i})+\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-\sigma^{\prime}})\sum^{d}_{j=1}\,|y|^{-1}|\partial_{\alpha}y_{j}|,

Hence by a summation and subtraction

\sum^{d}_{j=1}\,|y|^{-1}|\partial_{\alpha}y_{j}|\leq C\sum^{d}_{i=1}\,|\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i}|\leq C\sqrt{d}\,\Omega^{1/2},

which verifies (
4.19
).

Now we bound term by term

B_{1}+\dots+B_{8}=(B_{2}+B_{7})+(B_{4}+B_{5}+B_{6})+B_{8}+(B_{1}+B_{3}).

By (
1.6
) and (
4.19
)

\displaystyle B_{2}+B_{7}=\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}})\Omega^{1/2}

By (
4.20
), (
4.19
) and Theorem
1.3

\displaystyle B_{4}+B_{5}+B_{6}

\displaystyle=3|y|^{-4}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{k}y_{i}y_{k}\bigl((2\lambda)^{-1/2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}-|y|^{-1}y_{j}\bigr)(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+|y|^{-2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{i}\bigl((2\lambda)^{-1/2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}-|y|^{-1}y_{j}\bigr)(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}})\Omega^{1/2}.

By (
3.13
), (
4.20
), (
4.19
) and Theorem
1.3

\displaystyle B_{8}

\displaystyle=|y|^{-2}y_{i}\bigl((2\lambda)^{-1/2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}-|y|^{-1}y_{j}\bigr)|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla^{2}S_{\mathrm{L}})_{jk}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{k})

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}-\tfrac{1}{2}(2\lambda)^{-1/2}|y|^{-2}y_{i}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{2})_{k}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{k})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}})\Omega^{1/2}.

Finally by
(
4.20
), (
4.19
), Theorem
1.3

and (
3.2c
)
(see also Remark
3.2
)

\displaystyle B_{1}+B_{3}

\displaystyle=-(2\lambda)^{-1/2}|y|^{-2}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i})

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+|y|^{-2}\bigl((2\lambda)^{-1/2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}-|y|^{-1}y_{j}\bigr)|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})_{j}(\partial_{\alpha}y_{i})

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+(2\lambda)^{1/2}f^{-1}|y|^{-1}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\bigl((\partial_{i}f)(\partial_{j}f)+f(\nabla^{2}f)_{ij}\bigr)(\partial_{\alpha}y_{j})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}})\Omega^{1/2}.

Therefore, combining the above estimates, we obtain

(4.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i})&=\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}})\Omega^{1/2},\ \ \text{or}\ \ \tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\Omega=\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}})\Omega.\end{split}

This implies
\ln\Omega
is bounded, and thus the claim (
4.16
) is verified.

Step III.
Next we show
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

is continuously differentiable.
This is straightforward due to Step II.
By (
4.21
) and (
4.16
) it follows that

(4.22)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}(\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i})=\mathcal{O}(R^{-\sigma+\sigma^{\prime}}t^{-1-\sigma^{\prime}})

uniformly in
\lambda\in I
and locally uniformly in
\theta^{\prime}

in the coordinate region.
It is integrable in
t\in(1,\infty)
, and this implies there exists the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{i}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})

uniformly in
\lambda\in I
and locally uniformly in
\theta^{\prime}
in this region.
Hence the convergence (
4.13
) is in the
C^{1}
-topology,
and
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)
is continuously differentiable as wanted.
Note that
\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{+}
is continuous also in
\lambda\in I
since
these estimates are uniform in
\lambda\in I
.

Step IV.
We prove that
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism,
provided
R\geq R_{0}
is large.
By (
4.15
), (
4.22
) and the fact that

\omega(\lambda,t,\theta)=\theta
for any
t\in(0,(2\lambda)^{1/2}R]
, see (
4.6
),
it follows that for any sufficiently large
R\geq R_{0}
and any

(\lambda,\theta)\in I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(4.23)

\lvert{\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta)-\theta}\rvert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lvert\nabla_{\theta}(\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta)-\theta)\rvert\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

Due to the second bound of (
4.23
) we can apply the inverse function
theorem to
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)
, and hence the image
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\mathbb{S}^{d-1})\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is open.
On the other hand, since
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is compact and
\omega_{+}
is continuous,

\omega_{+}(\lambda,\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
is also closed.
Thus
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)
is surjective due to the connectedness of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Now it suffices to show the injectivity of
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)
.
Suppose that for some
\lambda\in I
and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}

\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta_{1})=\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta_{2}).

Then, if we let
\gamma
be a grand circle segment, or a geodesic of minimal length, on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

connecting
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
, we can estimate by using (
4.23
)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigl(\theta_{1}-\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta_{1}),\theta_{2}-\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\,\big\lvert\nabla_{\theta}(\theta-\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta))_{|\theta=\gamma(t)}\big\rvert\,\lvert\dot{\gamma}(t)\rvert\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\leq\tfrac{\pi}{4}\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),

where
\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}
are the standard metrics there.
This implies
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}
since
\pi<4
.
Thus we conclude
\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)\colon{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\to{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
is a
C^{1}
–diffeomorphism.

Step V.
Finally we show the identity (
4.14
) by
rewriting the Euclidean volume measure as follows.
In the standard spherical coordinates
(r,\omega^{\prime})
we can write

\mathrm{d}x(r,\omega^{\prime})=r^{d-1}\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}A(\omega(\omega^{\prime}))=r^{d-1}a(\omega^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{d}.

Let us further change the variables to
(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})
by using (
4.12
).
We compute the Jacobian by the cofactor expansion.
Some of the first order derivatives of (
4.12
) are bounded by (
4.15
) and (
4.16
),
and we only have to note that by Theorem
1.3
, (
4.19
) and (
4.16
)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}r

\displaystyle=|y|^{-1}y\cdot(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})/|\nabla S_{L}|^{2}=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}+\mathcal{O}(t^{-\sigma^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}r

\displaystyle=|y|^{-1}y\cdot\partial_{\alpha}y=\big(|y|^{-1}y-(2\lambda)^{-1/2}\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}\big)\cdot\partial_{\alpha}y=\mathcal{O}(t^{1-\sigma^{\prime}}).

Then the cofactor expansion yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x(r(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}),\omega^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=t^{d-1}\bigl((2\lambda)^{-d/2}K^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})+\mathcal{O}(t^{-\sigma^{\prime}})\bigr)a(\omega^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{d},

where
K^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\cdot)
is the Jacobian of
\omega(\lambda,t,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

in the local coordinates
\theta^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime}
.
The above expression has to coincide with (
4.8
), so that

\displaystyle t^{d-1}\bigl((2\lambda)^{-d/2}K^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})+\mathcal{O}(t^{-\sigma^{\prime}})\bigr)a(\omega^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{d}=J(\lambda,t,\theta(\theta^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{d}A(\theta(\theta^{\prime})),

or

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}A(\omega(\omega^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=K^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime})a(\omega^{\prime}(\lambda,t,\theta^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}_{d}

\displaystyle=\bigl((2\lambda)^{d/2}t^{-(d-1)}J(\lambda,t,\theta(\theta^{\prime}))+\mathcal{O}(t^{-\sigma})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}A(\theta(\theta^{\prime})).

Hence by letting
t\to\infty
we obtain (
4.14
).
We are done.
∎

4.2.4.
Comparison of eikonal distances

Here we compare
S_{\mathrm{L}}
(solving the eikonal equation for

V_{\mathrm{L}}
) with the function
S
from
Theorem
1.5
(solving the eikonal equation for

V
, with
\lambda
in the same interval
I
).
We show that their difference has a radial limit at
infinity (as a step of the proof we first establish the eikonal radial limit).
We present a slightly generalized assertion localized to a conic subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For any
R^{\prime}>0
and any open subset
U\subseteq{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
we set

\Gamma_{R^{\prime},U}=\bigl\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\,\big|\,\lvert x\rvert>R^{\prime}\text{ and }\hat{x}\in U\bigr\},\quad\hat{x}=|x|^{-1}x.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
R>0
be large enough as in Lemma
4.9
.
Let
R^{\prime}>0
, and
U\subseteq{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
be open,
and assume
S=\sqrt{2\lambda}|x|(1+s)\in C(I;C^{2}(\Gamma_{R^{\prime},U}))
satisfies:

(1)

For each
\lambda\in I
,
S(\lambda,\cdot)
solves (
1.3a
) on
\Gamma_{R^{\prime},U}
.

(2)

For any compact subset
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
there exist
\epsilon,C>0

such that for any
|\alpha|\leq 2
and
(\lambda,x)\in I^{\prime}\times\Gamma_{R^{\prime},U}

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}s(\lambda,x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\langle x\rangle^{-\epsilon-|\alpha|}.

Then the following assertions hold.

(1)

There exists the limit

\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(S(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\big)

taken locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\theta)
with
\lambda\in I
and
\theta\in\omega_{+}^{-1}(\lambda,U)=\theta_{+}(\lambda,U)
.
In particular, if
S_{1}\in C(I;C^{2}(\Gamma_{R^{\prime},U}))
also satisfies
the above conditions
1
and
2
, then there exists the limit
