\Sigma(\lambda,\theta):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(S_{1}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))-S(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\big)

taken locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\theta)
with
\lambda\in I
and

\theta\in\theta_{+}(\lambda,U)
.

(2)

The quantities in
1
can also be computed
as the limits

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\omega)

\displaystyle:=\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))=\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(S(\lambda,r\omega)-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,r\omega)\big),

\displaystyle\Theta(\lambda,\omega)

\displaystyle:=\Sigma(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))=\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(S_{1}(\lambda,r\omega)-S(\lambda,r\omega)\big),

both taken locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\omega)\in I\times U
.

Proof.

Step I.
To prove the assertions of
1
we only show the first one.
The
second assertion is obvious from the first one.
The following bounds are locally uniform
in
(\lambda,\theta)
with
\lambda\in I
and
\theta\in\theta_{+}(\lambda,U)
, however we shall not elaborate on that
feature.
By (
4.3a
), (
1.3a
) and (
1.6
)
we compute for large
t>0

(4.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(S-S_{\mathrm{L}})&=|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\\
&=-\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})^{2}-|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}V_{\mathrm{S}}.\end{split}

We are going to show the integrability of (
4.24
) at infinity.
The second term on the right-hand side of (
4.24
) is clearly integrable
due to Theorem
1.3
and (
4.5
),
and we discuss only the first term.
It suffices to show that for some small
\delta>0

(4.25)

\displaystyle u:=(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(t^{-1-\delta}),

see also
[
HS
, Theorem 2.3]
and its proof.
Similarly to (
4.24
) we can compute the derivative of
u
as

(4.26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u&=(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot(\nabla^{2}S-\nabla^{2}S_{\mathrm{L}})(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\\
&=-(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})-(\nabla V_{\mathrm{S}})\cdot(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}).\end{split}

From the assumptions on
S
we deduce that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}S\geq 2\lambda S^{-1}I-C_{2}S^{-1-\epsilon}I-S^{-1}(\nabla S)\otimes(\nabla S).

We apply this bound to (
4.26
), use (
1.3a
) and
(
1.6
) (as in (
4.24
)), and conclude that

(4.27)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u&\leq-2\lambda S^{-1}u+C_{2}S^{-1-\epsilon}u\\
&\phantom{{}={}}{}\quad\quad+S^{-1}\bigl((\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot(\nabla S)\bigr)^{2}+|\nabla V_{\mathrm{S}}|u^{1/2}\\
&=-2\lambda S^{-1}u+C_{2}S^{-1-\epsilon}u+4^{-1}S^{-1}(u-2V_{\mathrm{S}})^{2}+|\nabla V_{\mathrm{S}}|u^{1/2}.\end{split}

By Theorem
1.3
, (
4.5
) and the assumptions on
S

we observe , letting
\delta\in(0,\epsilon)
be small enough, that

|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{2}=2\lambda+\mathcal{O}(t^{-\delta}),\quad S=t+\mathcal{O}(t^{1-\delta}),\quad u=(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(t^{-\delta}).

Substituting these estimates into (
4.27
),
we obtain for large
t>0

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u

\displaystyle\leq-2t^{-1}u+C_{3}t^{-1-\delta}u+C_{3}t^{-2-\delta}\leq-(2-\delta)t^{-1}u+C_{3}t^{-2-\delta}.

This differential inequality implies (
4.25
). Hence we are
done with
1
.

Step II.
We prove the first assertion of
2

(the other one in
2
follows from that).
Fixing any compact subset
K\subset I\times U
we are going to prove

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\omega)\in K}\bigl|\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))-S(\lambda,r\omega)+S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,r\omega)\bigr|=0.

Using (
4.12
) and Lemma

4.9
, let us first
note that

(4.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\omega)\in K}\bigl|\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega)-\theta(\lambda,r\omega)\bigr|=0.

In fact, changing variables
from
(r,\omega)
to
(t,\theta)
and using
t=S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,r\omega)
, it follows
that uniformly in
(\lambda,\omega)\in K
the quantity
\tfrac{{\rm d}t}{{\rm d}r}=\nabla S_{L}\cdot\omega>\sqrt{\lambda}
for large
r
,
and hence taking
r\to\infty
corresponds to taking
t\to\infty
.
Thus for some compact subset
K^{\prime}\subset\{(\lambda,\theta);\ \lambda\in I,\ \theta\in\theta_{+}(\lambda,U)\}

we can compute (thanks to Lemma
4.9
)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\omega)\in K}\bigl|\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega)-\theta(\lambda,r\omega)\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\theta)\in K^{\prime}}\bigl|\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,t,\theta))-\theta(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\lambda,\theta)\in K^{\prime}}\bigl|\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega_{+}(\lambda,\theta))-\theta\bigr|=0.

Now by (
4.28
), the above change of variables and the assertion (1) it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\omega)\in K}\bigl|\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))-S(\lambda,r\omega)+S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,r\omega)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\omega)\in K}\bigl|\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta(\lambda,r\omega))-S(\lambda,r\omega)+S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,r\omega)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\theta)\in K^{\prime}}\bigl|\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta)))-S(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))+S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\theta)\in K^{\prime}}\bigl|\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta)-S(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))+S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))\bigr|

\displaystyle=0.

We are done.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

Such a localized version has an application in
3
-body long-range stationary scattering theory
[
Sk1
]
,
for which we should take
U\subseteq{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
such that the closure
\overline{U}
does not intersect the ‘collision planes’.
For such
U
the function

(4.29)

{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\Theta(\lambda,\omega)}={\rm e}^{{\rm i}\Sigma(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))};\quad\theta_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)=\omega_{+}^{-1}(\lambda,\cdot),

induces a well-defined family of unitary multiplication operators on

L^{2}(U)(\subseteq{\mathcal{G}})
being strongly continuous in
\lambda
.
Upon varying
U
under the above constraint the function (
4.29
)
is defined almost everywhere on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

and constitutes a strongly continuous
{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{G}}\big)
–valued function of
\lambda
.
The transformation
factor
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\Theta(\lambda,\omega)}
(exhibiting ‘covariance’) and (
1.10
) are
applicably to the

3
-body problem
[
Sk1
]
, cf. Subsection
1.2.5
. In particular it is not possible to
take
U=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
in that application. However in the
present paper we only use Lemma
4.10
with

U=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, see for example Remark
1.9

3

and Theorem
1.12

(
3
).

4.3.
Stationary wave operators

4.3.1.
Construction for the regularized potential

Here we discuss an analogue of
Theorem
1.5
for
H_{\mathrm{L}}
in the
spherical eikonal coordinates.
Once the strong radiation condition bounds from Theorem
1.23

are established
and the spherical eikonal coordinates are fixed,
the construction is rather straightforward,
following the schemes of
[
HS
,
Sk2
]
.
Set for any
\xi\in\mathcal{G}

(4.30)

\displaystyle\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi](\lambda,x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{1/2}}{(2\lambda)^{1/4}}\chi(r)r^{-(d-1)/2}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,x)}\xi(\hat{x}),\quad r=|x|,\,\,\hat{x}=|x|^{-1}x,

respectively, where
\chi
is from (
1.4
) (see also (
1.9
)).

Proposition 4.12
.

(1)

For any
\lambda\in I

there exist unique
E^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{G}})
such that for any

\psi\in\mathcal{B}

(4.31)

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi-\phi^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}_{\pm}[E^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi](\lambda,\cdot)\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0},

respectively.

(2)

The mappings
E^{\pm}\colon I\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{G}

are continuous.

(3)

For any
\lambda\in I

E^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}E^{\pm}(\lambda)=\delta(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda),

respectively.

Before proving Proposition
4.12

we present a trace type theorem in a form appropriate for our application.
Note that by Fubini’s theorem we can identify

L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1})\simeq L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};\mathcal{G}).

To be precise
we denote the above identification operator for the moment by
\iota
,
i.e. for any
\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

we let

\iota(\psi)(t)=\psi(t,\cdot)\in\mathcal{G}\ \ \text{for a.e.\ }t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\psi\in H^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
with
s>k+1/2
.
Then

\displaystyle\iota(\psi)\in C^{k}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};\mathcal{G}),\ \ \text{and}\ \ \tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}\iota(\psi)=\iota\bigl(\tfrac{\partial^{l}}{\partial t^{l}}\psi\bigr)\ \text{for}\ l=0,\dots,k.

Proof of Lemma
4.13
.

By a partition-of-unity argument we can reduce the
claims to similar ones in
a coordinate region.
Then we can mimic the proof of the familiar Sobolev embedding theorem.
We omit the details.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.12
.

Step I.
Let

\lambda\in I
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be
given, and then let

\Psi(t)=J(\lambda,t,\cdot)^{1/2}{\rm e}^{\mp{\rm i}S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\cdot))}\bigl(R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi\bigr)(y(\lambda,t,\cdot))\in\mathcal{G};\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

Since
R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it follows from
Lemma
4.13
, that
\Psi\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};\mathcal{G})
.
We first show the existence of the limits

(4.32)

D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi=\vGlim_{t\to\infty}\Psi(t).

By the fundamental theorem of calculus we have

\Psi(t)=\Psi(1)+\int_{1}^{t}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

and it suffices to show that the last integrand is integrable as a
\mathcal{G}
-valued function.
We can compute it
by Lemma
4.13
,
(
4.11
), (
4.5
), (
4.3a
) and (
1.21
) as

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi

\displaystyle=J^{1/2}{\rm e}^{\mp{\rm i}S_{\mathrm{L}}}\bigl(\mathrm{i}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla\cdot|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\bigr)R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi

\displaystyle=J^{1/2}{\rm e}^{\mp{\rm i}S_{\mathrm{L}}}\bigl(\mathrm{i}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}\gamma_{\|}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2})\cdot(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\bigr)R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi.

By (
1.23a
) and (
3.13
) we can find

\delta>0
and

\Phi\in L^{2}_{(1+\delta)/2}
such that

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi(t)=J(\lambda,t,\cdot)^{1/2}\Phi(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\cdot)).

Then by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and (
4.10
)

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\bigl\|\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi(t)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\Phi(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\lambda,t}(\theta)\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\biggl(\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{-1-\delta}\,\mathrm{d}t\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\biggl(\int_{1}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|(|\cdot|^{(1+\delta)/2}\Phi)(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{\lambda,t}(\theta)\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}_{(1+\delta)/2}}.

Hence there exist the limits (
4.32
).
We note
D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi
are continuous in
\lambda\in I

since
\Psi(t)
is continuous in
\lambda\in I
, and the above estimates are
locally uniform in this variable.

Step II.
Next we set for any
\lambda\in I
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(4.33a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}E^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi&=c(\lambda)(2\lambda)^{-d/4}J_{+}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\cdot))^{-1/2}\bigl(D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\bigr)(\theta_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)),\\
c(\lambda)&=(2\pi)^{-1/2}(2\lambda)^{1/2},\end{split}

and verify that they satisfy
(
4.31
). For completeness of presentation note that

(4.33b)

\bigl\|E^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}=c(\lambda)\bigl\|D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}},

cf. Lemma
4.9
.

By (
4.32
) it follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\|D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi-{}&J(\lambda,\tau,\cdot)^{1/2}{\rm e}^{\mp{\rm i}S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))}(R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi)(y(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\tau=0,\end{split}

and along with (
4.10
),
Lemma
4.8
and the asymptotics

|y(\lambda,\tau,\cdot)|/\tau\to(2\lambda)^{-1/2}
this implies

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\int_{\{S_{L}\leq t\}}\bigl|{}

\displaystyle(2\lambda)^{-(d-1)/4}|x|^{-(d-1)/2}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,x)}J_{+}(\lambda,\theta(\lambda,x))^{-1/2}(D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi)(\theta(\lambda,x))

\displaystyle{}-(R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm{\rm i}0)\psi)(x)\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

Hence it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\int_{\{S_{L}\leq t\}}|x|^{-(d-1)/2}\bigl|{}

\displaystyle J_{+}(\lambda,\theta(\lambda,x))^{-1/2}(D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi)(\theta(\lambda,x))

\displaystyle{}-J_{+}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\hat{x}))^{-1/2}\bigl(D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\bigr)(\theta_{+}(\lambda,\hat{x}))\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

In turn, if we let

u(\lambda,\theta)=J_{+}(\lambda,\theta)^{-1/2}(D^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi)(\theta)

and again use eikonal spherical coordinates, it suffices to prove
that

(4.34)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\|u(\lambda,\cdot)-u\bigl(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\tau=0.

To prove (
4.34
), first note that for any
v,w\in\mathcal{G}

(4.35)

\bigl\|v\bigl(\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))\bigr)-w\bigl(\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{3}\|v-w\|_{\mathcal{G}};\quad\tau\in[1,\infty).

Here we used that the coordinate change
\theta\to\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))
converges
to the identity map in the
C^{1}
-topology as
\tau\to\infty
. Next we
estimate
for any
v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

(4.36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bigl\|u(\lambda,\cdot)-u\bigl(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot)\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}\\
&\leq\|u(\lambda,\cdot)-v\|_{\mathcal{G}}+\bigl\|v-v\bigl(\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot)\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}+\bigl\|v\bigl(\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))\bigr)-u\bigl(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega(\lambda,\tau,\cdot))\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}.\end{split}

The first term on the right-hand side
of (
4.36
) can be arbitrarily small by choosing
appropriate
v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
. Due to (
4.35
)
then also the third term is small (uniformly in
\tau
).
For any such
v
fixed, clearly the second term converges to
0
as
\tau\to\infty
.
This verifies (
4.34
).

Step III.
We next show that for any
\lambda\in I
and
\psi\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

(4.37)

\displaystyle\|E^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}=\langle\psi,\delta(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda)\psi\rangle.

Using for
T>0
the function
\chi_{T}
from (
1.5
), we set

(4.38)

\eta_{T}=1-\chi_{T},\quad\eta_{T}^{\prime}=-T^{-1}\chi^{\prime}(|\cdot|/T).

Introducing also the notation
\phi=R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm{\mathrm{i}}0)\psi
, we then write

\displaystyle 2\pi\langle\psi,\delta(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda)\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda)\phi,\phi\rangle=\pm 2\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\langle(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda)\phi,\eta_{T}\phi\rangle.

By an integration by parts this leads to

(4.39)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\pi\langle\psi,\delta(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda)\psi\rangle&=\mp\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\langle\hat{x}\cdot p\phi,\eta_{T}^{\prime}\phi\rangle\\
&=\mp\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\langle\hat{x}\cdot\gamma\phi,\eta_{T}^{\prime}\phi\rangle-\lim_{T\to\infty}\langle|\nabla\chi_{1}S_{\mathrm{L}}|\phi,\eta_{T}^{\prime}\phi\rangle.\end{split}

The contribution from the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.39
)
vanishes due to (
1.23c
). As for the second term
we rewrite the integral in the standard spherical coordinates,
substitute (
4.31
) and conclude that

-\lim_{T\to\infty}\langle|\nabla\chi_{1}S_{\mathrm{L}}|\phi,\eta_{T}^{\prime}\phi\rangle=2\pi\|E^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2},

hence the claim (
4.37
) for
\psi\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

Step IV.
Now we prove the assertions (1)–(3).
The identity (
4.37
) immediately implies that

E^{\pm}(\lambda)
extend continuously as
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{G}
,
and the extensions obviously satisfy (
4.31
) and (
4.37
).
This verifies the assertions (1) and (3).
To see the joint continuity of
E^{\pm}\colon I\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{G}

we let
\lambda,\mu\in I
and
\psi,\varphi\in\mathcal{B}
.
We take another
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and split

\displaystyle\|E^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi-E^{\pm}(\mu)\varphi\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\|E^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi-E^{\pm}(\lambda)\zeta\|_{\mathcal{G}}+\|E^{\pm}(\lambda)\zeta-E^{\pm}(\mu)\zeta\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\|E^{\pm}(\mu)\zeta-E^{\pm}(\mu)\varphi\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\langle\psi-\zeta,\delta(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda)(\psi-\zeta)\bigr\rangle^{1/2}+\|E^{\pm}(\lambda)\zeta-E^{\pm}(\mu)\zeta\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\bigl\langle\zeta-\varphi,\delta(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\mu)(\zeta-\varphi)\bigr\rangle^{1/2}.

By the locally uniform boundedness of
R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{*})

the first and third terms on the right-hand side above can be
arbitrarily small (uniformly in the spectral parameter)
if we choose
\psi,\varphi
and
\zeta
close to each other.
For fixed such
\zeta
the second term can be arbitrarily small
if
\lambda,\mu
are close. This is easily seen using the formula
(
4.33a
) and the continuity of
D^{\pm}(\cdot)\zeta
recorded
in Step I.
Hence (2) is verified.
∎

4.3.2.
Construction in general

Now we prove Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
1.8
.
We implement the effects from
V_{\mathrm{S}}+q

by the second resolvent identities

(4.40)

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=R_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\bigl(1-(V_{\mathrm{S}}+q)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\bigr)\in{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{*}).}

Proof of Theorem
1.5
(
1
) and (
2
).

Take the function
\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}
from
Lemma
4.10
with
U=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and
we define
F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\in\mathcal{G}
for any
(\lambda,\psi)\in I\times\mathcal{B}
as

(4.41)

F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi=\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}E^{\pm}(\lambda)\bigl(1-(V_{\mathrm{S}}+q)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\bigr)\psi,

where

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\omega)=\Sigma_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega));\quad\theta_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)=\omega_{+}(\lambda,\cdot)^{-1}.

Then we can deduce (
1.10
)
by (
4.40
), (
4.41
) and (
4.31
),
verifying the assertion (
1
).
As for (
2
), note that the mappings

I\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B},\ (\lambda,\psi)\mapsto\bigl(1-(V_{\mathrm{S}}+q)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\bigr)\psi

are continuous thanks to Theorem
3.17
.
Then the assertion (
2
) is clear from Lemmas
4.9
and
4.10

and Proposition
4.12
.
∎

To prove the assertions
(
3
) and (
4
) in
Theorem
1.5
we will use the

Sommerfeld uniqueness
for
H
,
or a characterization of the limiting resolvents
R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)
.
The following version of the property is almost a direct consequence from Theorem
1.23
and (
4.40
),
cf.
[
AIIS2
,
Is1
]
,
however let us present it for completeness of the paper.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\lambda\in I
,
\psi\in\mathcal{B}

and
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
.
Then

\phi=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi
holds if and only if
both of the following assertions hold:

(1)

\phi
solves the Helmholtz equation
(H-\lambda)\phi=\psi
in the distributional sense.

(2)

\phi
satisfies the outgoing/incoming radiation condition
\gamma_{\|}\phi\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}
.

Remarks 4.15
.

(1)

Here
\gamma_{\|}
is defined by (
1.21
) with respect to

S=S_{\mathrm{L}}
. In the proposition we can equally well use the more natural

\gamma_{\|}
defined by (
1.21
) with respect to
the general
S
, or in fact
\gamma_{\|}
given in terms of the
expression
S=S_{0}=\sqrt{2\lambda}|x|
.

(2)

For
\psi=0
the above result implies the (sharp)
version of a
Rellich theorem
: If
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
solves

(H-\lambda)\phi=0
, then
\phi=0
.

Proof.

The necessity is clear from (
4.40
),
(
4.1a
) and (
1.23a
).
Thus it remains to prove the sufficiency.
Assume (
1
) and (
2
) of the assertion,
and set

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}=\phi-R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi\in\mathcal{B}^{*}.

Then by (
4.40
),
(
4.1a
) and (
1.23a
)

\phi^{\prime}
satisfies

(H-\lambda)\phi^{\prime}=0,\quad\gamma_{\|}\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}.

We can further verify
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}
.
Using a notation similar to
(
4.38
):

\eta_{T}=1-\chi(\chi_{1}S_{L}/T),\quad\eta_{T}^{\prime}=-T^{-1}\chi^{\prime}(\chi_{1}S_{L}/T),

we have

\displaystyle 2\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\eta_{T}(H-\lambda)\bigr)=\pm|\nabla\chi_{1}S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{2}\eta_{T}^{\prime}+\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\eta_{T}^{\prime}\gamma_{\|}).

Hence

\displaystyle 0\leq-\bigl\langle\phi^{\prime},|\nabla\chi_{1}S|^{2}\eta_{T}^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\leq\pm\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl\langle\phi^{\prime},\eta_{T}^{\prime}\gamma_{\|}\phi^{\prime}\bigr\rangle.

By letting
T\to\infty
,
it follows that indeed
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}
.

Since
H
does not have positive eigenvalues it does not neither have generalized eigenfunctions with positive eigenvalues
in
\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}
,
see
[
AIIS2
, Theorem 1.4]
,
and we certainly obtain that
\phi^{\prime}=0
. Hence
\phi=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi
.
∎

The Sommerfeld uniqueness provides the following useful
representations.
Recalling (
4.30
) we define for any
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi](x)=\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi](\lambda,x)=(H-\lambda)\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi](\lambda,x)=(H-\lambda)\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi](x).

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
(\lambda,\xi)\in I\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
.
Then

(4.42a)

\displaystyle\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]\in\mathcal{B}^{*},\quad\gamma_{\|}\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0},\quad\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]\in\mathcal{B}.

Moreover,

(4.42b)

\displaystyle\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]

\displaystyle=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]

and

(4.42c)

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi\bigr)&=\pm\tfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\bigl(\phi^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}_{\pm}[\xi]-R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)\psi^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}_{\pm}[\xi]\bigr),\end{split}

respectively.

Proof.

The first inclusion from (
4.42a
) is obvious.
To prove the last one
we use (
1.6
) to rewrite it for
|x|\geq 2R
as

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{1/2}}{2(2\lambda)^{1/4}}r^{-(d-1)/2}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}S_{\mathrm{L}}}\Bigl(p^{2}\pm 2(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot p+\mathrm{i}(d-1)r^{-1}(\nabla r)\cdot p\mp\mathrm{i}(\Delta S_{\mathrm{L}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\pm\mathrm{i}(d-1)r^{-1}(\nabla r)\cdot(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})+\tfrac{(d-1)(d-3)}{4}r^{-2}+2(V_{\mathrm{S}}+q)\Bigr)\xi.

Noting that

\displaystyle(\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot p

\displaystyle=\bigl((\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})\cdot(\nabla r)\bigr)(\nabla r)\cdot p+{\bigl(\nabla(S_{\mathrm{L}}-\sqrt{2\lambda}r)\bigr)}\cdot\bigl(1-(\nabla r)\otimes(\nabla r)\bigr)p,

\displaystyle(\nabla r)\cdot p\xi

\displaystyle=0,\quad p\xi={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1})

and using Theorem
1.3
,
we obtain the last inclusion of (
4.42a
).
The second one can be verified similarly.

Now (
4.42b
) follows from (
4.42a
)
and Proposition
4.14
,
and it remains to verify (
4.42c
).
We can write for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\psi,F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi\bigr)\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi,\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(2\lambda)^{1/2}}{2\pi}\lim_{T\to\infty}\bigl\langle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi,\chi_{T}^{\prime}\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]\bigr\rangle,

where
\chi_{T}^{\prime}=T^{-1}\chi^{\prime}(|x|/T)
, cf. (
4.38
).
We use Theorem
1.3
to proceed as

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\psi,F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi\bigr)\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{T\to\infty}\bigl\langle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi,\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}((\nabla\chi_{T})\cdot p)\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm\tfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\lim_{T\to\infty}\bigl\langle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi,[H-\lambda,1-\chi_{T}]\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm\tfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\bigl\langle\psi,\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]-R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}0)\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]\bigr\rangle.

This implies (
4.42c
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.5
(
3
) and (
4
).

To prove the first identity of (
3
) it suffices to show that
for any
\lambda\in I
,
\xi\in\mathcal{G}
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(4.43)

\displaystyle\langle(H-\lambda)F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\xi,\psi\rangle=0.

However, by Proposition
4.14
we have

R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda)\psi=\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \ \text{so that }\ F^{\pm}(\lambda)(H-\lambda)\psi=0.

Thus (
4.43
) follows.
On the other hand, by (
4.41
),
Proposition
4.12
and (
4.40
) we have for any
\psi\in\mathcal{B}

(4.44)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}&=\bigl\langle(1-(V_{\mathrm{S}}+q)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0))\psi,\\
&\qquad\delta(H_{\mathrm{L}}-\lambda)(1-(V_{\mathrm{S}}+q)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0))\psi\big\rangle\\
&=\langle\psi,\delta(H-\lambda)\psi\rangle.\end{split}

This implies the second identity of (
3
).

To prove (
4
) we use (
4.42b
).
In fact, along with (
1.10
) and Lemma
4.10
,
it says for any
(\lambda,\xi)\in I\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

(4.45)

\displaystyle F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\xi]=\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi

or

\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})\subseteq F^{\pm}(\lambda)\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
.
Hence we obtain the assertion (
4
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.8
.

The existence of the wave operators
F^{\pm}(\lambda)
is already shown in
Theorem
1.5
.
Next by Theorem
1.5
(
3
) and (
4
)
the scattering matrix
\mathsf{S}(\lambda)
is defined at least on a dense subspace of
\mathcal{G}
,
and in fact it preserves the norm and maps onto a dense set.
Therefore
\mathsf{S}(\lambda)
extends uniquely to a unitary operator on
\mathcal{G}
.

Finally we are left with the strong continuity.
By (
1.11
) and (
4.45
)
it follows that for any
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

(4.46)

F^{+}(\lambda)\psi_{-}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\eta]=\mathsf{S}(\lambda)F^{-}(\lambda)\psi_{-}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\eta]=\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\eta.

Note the above left-hand side is continuous in
\lambda\in I
,
and so is the right-hand side.
Now we fix any
\xi\in\mathcal{G}
and
\lambda\in I
,
and for any
\epsilon>0
choose
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
and
\delta>0

such that for any
\mu\in(\lambda-\delta,\lambda+\delta)

\bigl\|\xi-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\mu,\cdot)}\eta\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}<\epsilon.

Then by the unitarity of the scattering matrix, for any
\mu\in(\lambda-\delta,\lambda+\delta)

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\xi-\mathsf{S}(\mu)\xi\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\bigl(\xi-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\eta\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}+\bigl\|\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\eta-\mathsf{S}(\mu)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\mu,\cdot)}\eta\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\bigl\|\mathsf{S}(\mu)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\mu,\cdot)}\eta-\xi\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle<2\epsilon+\bigl\|\mathsf{S}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\eta-\mathsf{S}(\mu)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\mu,\cdot)}\eta\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}.

By letting
\delta>0
be smaller if necessary the above right-hand side is bounded by
3\epsilon
.
Thus we obtain the desired strong continuity.
∎

4.4.
Generalized eigenfunctions

We next prove Theorem
1.10
.

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

Step I.
We first show that, if
\phi\in\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
and
\xi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{G}
satisfy (
1.12a
),
then (
1.12c
) holds.
For that we first compute

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}2^{-m}\|1_{m}\phi\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}2^{-m}\bigl\|1_{m}\bigl(\phi_{+}^{S}[\xi_{+}]-\phi_{-}^{S}[\xi_{-}]\bigr)\bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\pi}{(2\lambda)^{1/2}}\biggl(\|\xi_{+}\|_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{2}+\|\xi_{-}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}-\lim_{m\to\infty}2^{2-m}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\int_{[2^{m-1},2^{m})\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}S(\lambda,r\omega)}\overline{\xi_{-}(\omega)}\xi_{+}(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}A(\omega)\biggr).

Here the last limit vanishes. In fact, we can integrate by parts as

\displaystyle\int_{2^{m-1}}^{2^{m}}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}S(\lambda,r\omega)}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}(\partial_{r}S(\lambda,r\omega))^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}S(\lambda,r\omega)}\Big|_{2^{m-1}}^{2^{m}}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\tfrac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\int_{2^{m-1}}^{2^{m}}(\partial_{r}S(\lambda,r\omega))^{-2}(\partial_{r}^{2}S(\lambda,r\omega))\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}S(\lambda,r\omega)}\,\mathrm{d}r,

and it does not contribute to the limit by the conditions of Theorem
1.5
.
Thus we obtain

(4.47)

\displaystyle\|\xi_{+}\|_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{2}+\|\xi_{-}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(2\lambda)^{1/2}}{\pi}\lim_{m\to\infty}2^{-m}\|1_{m}\phi\|^{2}.

On the other hand, proceeding as in the proof of
Proposition
4.16
and using in the last step the
integration by parts from above,
we compute

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\to\infty}\bigl\langle\phi,{\rm i}[H-\lambda,1-\chi_{T}]\phi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=-\lim_{T\to\infty}\bigl\langle\phi,\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}((\nabla r)\cdot p)\chi_{T}^{\prime}\phi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=-\lim_{T\to\infty}\bigl\langle\phi_{+}^{S}[\xi_{+}]-\phi_{-}^{S}[\xi_{-}],\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}((\nabla r)\cdot p)\chi_{T}^{\prime}\phi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=-(2\lambda)^{1/2}\lim_{T\to\infty}\bigl\langle\phi_{+}^{S}[\xi_{+}]+\phi_{-}^{S}[\xi_{-}],\chi_{T}^{\prime}\phi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=2\pi\bigl(\|\xi_{-}\|_{{\mathcal{G}}}^{2}-\|\xi_{+}\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\bigr).

In combination with (
4.47
) this verifies (
1.12c
).

Step II.
Here we prove the uniqueness asserted in
1
.
Suppose
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
and
\xi_{\pm}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
also satisfy (
1.12a
).
Then we have

(4.48)

\displaystyle(\phi-\phi^{\prime})-\phi_{+}^{S}[\xi_{+}-\xi_{+}^{\prime}]+\phi_{-}^{S}[\xi_{-}-\xi_{-}^{\prime}]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}.

If
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
, it follows that
\xi_{\pm}=\xi_{\pm}^{\prime}
by the result of Step I.
On the other hand, if either of
\xi_{\pm}=\xi_{\pm}^{\prime}
hold,
then we have
\xi_{\mp}=\xi_{\mp}^{\prime}
, respectively, again by the result of Step I.
This and (
4.48
) imply
\phi-\phi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
,
but then it follows that
\phi-\phi^{\prime}=0
thanks to Remark
4.15

2
.
Thus we obtain the uniqueness.

Step III.
Here we complete the assertions
1
and
2
.
Note for any
\xi\in\mathcal{G}

(4.49)

\displaystyle F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\xi\mp\tfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\bigl(\phi^{S}_{\pm}[\xi]-\phi^{S}_{\mp}[\mathsf{S}(\lambda)^{\mp 1}\xi]\bigr)\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0}.

In fact, by (
4.42c
), (
1.10
),
(
1.11
) and (
4.45
) (the latter applied as in (
4.46
))
we have for any
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

\displaystyle F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi\bigr)\mp\tfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\bigl(\phi^{S}_{\pm}\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi\bigr]-\phi^{S}_{\mp}\bigl[\mathsf{S}(\lambda)^{\mp 1}\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}\xi\bigr]\bigr)\in\mathcal{B}^{*}_{0},

and then by density of
\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subseteq\mathcal{G}

and the continuity of
F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}
,
\phi^{S}_{\pm}
and
\mathsf{S}(\lambda)^{\pm 1}

we obtain (
4.49
).
Now, if either of
\xi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{G}
is given,
then the vectors
\phi\in\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
and
\xi_{\mp}\in\mathcal{G}
are given by (
1.12b
), respectively, and
obviously satisfy (
1.12a
) thanks to (
4.49
).
By the uniqueness from Step II we are done with the case where either of

\xi_{\pm}\in\mathcal{G}
is given first.

Next, let
\phi\in\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
be given first.
By the above arguments and Step II it suffices to show there exist
\xi_{\pm}\in{\mathcal{G}}

satisfying
\phi=2\pi\mathrm{i}F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\xi_{\pm}
.
For each
T\geq 1
we can find
\xi_{\pm,T}\in\mathcal{G}

such that for any
\eta\in\mathcal{G}

\langle\eta,\xi_{\pm,T}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}=\pm\tfrac{(2\lambda)^{1/2}}{2\pi}\bigl\langle\phi^{S}_{\pm}[\eta],\chi_{T}^{\prime}\phi\bigr\rangle.

Obviously such
\xi_{\pm,T}\in\mathcal{G}
are uniformly bounded for
T\geq 1
, and
we can choose weakly convergent subsequences
(\xi_{\pm,T_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, cf.
[
Yo
, Theorem 1, p. 126]
.
Denote the weak limits by
\xi_{\pm}\in{\mathcal{G}}
.
Then for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we compute

\displaystyle\langle\psi,F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\xi_{\pm}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi,\xi_{\pm}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle=\pm\tfrac{(2\lambda)^{1/2}}{2\pi}\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl\langle\phi^{S}_{\pm}[F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi],\chi_{T_{n}}^{\prime}\phi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm\tfrac{(2\lambda)^{1/2}}{2\pi}\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl\langle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi,\chi_{T_{n}}^{\prime}\phi\bigr\rangle.

Then, as in the proof of Proposition
4.16
,
we use Proposition
4.14
, Theorem
1.3
and the assumption
\phi\in\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
to proceed as

\displaystyle\langle\psi,F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\xi_{\pm}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl\langle\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}((\nabla r)\cdot p)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi,\chi_{T_{n}}^{\prime}\phi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl\langle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi,[H-\lambda,1-\chi_{T_{n}}]\phi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\langle\psi,\phi\rangle.

Thus we obtain that
\phi=2\pi\mathrm{i}F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\xi_{\pm}
.
The assertions
1
and
2
are done.

Step IV.
Here we prove
3
.
Note that the identities (
1.12c
) are already established
in Step I.
Then in combination with
1
and

2
we see
F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\colon{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{*}

are indeed bi-continuous. Hence we obtain
3
.

Step V.
Finally we prove
4
.
Since
F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}
are injective with closed ranges
in
\mathcal{B}^{*}
by
3
,
Theorem
1.5
(
4
) and Banach’s closed range theorem
[
Yo
, Theorem p. 205]

imply that the ranges of
F^{\pm}(\lambda)
coincide with
\mathcal{G}
.
This along with
3
and
Theorem
1.5
(
3
) in turn implies
that the range of
\delta(H-\lambda)

coincides with
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
.
Hence we are done.
∎

4.5.
Generalized Fourier transforms

We close this section with the proof of Theorem
1.12
,
which is
rather routine thanks to Theorem
1.5
, see also
[
Sk2
,
IS1
]
.

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

Step I.

We may let
I
be compact.
In fact, if
I
is unbounded, decompose

I=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}[\lambda_{n},\lambda_{n+1}];\quad\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\dots<\lambda_{n}\to\infty\ \ \text{as }n\to\infty,

and, supposing that the assertion holds true for compact intervals, we define

\mathcal{F}_{I}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_{[\lambda_{n},\lambda_{n+1}]}\colon\mathcal{H}_{I}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I}.

Then the assertion for
I
follows
due to absence of positive eigenvalues for
H_{I}
and
M_{\lambda}
and closedness of
H_{I}
and
M_{\lambda}
.
Thus we let
I
be compact in the following.

Step II.
Let us construct the
isometries
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{H}_{I}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
.
By Theorem
1.5
and Stone’s formula
[
RS
, Theorem VII.13]

it follows that for any
\psi\in\mathcal{B}

(4.50)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}\psi\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I}}^{2}=\int_{I}\|F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=\int_{I}\langle\psi,\delta(H-\lambda)\psi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=\|P_{H}(I)\psi\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{I}}.

Since
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is dense,
also
P_{H}(I)\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{I}
is dense.
Thus for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{I}
we can choose
a sequence
(\psi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\mathcal{B}
such that

P_{H}(I)\psi_{n}\to\psi
in
\mathcal{H}_{I}
, and then we can define

\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\psi=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}\psi_{n}.

By (
4.50
) these limits are well-defined and certainly
define isometries as wanted.

To be used below we note that by construction for any
\psi\in\mathcal{B}

\mathcal{F}^{\pm}P_{H}(I)\psi=\mathcal{F}^{\pm}_{0}\psi\in C(I;\mathcal{G}).

Step III.
Next we show
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}H_{I}=M_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
.
Note all the involved operators are bounded.
Take any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\lambda\in I
.
If we then set
\psi^{\prime}=(H-\lambda)\psi
, it follows
from Proposition
4.14
that

\psi=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi^{\prime}
. Consequently

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)H\psi=\lambda R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi+\psi.

This implies that for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}H\psi=M_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}\psi.

Similarly to Step I, for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{I}
we can choose a sequence

(\psi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that
P_{H}(I)\psi_{n}\to\psi
, hence
P_{H}(I)H\psi_{n}\to H_{I}\psi
, in
\mathcal{H}_{I}
.
Then it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\pm}H_{I}\psi=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}H\psi_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}M_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\pm}\psi_{n}=M_{\lambda}\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\psi.

The claim is verified.

Step IV.
In order to complete (
1
) and (
2
) it remains to show

\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{H}_{I}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
are surjective.
It suffices to show that the ranges
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\mathcal{H}_{I}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
are dense.
Take any
\Xi\in C(I;\mathcal{G})\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
,
and fix any
\epsilon>0
.
By the compactness, the result from Step II and
Theorem
1.10

4

we can find a finite open covering
I\subseteq U_{1}\cup\dots\cup U_{n}

and
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\in P_{H}(I)\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{I}

such that for any
i=1,\dots,n
and
\lambda\in I\cap U_{i}

\bigl\|\Xi(\lambda)-(\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\psi_{i})(\lambda)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{G}}<\epsilon.

If we let
\{\chi_{i}\}_{i}
be a partition of unity subordinate to
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
,
then for any
\lambda\in I

\Big\lVert\Xi(\lambda)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi_{i}(\lambda)(\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\psi_{i})(\lambda)\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{G}}<\epsilon.

Since
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\psi_{i}\in C(I;\mathcal{G})
,
we can replace the above
\chi_{i}
by some polynomials
\widetilde{\chi}_{i}

due the Weierstrass approximation theorem with an additional
\epsilon
error.
Then set

\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\chi}_{i}(H)\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{I},

and we obtain by the result from Step III that for any
\lambda\in I

\left\|\Xi(\lambda)-(\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\psi)(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathcal{G}}<2\epsilon.

Hence (
1
) and (
2
) are done.

Step V.
The assertion (
3
) is clear from the definition of the generalized Fourier transforms,
see e.g. (
4.41
), and Lemma
4.10
with
U=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
We are done.
∎

5.
Time-dependent scattering theory

In this section we discuss the time-dependent scattering theory,
proving Theorems
1.14
,
1.17
and
1.20
.
Our arguments are heavily dependent on the stationary theory from the
previous sections in parallel to

[
IS2
]
, however for
2
-admissible
potentials the low degree of smoothness entails some complication.
In Subsection
5.1
we prove Theorem
1.14
by rigorously justifying the Legendre transform,
and then we compare the asymptotics of solutions to the time-dependent eikonal equation.
With these results in place we show Theorem
1.17
in
Subsection
5.2
, hence obtaining
the existence and covariance of the time-dependent wave operators.
Finally Theorem
1.20
, in particular including asymptotic completeness,
is shown by comparing the time-dependent wave operators with the generalized Fourier transforms.

5.1.
Time-dependent eikonal equation

Here we prove Theorem
1.14
.
We justify the Legendre transform,
investigating its properties.

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

Fix
\mu,\mu^{\prime},I,R
and
S=(2\lambda)^{1/2}|x|(1+s)
as in the assertion.
We first claim, letting
R>0
be larger if necessary, that for each
(t,x)\in\Omega_{\mu}

we can find a unique critical point of the function (
1.14
) in the
variable
\lambda\in I
.
In fact, we can compute

(5.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K}&=(2\lambda)^{-1/2}|x|\bigl(1+s+2\lambda(\partial_{\lambda}s)\bigr)-t,\\
\partial_{\lambda}^{2}\widetilde{K}&=-(2\lambda)^{-3/2}|x|\bigl(1+s-4\lambda(\partial_{\lambda}s)-4\lambda^{2}(\partial_{\lambda}^{2}s)\bigr),\end{split}

and thus by Corollary
2.14
for sufficiently large
R>0

\displaystyle(\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K})(\mu^{\prime 2}/2,t,x)\geq(\tfrac{\mu+\mu^{\prime}}{2})^{-1}|x|-t>0,\quad\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}(\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K})(\lambda,t,x)=-t<0,

and for any
\lambda\in I

\displaystyle(\partial_{\lambda}^{2}\widetilde{K})(\lambda,t,x)\leq-\tfrac{1}{2}(2\lambda)^{-3/2}|x|<0.

This implies that there uniquely exists
\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}(t,x)\in I
such that

(5.2)

(\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K})(\lambda_{\mathrm{c}},t,x)=(2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}})^{-1/2}|x|\bigl(1+s(\lambda_{\mathrm{c}},x)+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}(\partial_{\lambda}s)(\lambda_{\mathrm{c}},x)\bigr)-t=0.

By the implicit function theorem the function
\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\in C^{l-1}(\Omega_{\mu})
.
Now we set

(5.3)

K=\widetilde{K}(\lambda_{\rm c},\cdot,\cdot),

and then we can easily see by (
5.2
) that

(5.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}K

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{\rm c},\quad\nabla_{x}K=(\nabla_{x}S)(\lambda_{\mathrm{c}},\cdot),

which verifies
K\in C^{l}(\Omega_{\mu})
and (
1.15
). We have
proven Theorem
1.14
(
1
).

Next we prove the assertion (
2
).
For that we claim for any
k+|\alpha|\leq 1

(5.5)

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\bigl(\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}-\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\bigr)\bigr|\leq C_{1}t^{-k}\langle x\rangle^{-\sigma-|\alpha|}.

In fact, note that (
5.2
) and
Corollary
2.14
imply (uniformly in
large
R>0
)

(5.6)

\displaystyle c_{2}t^{-2}x^{2}\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\leq C_{2}t^{-2}x^{2}.

Note also that
\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}=\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}
for
|x|\leq R
, so to show the bounds
(
5.5
) and
(
1.16
) we can indeed assume that
|x|>R
.
Then by combining (
5.2
), Theorem
1.3
, Corollary
2.14
and (
5.6
)
we obtain

(5.7)

\displaystyle\bigl|\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}-\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\bigr|=\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\bigl|s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}(\partial_{\lambda}s)\bigr|\bigl|2+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}(\partial_{\lambda}s)\bigr|\leq C_{3}\langle x\rangle^{-\sigma}.

This shows the claim for
k+|\alpha|=0
.
For
k+|\alpha|=1
we compute the derivatives of
\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}
by
the Leibniz rule applied to (
5.2
), equivalently written as

1=\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1}(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s)^{2}.

In fact, we have for
k=1
and
|\alpha|=0

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{x^{2}}{t^{3}}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1}(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s)^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}-\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\bigl(\partial_{t}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\bigr)\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{-2}\bigl(1+s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda}^{2}s\bigr)(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s),

and for
k=0
and
|\alpha|=1

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\bigr)\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1}(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s)^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}-\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\bigl(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\bigr)\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{-2}\bigl(1+s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda}^{2}s\bigr)(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+\tfrac{x^{2}}{t^{2}}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{-1}\bigl(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}s\bigr)(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s).

Possibly by taking
R>0
larger from the beginning (if necessary) and
by using Corollary
2.14
, we can
for
k=1
and
|\alpha|=0
write

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{2}{t}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s)\bigl(1+s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda}^{2}s\bigr)^{-1},

and for
k=0
and
|\alpha|=1

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2t^{2}}{x^{2}}\big(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tfrac{x^{2}}{2t^{2}}\bigr)\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}(1+s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s)\bigl(1+s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda}^{2}s\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\bigl(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}s+2\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}s\bigr)\bigl(1+s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}\partial_{\lambda}s-4\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{2}\partial_{\lambda}^{2}s\bigr)^{-1}.

The claim (
5.5
) follows from the above expressions, (
5.7
) and Theorem
1.3
.

One can verify the assertion (
2
) by (
1.14
), (
5.3
), (
5.5
), (
5.4
)
and Theorem
1.3
.
While the bounds (
1.16
) follow
immediately unless
k=0
and
|\alpha|=2
, the latter case
requires some other computations. We omit the details of proof for that case.
∎

We next investigate the asymptotics of general solutions to (
1.3b
).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mu,T,\Omega_{\mu,T}
and
K
satisfy the assumption of Theorem
1.17
,
and let
K_{\mathrm{L}}
be the Legendre transform of the function

S_{\mathrm{L}}
taken from Subsection
4.1
with

I=I_{\mu^{\prime}}=[\mu^{\prime 2}/2,\infty)
,
\mu^{\prime}\in(0,\mu)
, and
l^{\prime}>1+2/\rho
.
In addition, let
y
be the flow associated with
S_{\mathrm{L}}

as in Subsection
4.2
, and set

\tau(\lambda,t,\theta)=\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,s,\theta))|^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Then the following assertions hold.

(1)

There exists the limit

\displaystyle\Xi_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta):=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\bigl(K(\tau(\lambda,t,\theta),y(\lambda,t,\theta))-K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau(\lambda,t,\theta),y(\lambda,t,\theta))\bigr)

taken locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\theta)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, where
J=[\mu^{2}/2,\infty)
.
In particular,
if
K_{1}
also satisfies the assumption of Theorem
1.17
,
there exists the limit

\displaystyle\Xi(\lambda,\theta):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\bigl(K_{1}(\tau(\lambda,t,\theta),y(\lambda,t,\theta))-K(\tau(\lambda,t,\theta),y(\lambda,t,\theta))\bigr)

taken locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\theta)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

(2)

The quantities in
1
can also be computed
as the limits

\displaystyle\Phi_{L}(\lambda,\omega)

\displaystyle:=\Xi_{L}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\big(K(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)-K_{L}(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)\big),

\displaystyle\Phi(\lambda,\omega)

\displaystyle:=\Xi(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\big(K_{1}(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)-K(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)\big),

both taken locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\omega)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Remarks
.

The requirement

l^{\prime}>1+2/\rho
(not used in the proof) will be needed in the proof of Lemma

5.2
,
cf. Remark
4.3
. The above function
\tau
should be considered as the
‘physical time’, cf.
Remark
4.5

2
.

Proof.

Step I.
The second assertion of
1
is clear from the first
one, hence we only prove the first assertion of
1
.
It suffices to show existence of the limits

(5.8a)

\displaystyle{\Psi(\lambda,\theta):=}\lim_{t\to\infty}\bigl(K(\tau(\lambda,t,\theta),y(\lambda,t,\theta))-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))+\lambda\tau(\lambda,t,\theta)\bigr)

and

(5.8b)

\displaystyle{\Psi_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta):=}\lim_{t\to\infty}\bigl(K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau(\lambda,t,\theta),y(\lambda,t,\theta))-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))+\lambda\tau(\lambda,t,\theta)\bigr)

taken locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\omega)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
We can prove them in the same manner, but it follows
easily from the proof of (
5.8a
) that in fact

(5.8c)

K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau(\lambda,t,\theta),y(\lambda,t,\theta))-S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,y(\lambda,t,\theta))+\lambda\tau(\lambda,t,\theta)=0,

hence we discuss only (
5.8a
).

Note that all the arguments below are locally uniform in
(\lambda,\theta)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Omitting the arguments, and using (
1.15
), (
4.3a
) and (
1.6
),
we can compute the
t
-derivative for large
t>0
as

(5.9)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(K-S_{\mathrm{L}}+\lambda\tau)=-\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}(\nabla K-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})^{2}-|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{-2}V_{\mathrm{S}}.

The second term on the right-hand side of (
5.9
) is
obviously integrable at infinity,
and thus it suffices to show that for some
\delta>0

(5.10)

\displaystyle u:=(\nabla K-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})^{2}=\mathcal{O}(t^{-1-\delta}).

For that, similarly to (
5.9
), we further differentiate it for large
t>0
as

(5.11)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}|^{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u=-(\nabla K-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})(\nabla^{2}K)(\nabla K-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}})-(\nabla V_{\mathrm{S}})\cdot(\nabla K-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}).

By (
1.7
), (
1.16
) and (
4.1a
),
it follows that for some (small)
\delta>0
and (big)
C_{1}>0
and any large
t>0

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u\leq-(2-\delta)t^{-1}u+C_{1}t^{-2-\delta}.

This certainly implies (
5.10
). Thus we are done with

1
.

Step II.
To prove the assertion
2
we discuss the following change of variables.
We claim that for all large
\tau>0
and any
(\lambda,\omega)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
there exist
\bar{\lambda}
and
\bar{\theta}
such that

(5.12a)

(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)=(\tau(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}),y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}))\quad\text{with}\quad t=S_{L}(\bar{\lambda},(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega),

and that

(5.12b)

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\,\bar{\lambda}(\tau)=\lambda\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\,\bar{\theta}(\tau)=\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega).

First we solve for fixed
\bar{\lambda}
and
\bar{\theta}
the equation

\tau=\int_{0}^{t}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L}}(\bar{\lambda},y(\bar{\lambda},s,\bar{\theta}))|^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

for
t=t(\tau,\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})

(with a
C^{1}
-dependence by the implicit function theorem).
For fixed large
\tau
we then need to solve the equation

(5.13)

(2\lambda)^{1/2}\omega=y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta})/\tau=:F_{\tau}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})

for
\bar{\lambda}
and
\bar{\theta}
. This can be done
by using the inverse
function theorem in a version applicable to
parameter-dependent problems (note that
\tau
is the relevant
parameter), for example the version stated as
[
Is2
, Theorem
D.1]
. We need
to verify that the derivative of

F_{\tau}
at
z_{0}:=(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))
is
non-degenerate near infinity.

First note that

F_{\infty}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta}):=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}F_{\tau}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})=(2\bar{\lambda})^{1/2}\omega_{+}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})\text{ in a neighbourhood of }z_{0},

in particular that

F_{\infty}(z_{0})=(2\lambda)^{1/2}\omega.

Next note
that

\omega_{+}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial s}\omega(\bar{\lambda},s,\bar{\theta})\,{\rm d}s+\bar{\theta}\quad\text{ and }\quad\partial_{\bar{\lambda}}\omega_{+}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\partial_{\bar{\lambda}}\partial_{s}\omega(\bar{\lambda},s,\bar{\theta})\,{\rm d}s.

By the latter formula, the representation (
4.15
) and Corollary

2.14
it follows that
\partial_{\bar{\lambda}}\omega_{+}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})
is small when the
parameter
R
is sufficiently big. When combined with
(
4.23
) this leads to the
conclusion that the map

(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})\to F_{\infty}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})=(2\bar{\lambda})^{1/2}\omega_{+}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})
is non-degenerate

C^{1}
near
z_{0}
(uniformly in large
R
).

Now to solve (
5.13
), by applying
[
Is2
, Theorem D.1]
,
all that remains to be seen is that the limits

\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\partial_{\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta}}{F_{\tau}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})}\text{
exist, uniformly in a neighbourhood of }z_{0}.

We skip the details of the verification of this
uniform convergence, and conclude
the solvability of (
5.13
). This justifies
(
5.12a
) and (
5.12b
).

Step III.
Now we prove the first assertion of
2
(this implies the second one).
We proceed partly in parallel to the proof of Lemma
4.10

2
.
It suffices to show that with notation from Step I

\displaystyle\Psi(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))-\Psi_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\bigl(K(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)-K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)\bigr)

locally uniformly in
(\lambda,\omega)\in J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Take any compact subset
L\subset J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Let
(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})=(\bar{\lambda}(\tau),\bar{\theta}(\tau))
be the change of variables from Step II.
Using the locally uniform limit
(
4.28
) along with
(
5.12a
) and (
5.12b
), and noting that
\tau\to\infty
corresponds to
t\to\infty
,
it follows that for some compact subset
L^{\prime}\subset J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\omega)\in L}\bigl|\Psi(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))-\Psi_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\theta_{+}(\lambda,\omega))-K(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)+K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\omega)\in L}\bigl|\Psi(\bar{\lambda},\theta(\bar{\lambda},(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega))-\Psi_{\mathrm{L}}(\bar{\lambda},\theta(\bar{\lambda},(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-K(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)+K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau,(2\lambda)^{1/2}\tau\omega)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})\in L^{\prime}}\bigl|\Psi(\bar{\lambda},\theta(\bar{\lambda},y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta})))-\Psi_{\mathrm{L}}(\bar{\lambda},\theta(\bar{\lambda},y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta})))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-K(\tau(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}),y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}))+K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}),y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}))\bigr|

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})\in L^{\prime}}\bigl|\Psi(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})-\Psi_{\mathrm{L}}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{\theta})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-K(\tau(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}),y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}))+K_{\mathrm{L}}(\tau(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}),y(\bar{\lambda},t,\bar{\theta}))\bigr|

\displaystyle=0.

Thus we are done.
∎

5.2.
Time-dependent wave operators

Here we prove Theorems
1.17
and
1.20
.

5.2.1.
Existence

Proof of Theorem
1.17
.

We first prove the existence of the strong limits
(
1.17
) by the
familiar Cook–Kuroda method.
Thanks to a density argument based on uniform boundedness of
U^{\pm}(t)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}tH}
in
t>T
,
it suffices to show that for any
h\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

with
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h\subseteq J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, there exist the limits

(5.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}tH}U^{\pm}(t)h.

For that we show integrability of

\bigl\|\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}tH}U^{\pm}(t)h\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\bigl\|\bigl(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\pm{\rm i}H\bigr)U^{\pm}(t)h\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}}

at infinity.
Using the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
1.3b
) and letting
\omega^{\prime}=(\omega_{2}^{\prime},\dots,\omega_{d}^{\prime})
be any local coordinates of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
we compute

\displaystyle\bigl(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\pm{\rm i}H\bigr)U^{\pm}(t)h

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\mp 3\pi{\rm i}/4}t^{-1}\lvert x\rvert^{1-d/2}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}K}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\cdot\Bigl[\mp\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}x^{2}t^{-4}(\partial_{\lambda}^{2}h)\mp\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}|x|^{-2}\bigl(\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}h\bigr)+(\partial_{i}\omega_{\alpha}^{\prime})(\partial_{i}K)(\partial_{\alpha}h)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\quad+\Bigl(-t^{-3}x^{2}\mp{\rm i}t^{-2}+t^{-2}x\cdot(\nabla_{x}K)\Bigr)(\partial_{\lambda}h)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\quad+\Bigl(-t^{-1}\pm{\rm i}\tfrac{d}{4}\bigl(1-\tfrac{d}{2}\bigr)|x|^{-2}\pm\mathrm{i}q

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}{}\quad\quad\ \ +\bigl(1-\tfrac{d}{2}\bigr)|x|^{-2}x\cdot(\nabla_{x}K)+\tfrac{1}{2}(\Delta_{x}K)\Bigr)h\Bigr].

By the assumption on
K
and the support property of
h

the last expression is of order
t^{-1-\min\{\epsilon,\tau\}}
with values in
\mathcal{H}
.
Hence the limits (
5.14
) exist.
Since
U^{\pm}(t)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}tH}
are isometries, it is clear that so are

W^{\pm}
. By the above computation we easily see that
W^{\pm}M_{\lambda}\subseteq HW^{\pm}
. In particular the mapping property stated in the
last part of
Theorem
1.17
(
1
)
follows.

The assertion Theorem
1.17
(
2
) follows
readily from
(
1
) and Lemma
5.1

2
.
∎

We present alternative representations of
W^{\pm}
, which
will be useful in the proof of their completeness.
See also
[
IS2
, Lemma 3.3]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

In the setting of Lemma
5.1
,
define for any
h\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1})

with
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h\subseteq J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
the evolutions

\displaystyle(\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h)(x)

\displaystyle=(\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{\mp{\rm i}\lambda t}\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[h(\lambda,\cdot)](\lambda,x)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,\quad(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[\cdot]
is from (
4.30
).
Then for each
t>0
it follows that
\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h\in\mathcal{H}
. Moreover

W^{\pm}\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}h=\lim_{t\to\infty}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}tH}\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h,

respectively.

Remark 5.3
.

The above evolution map
\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)
motivates our free comparison dynamics
U^{\pm}(t)
.
In fact, due to the stationary phase theorem the leading term is very similar to that of
U^{\pm}(t)
, see the proof below.
Note however that we need higher order derivatives of the phase function.
This is why we substitute
S_{\mathrm{L}}
for
S
in
\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)
.
The error is compensated by the factor
\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}
.

Proof.

Step I.
To show that
\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h\in\mathcal{H}
we introduce

\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)=S_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,x)-\lambda t,

cf. (
1.14
).
First fix any
t>0
, and let
R^{\prime}>0
be sufficiently large.
Then by the expressions (
5.1
) corresponding to
\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}

we have for any
(\lambda,\hat{x})\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h
with
|x|>R^{\prime}

(5.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)\geq c_{1}\langle x\rangle,\quad\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{2}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)\bigr|\leq C_{1}\langle x\rangle.

Thus we can integrate by parts as

(5.16)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\chi\lvert x\rvert^{-(d-1)/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}}\partial_{\lambda}\Bigl[(\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}})^{-1}(2\lambda)^{-1/4}h\Bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,

which implies, due to (
5.15
) again, that for any
|x|>R^{\prime}

\displaystyle\bigl|(\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h)(x)\bigr|\leq C_{2}\langle x\rangle^{-(d+1)/2}.

Since
\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h
is uniformly bounded for
|x|\leq R^{\prime}
, we
conclude that
\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h\in\mathcal{H}
.

We prove the second assertion in several steps.

Step II.
By the uniform boundedness of
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}tH}
and Lemma
5.1
it suffices to show that as
t\to\infty

\displaystyle(U^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h)(x)={\rm e}^{\mp 3\pi{\rm i}/4}t^{-1}\lvert x\rvert^{1-d/2}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}K_{\mathrm{L}}(t,x)}h\big(x^{2}/(2t^{2}),\hat{x}\big)+o(t^{0})\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{H}.

Recall that
S_{\mathrm{L}}
is defined for
I=[\mu^{\prime 2}/2,\infty)
as in Lemma
5.1
.
Let
\mu^{\prime\prime}=(\mu^{\prime}+\mu)/2
, and decompose

{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}=\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}\cup\Omega^{c}_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}
and correspondingly

(5.17)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h)(x)

\displaystyle=1_{\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}(t,x)(\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h)(x)+1_{\Omega^{c}_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}(t,x)(\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h)(x).

Here let us prove that the second term of (
5.17
) is negligible.
In fact, using (
5.1
) for
\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}
,
we can estimate for any
\lambda\geq\mu^{2}/2
and
(t,x)\notin\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}
with sufficiently large
t

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)\leq-c_{2}t,\quad\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{2}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)\bigr|\leq C_{3}\langle x\rangle.

This allows us to integrate by parts in the same way as for
(
5.16
) and deduce the pointwise bound

\displaystyle\bigl|1_{\Omega^{c}_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}(t,x)(\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h)(x)\bigr|\leq C_{4}t^{-1}\langle x\rangle^{-(d-1)/2}.

By integration we then conclude the norm-bound

1_{\Omega^{c}_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h=\mathcal{O}\bigl(t^{-1/2}\bigr)\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{H}.

Step III.
As for the first term of (
5.17
) we decompose it as follows.
Using that
\mu^{\prime\prime}>\mu^{\prime}
, we can for any
(t,x)\in\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}

find a unique critical point
\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c}=\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c}(t,x)
of
\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}
.
Then take any
\eta_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\eta_{0}(s)=1
for
|s|\leq 1/2
while
\eta_{0}(s)=0
for
|s|\geq 1
, and set

\eta(\lambda,t,x)=\eta_{0}\bigl(\langle x\rangle^{\delta}(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})\bigr),\quad\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{\rho}{6}<\delta<\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{4(l^{\prime}-1)}.

We recall that
l^{\prime}
is the fixed integer obeying the condition
l^{\prime}>1+2/\rho
. Obviously we can
find
\delta
fulfilling these constraints, henceforth taken fixed.
We now decompose

\displaystyle 1_{\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h

\displaystyle=\mp\mathrm{i}(2\pi)^{-1/2}1_{\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}\chi\lvert x\rvert^{-(d-1)/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\eta+(1-\eta))\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}}(2\lambda)^{-1/4}h\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=:\psi_{1}(t,x)+\psi_{2}(t,x).

Step IV.
The second term
\psi_{2}
is negligible.
In fact, for any
k=2,\dots,l^{\prime}
, sufficiently large
t

and
(\lambda,t,x)\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(1-\eta)
with

(\lambda,\hat{x})\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h
,

(t,x)\in\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}

we can bound

\displaystyle\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)\bigr|\geq c_{2}\langle x\rangle^{1-\delta},\quad\bigl|\partial_{\lambda}^{k}\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)\bigr|\leq C_{3}\langle x\rangle^{1+\sigma-m(k)+k}.

This and the lower bound
\delta>1/2-\rho/6>1/2-\rho/2
imply that
each time we integrate
\psi_{2}
by parts as in (
5.16
) we at
least gain a decay of order
\langle x\rangle^{-1+2\delta}
.
Hence by doing the integration by parts in total
(l^{\prime}-1)
times we
can
conclude that

\displaystyle\psi_{2}=o(t^{0})\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{H}.

Step V.
It remains to investigate
\psi_{1}
. Let us expand the phase function as

\widetilde{K}_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,t,x)=K_{\mathrm{L}}(t,x)+\tfrac{1}{2}A(t,x)(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{2}+B(\lambda,t,x)(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{3}

with

\displaystyle A(t,x)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\lambda}^{2}S_{\mathrm{L}})(\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c},x),

\displaystyle B(\lambda,t,x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\tau)^{2}(\partial_{\lambda}^{3}S_{\mathrm{L}})(\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c}+\tau(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c}),x)\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

We substitute the expression into
\psi_{1}
, and split the integral as

\displaystyle\psi_{1}

\displaystyle=\mp\mathrm{i}(2\pi)^{-1/2}1_{\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}\chi\lvert x\rvert^{-(d-1)/2}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}K_{\mathrm{L}}}\biggl((2\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{-1/4}h(\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c},\cdot)\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}A(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{2}/2}\eta\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}A(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{2}/2}\eta\bigl[{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}B(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{3}}(2\lambda)^{-1/4}h-(2\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{-1/4}h(\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c},\cdot)\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\biggr).

Let us denote the last integral by
T
, and show that its contribution is negligible.
By an integration by parts we can rewrite it as

\displaystyle T=-\int_{|\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c}|\leq\langle x\rangle^{-\delta}}

\displaystyle\biggl(\int_{\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c}}^{\lambda}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}A(\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda^{\prime}\biggr)

\displaystyle{}\cdot\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\eta\bigl[{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}B(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{3}}(2\lambda)^{-1/4}h-(2\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{-1/4}h(\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c},\cdot)\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}\lambda.

Then by the van der Corput Lemma, cf.
[
St
, p. 332]
,
and the assumed support property of
h
, it follows that on
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(1_{\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}\chi)

T=\mathcal{O}\bigl(\langle x\rangle^{-1/2-\delta^{\prime}}\bigr),\quad\delta^{\prime}=\delta-\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{\rho}{6},

so that indeed

\displaystyle\psi_{1}\pm\mathrm{i}(2\pi)^{-1/2}1_{\Omega_{\mu^{\prime\prime}}}\lvert x\rvert^{-(d-1)/2}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}K_{\mathrm{L}}}(2\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{-1/4}h(\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c},\cdot)\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm e}^{\pm{\rm i}A(\lambda-\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{2}/2}\eta\,\mathrm{d}\lambda=o(t^{0})

as a vector in
\mathcal{H}
.

Step VI.
Finally we remove
\eta
from the last integral with another admissible error, and then
implement the Gaussian integral to obtain

\displaystyle\psi_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}3\pi/4}\lvert x\rvert^{-(d-1)/2}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}K_{\mathrm{L}}}|A|^{-1/2}(2\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{-1/4}h(\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c},\cdot)+o(t^{0})\ \ \text{in }\mathcal{H}.

Thus, using Lemma
5.1
and

A=-(2\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c})^{-3/2}|x|+\mathcal{O}\bigl(\langle x\rangle^{1-\sigma}\bigr),\quad\lambda_{\mathrm{L},c}=x^{2}/(2t^{2})+\mathcal{O}\bigl(\langle x\rangle^{-\sigma}\bigr),

we obtain the second assertion of the lemma.
∎

5.2.2.
Asymptotic completeness

Now we are ready to prove the first part of Theorem
1.20
.

Proof of Theorem
1.20
(
1
).

It suffices to show the identity (
1.19
) since
then the asymptotic completeness is obvious by the unitarity of
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
.
Let
S_{\mathrm{L}}
be defined for an interval

I_{\mu^{\prime}}=[\mu^{\prime 2}/2,\infty)
,
0<\mu^{\prime}<\mu
, sufficiently big to include the given closed interval
I
.
Then for any
h\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
with
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h\subseteq(I\cap J)\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we can compute by Lemma
5.2

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\psi,W^{\pm}\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}h\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon t}\bigl\langle\psi,\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}tH}\widetilde{U}^{\pm}_{\mathrm{L}}(t)h\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}(\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl\langle\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}t(H-\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)}\psi,\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[h(\lambda,\cdot)]\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}t.

By Fubini’s theorem and (
4.42c
) we can further proceed as

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\psi,W^{\pm}\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}}h\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}(\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl\langle\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\psi,\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[h(\lambda,\cdot)]\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}(\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl\langle\psi-(H-\lambda)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\psi,\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[h(\lambda,\cdot)]\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}(\pm 2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl\langle\psi,\phi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[h(\lambda,\cdot)]-R(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\psi_{\pm}^{S_{\mathrm{L}}}[h(\lambda,\cdot)]\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl\langle\psi,F^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}\big(\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}h(\lambda,\cdot)\bigr)\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl\langle\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}(\lambda,\cdot)}F^{\pm}(\lambda)\psi,h(\lambda,\cdot)\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}}\mathcal{F}^{\pm}\psi,h\bigr\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}.

This implies, as operators
\mathcal{H}_{I\cap J}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{I\cap J}
,

(W^{\pm})^{*}=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}(\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}-\Phi_{\mathrm{L}})}\mathcal{F}^{\pm}.

We have proven (
1.19
) with
\Psi=\Phi_{\mathrm{L}}-\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}
,
and we are done with (
1
).
∎

To prove the remaining assertion
Theorem
1.20
(
2
) we use
an approximation argument to be studied in the following.
Fix any
\rho\in(0,\sigma)
and
\delta\in(0,\sigma-\rho)
,
and let
\epsilon=1/j
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
For these parameters
\rho,\epsilon
and
\delta
used as inputs in
Lemma
4.1
(
2
) we decompose
V
accordingly
and the denote the decomposition as

V=V_{\mathrm{S},j}+V_{\mathrm{L},j}.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mu>\mu^{\prime}>0
,
I=[\mu^{\prime 2}/2,\infty)
and
J=[\mu^{2}/2,\infty)
, and
fix a sufficiently large
R>0
.
Let
S,S_{\mathrm{L},j}
be determined as in Theorem
1.3
for
V,V_{\mathrm{L},j}
,
and let
K,K_{\mathrm{L},j}
be their Legendre transforms as in Theorem
1.14
, respectively.
Then

(5.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\Theta_{\mathrm{L},j}

\displaystyle=0\ \ \text{locally uniformly on }I\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

with
\Theta_{\mathrm{L},j}
being the limit from Lemma
4.10
for
S
and
S_{\mathrm{L},j}
,
and

(5.19)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\Phi_{\mathrm{L},j}

\displaystyle=0\ \ \text{locally uniformly on }J\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

with
\Phi_{\mathrm{L},j}
being the limit from Lemma
5.1
for
K
and
K_{\mathrm{L},j}
.

Remark 5.5
.

In the construction of
S,S_{\mathrm{L},j},K,K_{\mathrm{L},j}

we can use the same
R
since it depends only on sizes of zeroth to second derivatives of
V,V_{\mathrm{L},j}
,
which are uniformly estimated due to Lemma
4.1

(
2
).
See the proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.14
.

Proof.

Step I.
To prove (
5.18
) we can partially mimic the proof of Lemma
4.10
.
For details of the following computations see the proof there,
though one should note that
S
and
S_{\mathrm{L},j}
now are more specific than before.

Let
y_{j}
be the flow from Subsection
4.2
associated with
S_{\mathrm{L},j}
,
and we discuss the limit

{\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},j}(\lambda,\theta):=}\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(S(\lambda,y_{j}(\lambda,t,\theta))-S_{\mathrm{L},j}(\lambda,y_{j}(\lambda,t,\theta))\big).

Omitting the argument, we can compute its derivative in
t
as

(5.20)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(S-S_{\mathrm{L},j})=-\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j}|^{-2}(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j})^{2}-|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j}|^{-2}\chi_{R}V_{\mathrm{S},j}.

Hence we are led to consider

u=(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j})^{2}.

Its
t
-derivative is computed as

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}|\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j}|^{2}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u

\displaystyle=-(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j})\cdot(\nabla^{2}S)(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j})-(\nabla\chi_{R}V_{\mathrm{S},j})\cdot(\nabla S-\nabla S_{\mathrm{L},j}),

and thus we can deduce that for any (small)

\delta>0
there exist
C_{1},T>0
independent of
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}u\leq-(2-\delta)t^{-1}u+C_{1}j^{-1}t^{-2-\delta};\quad t\geq T.

By integration we then deduce that

(5.21)

\displaystyle u(t)\leq t^{\delta-2}T^{2-\delta}u(T)+C_{1}j^{-1}t^{-1-\delta};\quad t\geq T.

By another integration (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) imply a uniform bound

\displaystyle|S-S_{\mathrm{L},j}|\leq|(S-S_{\mathrm{L},j})(T)|+C_{2}Tu(T)+C_{3}j^{-1}.

On the other hand by integrating a version of (
4.26
) from

t=0
(where
u
vanishes) to
t=T
(for fixed
T
) we deduce that

(5.22)

|(S-S_{\mathrm{L},j})(T)|+C_{2}Tu(T)\leq C_{4}j^{-1/2}.

(In fact the bound holds with
j^{-1/2}
replaced by

j^{-1}
if we invoke the variational principle of Lemma

2.5
to bound the first term.)

We conclude
that for large
t

\displaystyle|S-S_{\mathrm{L},j}|\leq C_{5}j^{-1/2}.

In particular
|\Sigma_{\mathrm{L},j}|,\,|\Theta_{\mathrm{L},j}|\leq C_{3}j^{-1/2}
, and (
5.18
) follows.

Step II.
The proof of (
5.19
) is similar.
We consider versions of
(
5.8a
) and (
5.8b
) using versions of
(
5.8c
) and (
5.11
),
omitting here the details.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.20
(
2
).

Let
K
be the Legendre transform of
S
from Theorem
1.3
, as given in (
2
).
Note that the phase corrections
\mathrm{e}^{\mp{\rm i}\Psi}=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}(\Theta_{\mathrm{L}}-\Phi_{\mathrm{L}})}
are independent of choice of
V_{\mathrm{L}}

since so are
\mathcal{F}^{\pm}
and
W^{\pm}
.
Thus it suffices to choose
V_{\mathrm{L},j}
such that
the associated difference
\Theta_{\mathrm{L},j}-\Phi_{\mathrm{L},j}
converges to
0
as
j\to\infty
.
However this obviously follows from Lemma
5.4
.
∎
